- **`SyncFileSystem`** (open/read/write/seek/close/fstat/stat/read_dir/mkdir/remove_file/remove_dir/rename), with `SystemSyncFileSystem` as the OS-backed default over `std::fs`. It reuses `SystemFileSystem`'s `confine` jail verbatim, and the CLI constructs it with the **same base and same root** as the async one, so both agree on what is reachable. Handles live in a table the provider owns; the guest only ever sees an opaque `u32`, so a forged number can only miss. A directory handle holds a path rather than an OS handle — WASI uses directory fds purely as anchors, and opening a directory as a file is not portable.
- **Opening is two ops, not one.** `OpDecl::requires` carries a single capability, and a read-only open must not demand `FileWrite`. So `sync_fs_open` is `FileRead`-gated and rejects any mutating mode outright, while `sync_fs_open_write` is `FileWrite`-gated. Enforcement then stays **per operation** regardless of how the handle was obtained: bytes are read through the `FileRead`-gated `sync_fs_read` and written through the `FileWrite`-gated `sync_fs_write`, so a handle cannot be laundered into the other capability's authority.
- **Three checks, not one.** A WASI file access must satisfy: (1) a **preopen** maps the path, and `..` cannot climb out of it — enforced in `runtime:wasi`, which keeps sibling preopens isolated from each other; (2) the **capability** on the op (D7); (3) the provider's **root jail** (D25). Each is independent, and the first is WASI's own model rather than something invented here.
- **Blocking is admitted, not hidden.** These ops occupy the runtime's thread for the call. That is stated in the trait docs, the module docs and `API.md`; an embedder unwilling to pay it installs no `SyncFileSystem` and every WASI file call reports `ENOTCAPABLE`, exactly as before this change.

**Consequences:** `wasm32-wasip1` binaries that read and write files now run, and the capability model makes that **enforced** rather than advisory — the gap Node's own WASI docs disclaim ("does not provide secure sandboxing"; capabilities "do not form a security model"). The provider surface grows by one trait; embedders that do not use WASI are unaffected (the field is optional, defaulting to `None`). Verified by 6 provider unit tests (round-trip, seek anchors, bad handles, directory handles, the jail on every path method, directory entries), 3 runtime tests proving the gates deny *before* the provider is consulted, and 3 end-to-end CLI tests over a real preopened directory covering the full call surface plus preopen isolation and traversal refusal. **Still unserved:** `fd_pread`/`fd_pwrite`, hard/symbolic links, and the `*_set_times`/`set_size` calls — no host primitive behind them yet; they report `ENOTCAPABLE` while remaining linkable. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/runtime-wasi.d.ts`, site `docs/modules`, `CHANGELOG`).

### D37 — `runtime:system`: child processes, no shell, no inherited environment · *Proposed (2026-07-31)*
**Context:** A server runtime that cannot start a program cannot do the work servers actually get handed: transcode an upload with `ffmpeg`, read a repository with `git`, dump a database with `pg_dump`, drive a Python or Go sidecar over pipes, or run a deploy step. `runtime:process` covers *this* process; nothing covered the host's other processes. The deferral was named twice already — D26 and D30 both record env propagation to a child as "future work tied to `child_process`". Because `node:` builtins are rejected (D22/D24), `execa`, `cross-spawn`, `zx` and `tinyexec` cannot run here at all: whatever ships is the entire surface a user gets, so the things Node leaves to npm have to be in the box.

**Decision:** A new standard module, `runtime:system` (the namespace for the *host machine*, leaving room for host info later), over a new `CommandProvider` seam and a new `Capability::Run`.
- **`Run` is its own capability and is never implied.** A child process runs *outside* every confinement this runtime applies — no capability check, no D25 root jail, no execution deadline reaches it. Granting `Run` to guest code is granting everything the host user can do, and the docs say so in those words. The policy seam for an embedder that must grant it anyway is the provider: `SystemCommands::with_allowlist([...])` and `with_max_children(n)`.
- **No shell, ever.** No `exec`, no `shell: true`, no template form. A command is a program plus an argv, so a guest-supplied argument is data and never a second command — the largest CVE class in this API in Node and Bun is designed out rather than documented around. Consequence accepted: no pipelines, globs or `&&`, which is real friction for the script-runner use case (a userland helper can compose `Command`s).
- **The environment is empty unless asked for.** Every incumbent (Node, Deno, Bun, execa) inherits by default. Here the child gets exactly the `env` passed, and `inheritEnv: true` **additionally requires `Env`** — implemented by having the JS module read the environment through the existing `Env`-gated `process_env` op and pass the pairs across the seam, so the provider never merges anything and `Run` alone cannot hand a child the host's secrets. This resolves the D26/D30 deferral. Cost: `PATH` must be passed (or inherited) — mitigated because program *resolution* stays host authority (below). A `Secret` (D30) is unwrapped on its way to the child, or it would arrive as the literal `"[redacted]"`; the unwrap is a symbol-keyed getter, invisible to console/JSON/coercion, so masking still covers every accidental path.
- **Program resolution is explicit, not the platform's.** The provider resolves the program itself: a bare name against the *host* `PATH` (plus `PATHEXT` on Windows), a relative path against the child's `cwd`. `std::process` leaves both unspecified and they differ between platforms. Windows `.bat`/`.cmd` are **refused**: running one requires handing `cmd.exe` a line to re-parse, which is exactly the injection this module refuses to enable (CVE-2024-27980). Accepted limitation — `npm.cmd` and friends need their underlying executable named instead.
- **Web streams, and an async `spawn()`.** `stdout`/`stderr` are pull-based `ReadableStream`s (a child that outruns its reader is stopped by a full pipe, not buffered without limit) and `stdin` is a `WritableStream` that also accepts any web body. `spawn()` is **async**, unlike Deno's: the seam is async, and a failure to *start* belongs to that call rather than surfacing later on three different objects (the shape `runtime:net`'s `Socket` has to work around).
- **`output()` is bounded.** `maxBuffer` defaults to 16 MiB and kills the child past it (`ERR_MAX_BUFFER`). Node caps `exec` at 1 MiB, Deno does not cap at all; unbounded buffering of a child's output is a memory-exhaustion path a *server* runtime should not offer by default.
- **Liveness is opt-in; orphans are not possible.** Streams are built on first use and reading `status` is what holds the program open, so a child nobody waits on keeps nothing alive — and `kill_on_drop` plus a `Drop` on the provider registry means whatever is still running when the runtime goes away is killed rather than reparented. Node leaks orphans by design (`signal-exit` exists for this).
- **Deliberately absent:** `fork()`/IPC (worker processes want their own design, not a flag on `Command`), `stdio` arrays and raw fd numbers (an fd hands the guest authority over anything the host inherited), `detached`/`uid`/`gid`/`argv0` (privilege and identity belong to the embedder), sync variants (the D36 blocking wall), and PTY (Bun now ships one; this runtime pipes).

**Consequences:** The module fills the last "a server has to shell out" gap, and does it with a smaller surface than any incumbent — the trade being that shell one-liners and `fork()`-style workers are not expressible. `Capability::Run` is the twelfth capability and the first whose honest description is "this grant ends the sandbox"; `SECURITY.md` and the site's security page say so. `Signal` gains send-only `SIGKILL`/`SIGQUIT` variants (never watchable — `Signals::available` excludes them) so a kill can escalate. `rustix` becomes a direct unix dependency of `default-providers`, chosen over `libc` because that crate forbids `unsafe`; it was already in the tree via `tempfile`, so the graph is unchanged. Verified by 12 provider unit tests (real processes: pipes, cwd, allowlist, `max_children`, signal reporting, argv inertness), 12 runtime tests against a fake provider (the capability gates, the environment seam, `Secret` unwrapping, `maxBuffer`, release-on-exit), and 14 end-to-end CLI tests driving the real binary. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `SECURITY.md`, `types/runtime-system.d.ts`, the site's subprocess guide + API page, `CHANGELOG`). **Left for later:** killing a process *tree* (only the direct child is signalled — no runtime does this natively, and it wants process groups), `resourceUsage()`, and an `execa`-style convenience layer.

### D38 — `esrun` permissions: `--deny-all` **or** `--deny-<name>`, never both · *Locked (maintainer sign-off, 2026-08-02)* · *default superseded by D65*

> **D65 (2026-08-14) flipped the default this entry argues for.** `esrun` now grants nothing unless told to; `esdev` keeps the permissive default described below. The vocabulary, the scoping and the "no flag ever overrides another" property are unchanged — D65 lifts the baseline out of the rules and mirrors them, so what follows is still the reasoning behind the grammar, and only its starting point has moved.

**Context:** ES Runtime ships two paths and only one of them is secured. The embeddable library is deny-by-default: `CapabilitySet::none()` grants nothing and the embedder threads in exactly what it wants (D7). `esrun` is the opposite by design — it runs a local script the user named, so it called `set_capabilities(CapabilitySet::all())` unconditionally and offered no way to run anything with less. That left no answer at all for the ordinary case of running a script you have not read: a dependency's build step, a snippet from the internet, a generated program. Node reached the same point and answered with `--permission` (stable since v22.13/v23.5); Deno was deny-by-default from the start; Bun has nothing.

**Decision (maintainer):** Keep `esrun` permissive by default — the default is *not* flipped, and no interactive prompt is introduced. Restriction is opt-in, expressed as **denials**, which is the inverse of Deno's allow-list because the starting point here is "everything granted" rather than "nothing granted".
- **Two ways to restrict, and they are mutually exclusive.** `--deny-all`, or one or more `--deny-<name>` — passing both is an **error**, not a merge. `--deny-all` is exactly the union of the granular flags, so a combination could only ever be redundant, and no reader should have to work out whether one narrows the other. *(Amended below: `--allow-<name>` joins `--deny-all` as the second mode's additive half.)*
- **One vocabulary, eight names**, each mapping 1:1 onto a host-facing capability: `read` (`FileRead`), `write` (`FileWrite`), `imports` (`FileSystem`), `net` (`Net`), `listen` (`NetListen`), `env` (`Env`), `run` (`Run`), `signals` (`Signals`). `--deny-net` ⇔ `permissions.has("net")` ⇔ the `(permission "net")` in the denial message. The list is Rust-side in `Capability::HOST_FACING` / `flag_name()`, so the CLI, the JS API, and the error text cannot drift.
- **No flag for `Clock`/`Entropy`/`Timers`/`TaskSpawn`.** No op gates them, so a flag revoking them would deny nothing while implying otherwise. `--deny-all` leaves them granted: a fully denied script still computes, it just reaches nothing. `Date.now()` and `crypto` are not the threat.
- **`--deny-all` still runs the entry file.** The entry is read by the CLI with `std::fs` before a `Runtime` exists, so it is outside the capability system entirely — the user named that file, and the flags govern what it may then *do*. Node made the same call (entry points are implicitly readable under `--permission`). Because `--deny-all` includes `--deny-imports`, a fully denied run is a **single-file** run; multi-file programs need a granular flag set instead. This is stricter than Deno, which lets statically analysable imports through without `--allow-read`.
- **Importing a `runtime:` module is always allowed; only the operation fails.** This is D26 restated, and enforcing it surfaced a **pre-existing bug**: `runtime:process` called `Env`-gated ops (`process_env`, `process_args`, `process_platform`, `process_arch`) at *module-evaluation* time, so `--deny-env` would have made the module unimportable — taking `exit()` and `onSignal()`, which have nothing to do with the environment, down with it. Fixed by seeding `env` and `args` lazily behind a `Proxy` on first access, and by **ungating** three ops: `process_platform`/`process_arch` (values from `std::env::consts` — properties of the binary the guest is already running, not host state) and `process_exit` (stopping is the guest's own control flow; code that can run can always stop running, and neither Node nor Deno gates it). A test asserts every `runtime:` module imports and evaluates under `CapabilitySet::none()`.
- **`permissions` lives on `runtime:process`, not a new module.** A single process is the running entity and the policy is a property of it — Node's placement (`process.permission`) for the same reason. `permissions.has(name)` is a **synchronous boolean** and there is no `request()`: the policy is fixed at launch, so a promise would imply a prompt that can never happen. The backing op is **ungated** — it reveals only what the guest could learn by calling each op and catching the denial, and code that must ask "may I?" is exactly the code running under the tightest policy.
- **`Runtime::set_capabilities` now also refreshes a live view** (`Rc<Cell<CapabilitySet>>`) that the reporting op reads, so the answer is never stale. The engine's own set remains the security boundary; the mirror is never consulted to authorize anything.

**Consequences:** `esrun` gains a real sandbox without a breaking change to its default: existing command lines behave exactly as before, and `--deny-all` is one flag away. The eight-name vocabulary is now public surface — adding a ninth capability means adding its flag, its name, and its entry in `HOST_FACING`, or `--deny-all` silently stops covering it (the `only_host_facing_capabilities_have_flag_names` test enforces the pairing). Denials remain **coarse**: `--deny-net` is all-or-nothing, not per-host. Scoped values (`--deny-net=<hosts>`, `--deny-read=<paths>`) are the obvious next step and are where D24/D25's deferred "additional allowed roots" relax flag would land; they need enforcement inside `default-providers` (post-canonicalization for paths, per-redirect-hop for `fetch`), which is why they are not in this change. **Rejected, not deferred: `permissions.deny(name)`** — irreversible runtime narrowing, as Node's `permission.drop()` does. It was proposed during this design and turned down (maintainer, 2026-08-01), because it would undo the property that makes `has()` useful and add a second authority over the same policy:
- **The answer stops being stable.** `has()` is currently a fixed fact about the run, decided by the command line, so a library can check it once and cache it. Runtime narrowing makes it time-dependent, forcing every consumer to re-check before every call or be silently wrong.
- **It is a denial-of-service primitive handed to the least trusted code.** `permissions` is a module export, so any transitive dependency could irreversibly revoke a capability the application needs, with no recovery (irreversibility being the point) and nothing on the command line to explain it.
- **Two authorities over one policy is the confusion this decision exists to avoid.** `esrun --deny-net app.js` is auditable before the run; a `deny()` on line 400 of a dependency is not. The whole shape of D38 — one vocabulary, two mutually exclusive modes — is about there being exactly one place the policy is decided.
- **It inverts the architecture.** Authority flows inward from the host (D5/D7); the guest mutating its own grants is the wrong actor. Node needs `drop()` because its model is process-global with no embedder seam — this runtime *has* one (`Runtime::set_capabilities`), and a CLI user wanting a reduced-privilege phase can spawn a subprocess with tighter flags.
- **The motivating pattern does not survive scrutiny.** "Read config at boot, then drop `fs`" only helps against an attacker who arrives strictly after the drop point; one who arrives before it prevents the drop, and one who arrives after is in the same trust domain as the code that called it.

It is also the only piece that would have needed a new mutation path into `OpState.capabilities` — the security boundary itself — which is a poor trade for a speculative benefit. Verified by 8 `common` unit tests (the vocabulary, `without_host_access`, `denied_names`), 2 `runtime` tests (every built-in imports with nothing granted; the name list matches the registry), and 15 end-to-end CLI tests. Documented per D27.

**Amendment (`--allow-<name>` + a strict parser, 2026-08-01).** The original decision shipped denials only, which left `--deny-all` unusable for the case that matters most to a *server*: it includes `--deny-imports`, so `node_modules` never loads and a fully denied run is single-file. The maintainer asked what a server deployment actually needs, and the answer is not coarse denial at all — a server *needs* net and fs, so `--deny-net` is not a flag anyone can pass. What protects a server is naming what it may reach, which only exists in allow-list form. So the escape hatch the original decision deliberately left open is now taken, under a **third rule** that keeps the design's central property intact:

1. `--deny-all` and `--deny-<name>` are mutually exclusive *(unchanged)*.
2. **`--allow-<name>` requires `--deny-all`.** Against the default baseline — everything granted — an allow is either a no-op or a direct contradiction of its own `--deny-<name>` sibling.
3. Each mode therefore has exactly **one direction**: `--deny-<name>` subtracts from everything; `--deny-all --allow-<name>` adds to nothing. **No flag ever overrides another**, in either mode, so there is still no precedence rule anywhere — which is the property rule 1 existed to protect.

What this costs: "everything on, but network narrowed to one host" is not expressible without spelling out the other seven allows. That case is rare and arguably incoherent (caring enough to scope the network means caring enough to start from `--deny-all`), and `--deny-net --allow-net=host` remains addable later as a deliberate scoped-narrowing rule — additive, and breaking nothing.

**Parser strictness — one grammar for the whole CLI.** The maintainer called for a strict parser and, on review of the first attempt, for it to be strict *everywhere* rather than only on the new flags: the first cut left `--timeout 500` taking a space-separated value while permission flags required `=`, which is two grammars in one CLI and exactly the kind of looseness the request was aimed at. The rule is now uniform, and it is **two rules for every flag**:

1. **A flag is `--flag` or `--flag=value`.** A value never arrives as a separate argument. This is what makes the parser total: with one rule it never has to decide whether the next word is a flag's value or the script to run, so there is nothing to decide wrong. `--timeout 500`, `--env-file .env` and `-e 'code'` are now errors that name the fix. **Breaking**, deliberately, pre-1.0.
2. **esrun's flags come before the script**; everything after it belongs to the script. A flag esrun knows, written after the script, is an error rather than a silent no-op — `--` opts a script's own argument out.

On top of the grammar, each of these fails loudly rather than guessing, and for the permission flags the reason is sharper than tidiness — the silent version is a *security* failure, a run the user believes is narrower than it is:
- **A value on a permission flag is rejected, never ignored.** `--allow-net=example.com` errors with "scoping is not implemented yet". Accepting and dropping the value would leave the user believing the run is scoped to one host when the network is wide open. This is the single most important rule here, and it stays until scoping actually lands.
- **A bare word following a flag is diagnosed.** `--allow-net example.com app.js` obeys rule 1 — `example.com` is simply the script — but that is not what the user meant, and running it would give a "cannot read" three steps from the cause. The parser remembers which flag preceded and says so.
- **Any esrun flag after the script is an error** (rule 2), not just a permission one: a misplaced `--timeout=500` is a confusing no-op for the same reason a misplaced `--deny-net` is a dangerous one.
- **A value on a flag that takes none** (`--env-override=yes`, `--version=1`) is named rather than swallowed by the "unknown option" arm, which would hide what is actually wrong.
- **`--allow-all` / `-A`** (a Deno habit) points at the default rather than reading as an unknown option.
- **An unknown name** in either family lists the eight.

**Settled ahead of time: the value grammar for scoping.** When scoped values do land they attach with `=` (rule 1) and hold a **comma-separated list**, with surrounding whitespace on each entry trimmed — so `--allow-net="a, b"` and `--allow-net=a,b` are the same thing, and quoting is a shell convenience rather than a syntax difference. An empty entry (`a,,b`) is an error, since a typo must not silently widen or narrow a list. Recorded now so the syntax users see the day it ships is the syntax decided here, not whatever the first implementation happened to do.

**Consequences of the amendment:** `--deny-all --allow-imports --allow-net ... server.js` is now the expressible shape for a real deployment, and the deploy manifest doubles as a readable statement of what the service may touch. The eight names are unchanged and now carry both prefixes. Coarse-only remains the gap: the allow flags are all-or-nothing, so the *scoping* that gives a server its real protection (`--allow-net=db.internal:5432` stopping an exfiltration a compromised dependency attempts over the app's own legitimate network access) is still the outstanding work — and rules 1–3 were chosen so it can land per capability without changing any of them. Verified by 14 further end-to-end CLI tests (the allow layer, and one per strictness rule), 29 in that file total.

**Amendment (scoped grants, first two capabilities — `run` and `env`, 2026-08-01).** The value grammar settled above now has an implementation behind it, for the two capabilities where enforcement is a **provider-side list check and nothing more**: `--allow-run=<programs>` and `--allow-env=<names>`. Scoping is per capability by construction — each one is enforced in a different provider, at a different choke point — so it lands one capability at a time rather than as a single feature, and the flags for the other six keep rejecting a value until their enforcement exists. `run` and `env` come first because they are the two that need no new machinery (`SystemCommands::with_allowlist` was already the embedder's seam for granting `Run` without granting a shell) and because, for a *server*, `--allow-env` is the highest security-per-line flag in the set: the environment is where the credentials are, and a service needs perhaps five variables out of a hundred.
- **A scoped grant grants the capability.** `--allow-env=HOME` sets the `Env` bit, so `permissions.has("env")` is `true` and `env` is absent from `permissions.denied`. The capability bit answers "is the door open"; the scope list is what the provider then declines to hand over. Reporting it as denied would be the more damaging lie: code checking `has("env")` would take its no-environment path and never read the variable it was deliberately given. The corollary is that `has()` alone cannot answer "may I read *this*" — and, as of the amendment below, it never will: the per-value form is **rejected**, not deferred.
- **Unlisted environment variables are absent, not unreadable.** `env` hands out a map, so the honest way to withhold one is to leave it out; there is no per-variable throw to design and nothing for a guest to probe. It also denies *enumeration*, which matters on its own — the names alone (`AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY`, `DATABASE_URL`) tell an attacker where the host keeps its secrets. The filter is applied after the `--env-file` overlay is merged, so an overlay is not a way around the list.
- **A scoped denial is a different error from a capability denial.** Spawning an unlisted program fails with `ERR_PERMISSION_DENIED` ("you have `run`, but not this program"), where `--deny-run` fails with `ERR_CAPABILITY_DENIED` ("you never had `run`"). Two different facts, and a caller that retries or degrades needs to tell them apart. This came out of the existing provider allowlist rather than being designed here, which is a point in its favour.
- **Repeating a scoped flag unions its entries** (`--allow-run=git --allow-run=ls` ≡ `--allow-run=git,ls`): two flags that both *add* can be read in any order, so rule 3 survives intact.
- **Mixing the bare and scoped forms of one flag is an error** (`--allow-env --allow-env=HOME`). There is no precedence rule to reach for and that is the design: taking the wider flag widens a run the user asked to narrow, and taking the narrower one silently ignores a flag they typed.
- **A denial still takes no value.** `--deny-run=git` is rejected with the shape that expresses the intent (`--deny-all --allow-run=<list>`). "Everything except these" is the second direction rule 3 exists to forbid; `--deny-net --allow-net=host` remains the deliberately-left-open door, unchanged by this.

**Consequences:** `esrun --deny-all --allow-imports --allow-env=PORT,DATABASE_URL --allow-run=git server.js` is now a statement of exactly which two variables and which one program the service may touch, and it is enforced. `scope_hint()` in the CLI is the single list of what accepts a value, so a capability is scopable only once its enforcement exists — a value on any of the other six is still rejected, now with an error that names the two that work. **Still outstanding:** `net` (the one that actually stops exfiltration; needs a per-hop check via `redirect::Policy::custom`, since reqwest follows redirects on a client-level policy and a 302 to a denied host would otherwise slip through), `listen` (bind-address matching, `0.0.0.0` vs a specific interface), and `read`/`write` (must be checked *after* canonicalization or a symlink walks out of the list — ~30 methods behind 3 choke points, and where D24/D25's deferred "additional allowed roots" will land). Verified by 5 provider unit tests (the allowlist, the empty list, exact matching, the overlay interaction) and 12 further end-to-end CLI tests (both capabilities, and one per rule of the value grammar), 45 in that file total. Documented per D27.

**Amendment (scoped addresses — `net` and `listen`, 2026-08-01).** The capability that motivated scoping in the first place. A server *needs* the network, so `--deny-net` is not a flag anyone can pass; what protects it is naming where it may go, so that a compromised dependency exfiltrating over the app's own legitimate access has to reach an address the operator wrote down.
- **One address type, five call sites.** `HostAllowlist` in `default-providers` is consulted by `fetch`, `runtime:net` `connect`, and `WebSocket` for `net`, and by `runtime:net` `listen` and `runtime:http` `serve` for `listen`. A capability is one grant; which provider happens to serve which API is not something a policy should have to know, and five separate matchers would be five places for them to drift.
- **`net` and `listen` are separate lists**, as they are separate capabilities. Reaching out and being reachable have nothing to do with each other, and an address that makes sense for one (`api.example.com`) is meaningless for the other.
- **The grammar: a host (any port), a `host:port`, or a bare port (any host).** The bare port is written for `listen`, where the port is usually the whole intent and the interface is the deployment's business; it settles the `0.0.0.0`-vs-specific-interface question by not guessing — a bind must match an entry exactly, and an operator who does not want to name the interface writes the port alone. IPv6 brackets when it carries a port (`[::1]:8080`), exactly as in a URL.
- **Matching is exact and never widens.** `example.com` does not admit `api.example.com`: a subdomain is a different server, often a different operator, and a list that quietly covered them would grant more than it reads as. No wildcards, for the same reason. Hosts are judged **as written, before resolution** — so a denied name cannot be smuggled through by an attacker-controlled DNS answer, an IP entry never silently admits a name that resolves to it, and DNS is not part of the policy surface.
- **Every redirect hop is checked, and this was the hard part.** reqwest's redirect policy is a property of the *client*, not of a request, so an allowlist enforced where the guest wrote the URL and nowhere else would follow a `302` from an allowed host to a denied one transparently and hand back the denied host's body — the exfiltration the flag exists to stop, wearing the flag as cover. `redirect::Policy::custom` now checks each hop, which also means re-implementing the Fetch redirect cap that `Policy::limited` was providing. Both a refused hop and an exhausted budget reach us as `is_redirect()` errors, so the refusal travels in a private error type that `classify_reqwest` unwraps into `ERR_PERMISSION_DENIED` rather than `ERR_TOO_MANY_REDIRECTS`.
- **A malformed address is an argument error**, reported against the flag before anything runs, rather than a provider failure at the first connect. The list is therefore parsed twice — once in the parser for the message, once at wiring time for the providers — which is a handful of strings for a much better error.

**Consequences:** the deploy manifest is now a readable, enforced statement of which hosts a service may reach and which port it may claim: `esrun --deny-all --allow-imports --allow-net=db.internal:5432 --allow-listen=8080 --allow-env=PORT,DATABASE_URL server.js`. Four of the eight names now take a list. **Still outstanding: `read`/`write`** — the check must run *after* canonicalization or a symlink walks out of the list, and it is where D24/D25's deferred "additional allowed roots" will land; `imports` and `signals` remain coarse (a path list for the loader is the same problem as `read`; a signal list is a vocabulary, not an address). Verified by 12 `HostAllowlist` unit tests (the three entry shapes, exactness, IP normalization, malformed entries), 9 provider tests (per socket, plus both redirect directions), and 6 further end-to-end CLI tests, 51 in that file total. Documented per D27.

**Amendment (scoped paths — `read` and `write`, 2026-08-01).** The last pair, and the one D24/D25 has been waiting for: a path list *is* the "additional allowed roots" relax flag those decisions deferred, pointing the other way — it narrows rather than widens, which is the direction that needs no new trust.
- **The check runs after canonicalization, and that is the whole design.** A path is a *name* for a file; a symlink lets one name stand for a file somewhere else. Judging the name the guest wrote would let `--allow-read=./data` admit `./data/link-to-etc/passwd` — precisely the hole the root jail (D25) exists to close, reopened one level in. So the list holds canonicalized entries and is matched against the real path the jail has already resolved. An entry naming a file that does not exist yet keeps its literal tail attached to the part that does, which is the same shape the jail produces for a file about to be created, so the two compare.
- **The jail was the reason this was cheap.** ~30 filesystem methods funnel through three choke points (`jailed`, `jailed_nofollow`, `temp_base`, plus the synchronous jail's own), because the root jail already needed exactly one place to resolve a path. What the choke point did *not* know is whether a call is a read or a write, so an `Access` now travels with the path: `rename` is a write on both ends (the source is removed), `copy` reads the source and writes the destination, and a synchronous `open` needs the access it asks for — both, when it asks for both, since the later `read`/`write` calls have only an fd and the path is gone.
- **A list narrows the jail.** ~~An entry outside the project root grants nothing~~ — **amended by D54**: an entry outside the jail now *adds* that subtree, because the cert-and-key case proved the rule made HTTPS unservable. Inside the jail the list narrows as described, and a path in neither the jail nor the list is still `ERR_JAIL_ESCAPE` rather than a scoped denial: two different problems with two different fixes should not share an error.
- **`runtime:fs` and `runtime:wasi` take the same lists.** They are two doors onto one filesystem; a policy that differed between them would be a bug wearing a feature's clothes, and a WASI program would be the way around `--allow-read`.
- **Entries resolve against the working directory**, not the entry file's directory. `./data` on a command line means what it means in the shell it was typed in; the jail's base is the script's directory, which is right for the guest's own relative paths and wrong for a flag.

**Amendment (scoped imports and signals — the last two, 2026-08-01).** Both were argued as probably-not-worth-it in the previous amendment; the maintainer asked for the scoping work to be finished rather than shipped two names short, and on building them the arguments against turned out to be weaker than they looked.
*(Withdrawn before release — see D39: `imports` keeps its capability flags, but what may be loaded moved to an import policy. The paragraphs below record the design that produced the matcher D39 now uses.)*

- **`--allow-imports=<packages|paths>` reads its entries the way the loader reads specifiers.** An entry beginning with `.` or `/` is a path covering its subtree; anything else is a package name (`lodash`, `@scope/pkg`). That is not a second grammar to learn — it is the split the loader already makes between a bare and a relative specifier, which is what makes the flag legible without documentation.
- **A module inside `node_modules` is judged as its package, not as a path.** A package whose own files could not load would be no grant at all, so an allowed package covers its own tree — but it says nothing about the packages *it* imports, including a nested `node_modules` copy. Each package is named in its own right, whoever pulled it in, which is the property that makes the flag worth having: a dependency cannot quietly bring another along. The cost is that a large tree is impractical to list, and the honest advice there is to grant `--allow-imports` whole.
- **Checked on the resolved module, in `finalize`, after the sandbox root.** Same discipline as the filesystem lists, and it is also what makes package matching work under pnpm: `node_modules/lodash` is a symlink into a content store, and the real path still ends in `…/node_modules/lodash/…`, so the package is recoverable from the path itself.
- **The entry file is exempt**, as it is under `--deny-all`: it is read before a loader exists, and the user named it.
- **`--allow-signals=<names>` narrows `watch` and narrows `available` to match.** Hiding what may not be used is the same call as an unlisted environment variable being absent: a program should enumerate what it can act on. The argument that a signal list is "a vocabulary, not an address" was true and beside the point — the grant being narrowed is *the privilege to decline to die on request*, and a service that drains on `SIGTERM` has no business also intercepting `SIGINT`, or an operator reaching for `kill` finding a dependency has taken it. An unknown name is an argument error naming the vocabulary, since a name this runtime does not know would watch nothing while reading as protection.

**Consequences:** seven of the eight names take a list (`imports` moved to D39's policy), and D38's scoping work is complete. `scope_hint()` keeps its `None` arm — not as a placeholder but as the standing rule: a ninth capability rejects a value until its enforcement exists. The end-to-end test that proved "a value on an unenforceable flag is refused" had no subject left and was replaced by one asserting every name is scopable, which is what should fail if a capability is added and its flag wired up without enforcement. Verified by 8 `ImportAllowlist` unit tests, 4 loader tests (package, path, symlink, unscoped), 2 signal-registry tests, and 3 further end-to-end CLI tests, 58 in that file total. Documented per D27.

**Amendment (`permissions.has()` takes one argument — the per-value form is rejected, 2026-08-01).** `has("read", "/etc/passwd")` previously answered about the *capability* and silently dropped the value, returning `true` under `--allow-read=./data`. That is the same lie the parser refuses to tell when it rejects a flag it cannot enforce, told by the API instead, so it now throws a `TypeError`. The maintainer's call is that it should throw permanently rather than become a feature (2026-08-01), and the reasons hold on their own:
- **Scoping is the deployment's job, not the application's.** The whole value of expressing policy on the command line is that application code stays ordinary — `fetch(url)`, `fs.file(p)`, no permission plumbing. An API that invites `if (has("read", p))` pushes the policy back into every library that might want to be well-behaved, which is exactly the bloat the flags exist to avoid.
- **The answer cannot be given honestly without doing the work.** A path is judged *after* canonicalization (that is what stops a symlink walking out of a list), so answering for one path means resolving it — a filesystem operation the caller may not be permitted to perform.
- **Any answer is stale on arrival.** Between the check and the call, `./data/x` can become a symlink elsewhere. Enforcement has to happen at the point of use regardless, so the query could only ever be advice.
- **The exact, current answer already exists**: perform the operation and catch `ERR_PERMISSION_DENIED`. `has(name)` keeps answering the question it can answer — is this door open at all — which is what a boot-time sanity check needs.

**Consequences:** all eight names now exist in both prefixes and six of them take a list — `read`, `write`, `net`, `listen`, `run`, `env`. `esrun --deny-all --allow-imports --allow-read=./data --allow-write=./out --allow-net=db.internal:5432 --allow-listen=8080 --allow-env=PORT,DATABASE_URL --allow-run=git server.js` is a complete, enforced statement of what a service may touch, and the scoping work D38 opened is finished except for two names: **`imports`** (a path list for the module loader — the same machinery as `read`, but the loader has its own resolution path, and "which packages may be imported" is arguably a different question from "which paths may be read") and **`signals`** (a vocabulary, not a location; the case for narrowing it is weak enough that nobody has asked). Both still reject a value rather than ignoring one. Verified by 6 `PathAllowlist` unit tests, 7 filesystem provider tests (including the symlink walk-out and the jail-still-wins case) and 4 further end-to-end CLI tests, 55 in that file total. Documented per D27.

### D39 — import & loader policy: what may be *loaded* is not a capability · *Locked (maintainer sign-off, 2026-08-02)*
**Context:** D38 finished by giving all eight permission names a scope list, `--allow-imports=<packages|paths>` among them. Building it exposed that it did not fit. The other seven lists bound what running code may *reach*; this one bounded what may *become* running code, and the difference showed up as three separate awkwardnesses: the useful version needs integrity hashes, aliases and org-wide rules, none of which fit on a command line; a "warn instead of fail" mode is reasonable for dependency governance and unthinkable for a capability check; and a package list long enough to be honest about a real dependency graph is unwritable as a comma-separated flag. The maintainer proposed splitting the concern out (2026-08-02) rather than growing the permission model to fit.

**Decision (maintainer):** Separate the two questions, and give each its own mechanism.
- **Capabilities answer "what may executing code reach"** — filesystem, network, environment, processes. Flags, one vocabulary of eight names, D38's rules unchanged.
- **The import policy answers "which modules may become executing code"** — a JSON file named by `--import-policy=<file>`.

They are **two layers, not two alternatives**, and the layering is the load-bearing part:
- The `imports` **capability** decides whether the loader runs *at all*. It stays in `HOST_FACING`, which is what makes `--deny-all` mean "runs only the file you named" — remove it and a fully denied run could still pull in arbitrary local files. A policy therefore cannot re-open a loader that `--deny-all` closed; the capability is checked first, the policy second.
- The **policy** decides, given that the loader runs, what it may resolve. It grants nothing: it can only narrow.

**The v1 format is `allow` and/or `deny` lists** of package names and paths:
```json
{ "allow": ["./src", "express", "@acme/ui"], "deny": ["aws-sdk"] }
```
- **Entries are read the way specifiers are.** `.`/`/` prefix ⇒ a path covering its subtree; anything else ⇒ a package name. This is the split the loader already makes between a bare and a relative specifier, so there is no second grammar.
- **Deny wins over allow.** One rule to remember. "The more specific entry wins" would make the answer depend on comparing two entries, which is the precedence reasoning D38 removed from the flags; there is no reason to reintroduce it in a file.
- **Omitting `allow` permits everything not denied** — the shape for an organisation excluding a handful of packages without enumerating the whole graph. `"allow": []` is an error rather than a silent "nothing loads".
- **Unknown keys are an error.** A misspelled `"allowed"` that parsed as an empty policy would read as protection that is not there — the same reason the argument parser refuses a flag it does not know.
- **Paths resolve relative to the policy file**, not the working directory: a policy is committed next to the project it governs and should mean the same thing wherever it is invoked from. (Contrast `--allow-read=./data`, whose entries resolve against the working directory, because a flag is typed in a shell.)
- **Never auto-discovered.** Read only when `--import-policy` names it, exactly as `--env-file` works (D30).
- Matching runs on the **resolved, canonicalized** module, after the sandbox root — a symlink cannot name its way in, and a pnpm store path is still recognisably its package. A module inside `node_modules` is judged as the package it belongs to, so an allowed package's own files load while the packages *it* imports are named in their own right.
- The **entry file is exempt**: it is read before a loader exists, and the user named it.

**Out of scope, deliberately.** Built-in `runtime:` modules are **not** policy-governed: D26 says importing one always works and only its operations throw, which gives a `NotAllowedError` at the call site instead of a module-not-found error three steps from the cause. Capabilities already answer that question.

**Consequences:** `--allow-imports=<list>` is withdrawn before it ever shipped, so nothing carries over; `imports` keeps its bare flags. The permission model stops at seven scoped names plus one gate, and the room the split creates is real: **integrity hashes** (the gap that `--allow-imports=lodash` could never close — it names a package, not a version, and says nothing about whether it is the code you audited), **aliases and replacement**, **warn-only rollout**, and org-wide policy files, none of which would have been safe to add to a permission flag. Aliases and replacement in particular need a trust rule written down before they land: a policy that can substitute one module for another is a code-execution primitive, so it must be host-supplied, read before the sandbox exists, and never reachable through the guest's own filesystem grants. **Not solved here:** the policy still names packages rather than pinning content, so it bounds *which* dependencies run and not *whether they are the ones you audited* — integrity is the next piece, and lockfiles remain the install-time counterpart. Verified by 13 `ImportPolicy` unit tests (both lists, precedence, the deny-only shape, path resolution, malformed input), 4 loader tests, and 7 end-to-end CLI tests including the two-layer case (a policy cannot re-open what `--deny-all` closed). Documented per D27.

### D40 — ESM resolution completed: author-order conditions, standards-only condition set, `imports` and self-reference · *Proposed (2026-08-02)*
**Context:** D22 shipped the useful subset of `node_modules` resolution and named what it left out: full condition precedence, `"imports"`/`#internal`, and self-reference. The subset was picking a target with a fixed `import`-then-`default` preference over a `serde_json::Value`, which sorts object keys — so the manifest's own order was not merely ignored, it was unavailable. That is fine until a package writes `{"default": …, "import": …}`, ships a nested condition object whose matched branch is empty, or uses an array fallback; each of those resolves to the wrong file or to nothing, and the failure looks like "package not found" rather than "the loader does not implement this".

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):** implement the whole algorithm, and settle the condition vocabulary while doing it.
- **The condition set is standards-only: `import` and `default`.** No `node`, no `browser`, and — the part worth stating — **no ES-Runtime key of our own**. Runtime-branded conditions are how a package learns which runtime it is on so it can branch; a package that needs to branch on us is a package this runtime is not trying to run, and inventing the key would invite exactly the fork it is meant to serve. `require` is never asserted (D24), but a `require`-only node is remembered so the error says "this package is CommonJS" instead of "not exported". Rejected: matching `node` for reach — a `node`-conditioned build is precisely the one that imports `node:` builtins, so it would trade a clear resolution error for an obscure load-time one.
- **Precedence is the manifest's key order**, per the algorithm, with nested objects walked the same way and a matched-but-empty branch falling through to the next key. This needed key-order-preserving parsing, and the workspace-wide `serde_json/preserve_order` feature was **rejected** — it would silently change how *user* data is serialized by the TOML/MessagePack builders in `runtime`, which has nothing to do with resolution. A small ordered JSON tree (~90 lines, `esm_resolve::Json`) buys the correct semantics with no blast radius outside this file.
- **Targets are validated, and validation is a security boundary, not tidiness.** After `*` substitution, no target may contain a `..`, `.` or `node_modules` segment; `exports` targets must start with `./`; trailing-slash directory mappings are refused. Without the post-substitution check, `"./*": "./dist/*.js"` lets any consumer name `pkg/../../anything` and read it *as the package* — the D25 root jail would still hold, but the package boundary inside the root would not. Validation runs in the pure layer, so it is tested without a filesystem.
- **`imports` and self-reference land together**, because they are the same question asked twice: what does a specifier mean *inside* the package that declares it. `#private` targets may name a path, a pattern, or another package (resolved from the declaring package, so its own dependencies are in scope); self-reference requires `exports`, since without one there is no public map to be consistent with.
- **A malformed manifest is an error naming the `package.json`**, never a silent "not found" — an invalid target or an `exports` object mixing subpath and condition keys is a bug in the package, and saying so is the difference between a five-minute fix and an afternoon.
- **Pure/impure split.** The algorithm (`esm_resolve`) maps a parsed manifest + subpath to a target string; `node_modules` keeps the walk, the probe, the realpath, the jail and the policy check. The pure half is where the edge cases are, and it is testable without a temp tree.

**Consequences:** modern pure-ESM packages resolve as their authors intended, including the shapes the subset silently mis-resolved (author-order conditions, nested fall-through, array fallbacks, `null` withdrawals). The D22 deferral list is closed. The condition set is now public surface: adding a key later is a compatibility event, since a package can observe which branch it got — which is the reason for choosing the smallest defensible set now rather than the most permissive. **Not solved here:** an `esrun --conditions=<list>` escape hatch (Node and Deno both have one) is deliberately not shipped — it is a real need for monorepos with dev/prod builds, but a user-supplied condition changes which code runs, so it wants its own decision alongside the D39 policy layer rather than a flag added in passing. `import.meta.resolve` for bare specifiers stays deferred for the D22 reason (it is synchronous; this is I/O). Verified by 16 `esm_resolve` unit tests (key order, precedence, nesting, arrays, `null`, patterns, every validation rule, `imports` shapes), 5 new loader integration tests over real trees (self-reference, `#private` including a package target, withdrawal, author order, an escaping target), and 2 end-to-end CLI suites driving the real binary. Documented per D27 (`SPEC.md`, `API.md`, site `docs/modules`, `CHANGELOG`).

### D41 — `import.meta.resolve` resolves for real: one synchronous core, an optional loader method · *Proposed (2026-08-02)*
**Context:** `import.meta.resolve` shipped as pure URL resolution: relative, absolute-path and absolute-URL specifiers resolved, and a bare one threw a `TypeError` saying resolution needs `node_modules` and `resolve` is synchronous. That reasoning was sound when `ModuleLoader::resolve` was the only path and it was async (D22). Two things have since changed it: **D36** added `SyncFileSystem`, a genuinely synchronous capability-gated seam, proving blocking host I/O on this thread is something the runtime already does; and **D40** made the resolution *algorithm* pure, so a second entry point no longer means a second implementation. What remained was a gap with a real cost: there is no way to locate a **non-JS file inside a dependency** — migrations, a `.proto`, templates, a CA bundle — because the install layout (pnpm's store, hoisting) is exactly what resolution knows and a hand-built path does not.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**
- **Synchronous, because the language says so.** `import.meta.resolve` returns a string, not a promise. An async twin under `runtime:` was considered and **rejected**: it would be non-standard, it colours every caller that wants a path, and it buys nothing — the `await fs.readFile` that follows was already async. Two resolvers would be two code paths obliged to agree forever, for a difference no user benefits from.
- **One core, two entry points.** `NodeModuleLoader`'s resolution is now a synchronous function; the async `ModuleLoader::resolve` returns `ready(core(..))` and `resolve_sync` calls it directly. This is the load-bearing choice: `import.meta.resolve` and `import()` cannot drift, because there is nothing to drift *from*. The confinement checks (D25 root jail, D39 import policy) live inside that core, so both paths are confined identically — a policy-refused package is refused to `resolve` too, or `resolve` would report the location of a module the policy exists to keep out.
- **`resolve_sync` is an optional trait method defaulting to `None`.** An embedder whose modules come from a network or a database cannot answer synchronously — that is impossible, not merely slow — so the default keeps their guests on today's `TypeError` and the change is not breaking. Node reaches the same place with sync `fs`.
- **Gated on `FileSystem`, like the loader itself.** `resolve` now touches the disk, so a run denied imports must not get it as a filesystem-probing oracle: "is this package installed" is an answer worth withholding from code that was denied the loader. Under `--deny-all` it fails as an import fails.
- **Cost is bounded and mostly at startup:** a handful of `stat`s and `package.json` reads, page-cached, on a path that no request touches. Rejected as premature: a resolution cache — worth adding when a workload shows repeat cost, not before.

**Consequences:** `import.meta.resolve("pkg/migrations/001.sql")` now answers, which closes the "find an asset inside a dependency" gap for the packages that ship data rather than only code; JS and `.wasm` were already reachable through `import()` and the WebAssembly ESM integration. `ModuleLoader` gains a method — public surface, though a defaulted one — and the pure/impure split from D40 is what made the whole change small. **Not solved here:** `import.meta.resolve` still does no existence check for *path* specifiers (unchanged, and matching the platform), and a loader without a synchronous path still refuses rather than blocking a promise into a string. Verified by a provider parity test asserting the sync and async paths agree — same ids, and the *same errors* — across ten specifier shapes (relative, bare, self-reference, withdrawn subpath, private, undeclared private, missing package, `node:`, root escape), a policy test binding the sync path to D39, an end-to-end test that imports the URL `resolve` returned and asserts it is the same module instance, and a permissions test asserting a denied run learns nothing. Documented per D27.

### D42 — HTTP/2 in `runtime:http`: version detection per connection, h2c by prior knowledge · *Proposed (2026-08-03)*
**Context:** D31 shipped the `serve()` server on hyper's HTTP/1.1 connection path, and D20's `fetch` client has spoken HTTP/2 since it was written — so the runtime *originated* h2 while refusing to serve it. That asymmetry has a cost past keeping-up: an origin that only speaks HTTP/1.1 cannot sit behind a proxy that talks h2 to its backend, cannot serve a gRPC client, and gives up multiplexing precisely where it hurts most — a single-threaded isolate answering many concurrent requests, where one connection per in-flight request means the connection count is the concurrency. The handoff D31 built (`(request, oneshot)` per request, not per connection) already answers requests out of order; the wire format was the only thing that could not.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**
- **The version is the client's choice, detected per connection**, via hyper-util's `auto` builder rather than two accept paths. Everything above the socket — the service, the response handoff, the drain — is version-agnostic and stays a single code path, which is the point: a second path is how the two quietly drift apart, and the streaming/backpressure/disconnect semantics are the parts that must not.
- **Cleartext h2c is by prior knowledge — the connection preface — and the `Upgrade:` header dance is deliberately not implemented.** The `h2c` upgrade mechanism is deprecated in the standard and no client depends on it; prior knowledge is what proxies and gRPC clients actually send when TLS terminates in front of the runtime. Accepting only what is used keeps a parser off the untrusted path rather than adding one for symmetry.
- **The default ALPN list becomes `["h2", "http/1.1"]` (h2 first, since ALPN order is the server's preference).** This is a behaviour change for existing HTTPS servers: an h2-capable client that used to be served HTTP/1.1 now gets HTTP/2. It is the right default — the version is invisible to the handler, so nothing in guest code can observe it — and the pre-existing `alpn` option is the escape hatch, `alpn: ["http/1.1"]`, for a client that mishandles h2.
- **Concurrent streams per connection are capped explicitly (256), not inherited.** An HTTP/2 peer opens streams far faster than a single-threaded isolate answers them, and each open stream holds a queued request plus its body channel. The cap is what stops one connection from deciding how much the server holds; the request channel's buffer then spreads across connections instead of being filled by one client. Rejected: leaving it to the library default, which is a resource limit chosen by someone who does not know the consumer is one isolate.
- **`request.url` is rebuilt from `:authority` when there is no `Host`.** HTTP/2 replaced the header with a pseudo-header; reading only the header would have left every h2 request reporting the *bound address* as its host — a silent wrong answer for anything doing virtual hosting, not an error.
- **No new dependency.** `h2` is already in the tree behind the HTTP/2-capable `fetch` client, so this is a hyper feature flag, not a supply-chain event (D7).

**Consequences:** one port now serves both versions with the same handler, so multiplexing, a single TLS handshake per session, and HPACK header compression arrive without a guest-visible API. The `Response` a handler returns is unchanged, and framing stays the server's to compute — HTTP/2 forbids `Transfer-Encoding`, so a streamed body is framed by the version rather than chunk-encoded. **Not solved here:** HTTP/3 (QUIC), which is a UDP transport with its own TLS integration and congestion control — a new listener and a new dependency rather than another version negotiated on the connection `serve()` already accepts. It is not infeasible: Bun ships an HTTP/3 server on an embedded lsquic, so the shape of the answer is known, and whether to take a C QUIC library into the tree (against D7 and §7) is the decision that would need making. Also not solved: server push, which is deprecated and removed from browsers; and h2 for `wss:` (RFC 8441 extended CONNECT), which waits on the WebSocket *server* that is still a follow-up. Verified by 13 provider tests over real sockets (h2c on a cleartext port; out-of-order responses to concurrent streams, which deadlocks on HTTP/1.1; one listener answering an HTTP/1.1 *and* an HTTP/2 client; ALPN negotiating h2 over TLS; a narrowed `alpn` holding a capable client on HTTP/1.1; streamed bodies in both directions, including an upload that exhausts the initial stream window; method/status/header round trips through HPACK; connection-specific headers not breaking the response; a 1 MiB body crossing flow control; an abandoned stream reported as a disconnect; an unanswerable request ending as a 500; and a drain finishing an in-flight request), 3 runtime-layer tests pinning the guest-visible ALPN default and its override, and an end-to-end CLI test driving the real binary with hand-written HTTP/2 frames over `runtime:net` — three concurrent streams, a request body as DATA frames, the advertised stream cap read out of the server's `SETTINGS`, and an HTTP/1.1 `fetch` to the same port — since `fetch` speaks HTTP/1.1 to a cleartext origin and so cannot prove any of it alone. Benchmarked by `bench/http2.sh` in two client shapes: on one connection with 50 streams, **73,541 req/s — a 3.65× gain and the fastest of the four runtimes measured** (Bun 49,142, Node 39,700, Deno 39,209); across 50 connections, **0.79×**, a loss. Both halves ship, because the loss is the honest shape of the trade and a single number would hide it. The table states which of its comparisons are apples-to-apples: down a column always, the h2÷h1 ratio only for the runtimes that answer both versions on *one* server (us and Deno) — Node's and Bun's cleartext h2 is a different server (`node:http2`) from their h1, so their ratio carries an implementation change too. Sampling follows the existing `run.sh` methodology (interleaved, shuffled, warmup discarded, best of N) rather than a second one invented for this script. Documented per D27 (`SPEC.md`, `API.md`, `types/`, site `api/http` + `docs/http`, `CHANGELOG`).

### D43 — Connection timeouts: on by default, opt-out per server · *Proposed (2026-08-03)*
**Context:** the server had no deadline of any kind. A peer could complete the TCP handshake and then say nothing — one syscall, no state to keep — and hold a task and a file descriptor for as long as it liked; a request head could dribble forever; an HTTP/2 peer that vanished without a FIN kept its connection *and* its share of the 256-stream budget (D42) until the OS TCP keepalive noticed, two hours later on Linux. hyper had in fact shipped a defence for the middle one: `header_read_timeout` defaults to 30s. It was never running, because a *default* timeout with no `Timer` installed is silently dropped with a `warn!` (hyper's `common/time.rs`) and no timer was ever installed — so the protection was present, off, and saying so in a log nobody reads.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**
- **On by default, disabled per option.** `HttpTimeouts::default()` is the posture — 10s handshake, 30s header read, 20s HTTP/2 keepalive — and a guest turns one off with `timeouts: { headerRead: null }`. A timeout nobody configures protects nobody, and the deployments that genuinely want none (a private port behind a proxy that already does this) are the ones best placed to say so. Rejected: shipping the knobs off, which would have left every existing program exactly as exposed as before while letting us claim the issue was addressed.
- **Three stages, not one number.** A connection can stall before TLS finishes, before its version is known, before its request head completes, or after it goes idle, and those are different waits with different right answers. Rejected: a single "connection timeout", which cannot both allow a 30s idle keep-alive and kill a 10s dead handshake.
- **The first-byte deadline comes from under the socket.** hyper-util's version detection reads up to 24 bytes *before* either kind of connection is constructed, so no timer hyper owns can reach a peer stalling there. `FirstByteTimeout` wraps the stream, applies the deadline until one byte lands, and goes inert after — a long-lived connection must not be interrupted by it. Rejected: wrapping the whole connection future in `tokio::time::timeout`, which bounds the version-detect phase by also killing every connection that outlives it.
- **The values live in exactly one place.** `HttpTimeouts::default()` in `providers`; the prelude sends `null` for "the guest said nothing" and `0` for "off". Rejected: defaults in the JS prelude, which is two copies of three numbers and a drift waiting to happen.
- **HTTP/1.1's idle limit is the header-read timer, not a second one.** hyper arms that timer whenever it waits for a request head, and waiting for the *next* request on a kept-alive connection is exactly that. Splitting them would mean our own activity tracker and watchdog; the merged behaviour is also what nginx (75s) and Node (5s keep-alive) ship.

**Consequences:** the cheapest connection-holding attacks now cost the peer a reconnect. The visible change for existing programs is that an **idle HTTP/1.1 keep-alive connection is closed after 30s** where it previously lived forever — clients reopen transparently, and this is standard behaviour, but it is a behaviour change and not only a hardening. HTTP/2 gains PING traffic on idle connections: one wakeup per idle connection per 20s, in exchange for reclaiming a dead peer within 40s instead of two hours. `HttpServeOptions` gains a field, which breaks an embedder's struct literal — pre-1.0 and unstable per SPEC §14, but a real break. **Not solved here:** a slow *body* read (a 1-byte-per-minute upload is still unbounded — it needs a body wrapper and its own thinking), a total request-duration cap (which would break SSE, long-polling, and large uploads, and there is no per-route override to escape it), and a cap on concurrent connections. Verified by 7 provider tests over real sockets — a silent connection, a TLS peer that never sends a ClientHello, a dribbled request head, an idle keep-alive connection, an h2 peer that ignores PINGs, a **streamed response running well past the header timeout completing untouched**, and every timeout disabled surviving the same stall — plus 4 first-byte-wrapper unit tests, 5 runtime-layer tests pinning the crossing (unset ⇒ provider default, `null` ⇒ off rather than default, one named leaving the rest alone, bad values rejected before the bind), and an end-to-end CLI test that stalls a real socket from `runtime:net`, since `fetch` will never go quiet mid-request. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/`, site `api/http` + `docs/http`, `CHANGELOG`).

### D44 — The client's address: a handler argument, and never a forwarded header · *Proposed (2026-08-03)*
**Context:** `accept` returns the peer and the server dropped it. A handler could not tell who was calling, which rules out per-IP rate limiting, abuse logging, allowlisting, geo — anything needing an identity. The address existed at the socket and died two lines later.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**
- **A second handler argument**, `(request, info)`, carrying `info.remoteAddr` in the shape `Deno.serve` passes. Additive: a one-parameter handler is unaffected, since JS discards extra arguments. Rejected: a property on the Fetch `Request` (`request.remoteAddress`), which is shorter to type and is what Node effectively has — but it puts a non-standard field on a standard object, so a handler written against it silently stops working elsewhere and code copied in from elsewhere silently reads `undefined`. Also rejected: Bun's `server.requestIP(request)`, which costs nothing until asked but needs a `WeakMap` from `Request` object to peer held for the life of every request, and makes the handler close over `server` while `server` is still being defined.
- **`X-Forwarded-For` is never consulted.** `remoteAddr` is the socket peer, one meaning, never spoofable. Rejected: an opt-in `trustProxy` allowlist (Express, Fastify), which is convenient but makes one field mean two different things depending on configuration, and turns a misconfigured allowlist into a spoofable identity. The header is delivered untouched, so a deployment that knows which hop to trust resolves it in one line of its own.
- **Unknown is `null`, not an empty address.** A provider with no socket has no peer, and an address-shaped object full of blanks is worse than useless — a handler would happily key a rate limit on the empty string.
- **Eagerly marshaled, in the batch that already crosses.** Two more values on the flat per-request array rather than an op per query. Rejected: lazy resolution, which is free for handlers that never ask but holds per-request host state on exactly the path that already tracks bodies and disconnect halves.

**Consequences:** rate limiting, abuse handling, and access logging become possible in guest code. The eager crossing costs **1.4%** of throughput on the connection-per-request shape (67,697 → 66,759 req/s, interleaved best of 5, the baseline ahead in every rep) — measured, not asserted, and worth stating plainly since the first attempt cost 2.9% until the address was formatted once per connection rather than once per request. **Not solved here:** the identity of a client behind a proxy, which is deliberate and documented rather than missing; and client-certificate identity for mTLS, which waits on per-listener certificate work. Verified by a provider test reading the address off a real accepted socket (asserted against the client socket's own ephemeral port, not a guess), an HTTP/2 test that two streams multiplexed on one connection report the same peer, three runtime-layer tests (the shape a handler sees, `null` for a provider with no peer, and a one-argument handler still working), and an end-to-end CLI test that a forged `X-Forwarded-For` changes nothing while still reaching the handler. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/`, site `api/http` + `docs/http`, `CHANGELOG`).

### D45 — A connection cap that holds rather than refuses, and off by default · *Proposed (2026-08-03)*
**Context:** nothing bounded how many connections one server would hold. D43's timeouts bound how long a connection may stall, and D42's stream cap bounds one connection's share of the isolate, but neither bounds the count. The descriptor limit is a hard ceiling and `accept` failing at it is survivable since the acceptor retries, so this is not about the process dying — it is about what a connection costs on the way there. hyper's HTTP/1.1 read buffer can reach ~408KB, so ten thousand connections that are valid, idle, and doing nothing is several gigabytes the server allocates before anything stops it.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**
- **Enforced by not accepting.** A permit is taken *before* `accept` and released when the connection ends, so at the cap the acceptor simply stops accepting: excess connections wait in the kernel's backlog, and the OS refuses them once that fills. Nothing is spent on a connection the server will not serve — no descriptor, no task, no read buffer. Rejected: accepting and closing immediately, which is friendlier to diagnose but does work proportional to the flood the cap exists for; and answering `503`, which parses a whole request head before refusing, so a flood extracts *more* work than doing nothing would.
- **Held, not refused.** When a slot frees the waiting connection is served. That is the difference between a queue and a rejection, and it is what makes a cap safe to set conservatively.
- **Unlimited by default**, unlike D43's timeouts. The right number follows from a deployment's descriptor budget and the memory a connection costs, neither of which this crate can read — there is no portable way to see `RLIMIT_NOFILE` without `unsafe`, which the crate forbids. A cap guessed here would throttle real traffic with nothing anywhere to explain it, and a silent throughput cliff is harder to diagnose than a missing limit. Measured against the field: Node (`maxConnections` unset), Deno and Go all leave it unlimited.
- **The per-connection limits are stated, not inherited.** `max_headers` (100), `max_buf_size` (~408KB) and h2 `max_header_list_size` (16KB) are now written at hyper's own current values. Nothing changes today; what changes is that a hyper release adjusting a default cannot quietly adjust ours — which is exactly how this server ran without hyper's 30s header timeout (D43).

**Consequences:** a deployment can bound its worst-case memory and descriptor use with one number, and the number it multiplies against is now written down rather than inherited. **A correction to the record:** the audit that raised this also claimed h2's `max_header_list_size` was left at a permissive default. That was wrong — hyper bounds all three header limits — so this decision only *states* them and adds no new limit. Measured against the other runtimes, our advertised HTTP/2 `SETTINGS` are the strict end of the field: 256 concurrent streams and a 16KB header list, against Deno's 200/16KB (also hyper) and **unlimited concurrent streams from both Node and Bun**, with Node advertising an unlimited header list and Bun 64KB. **Not solved here:** a per-IP connection cap (one peer can still take every slot, which needs the peer address D44 added plus a policy to apply it), and a cap shared across several servers in one process — this one is per `serve()`. Verified by two provider tests (a connection over the cap is not served while the cap is full and *is* served once a slot frees; slots are reusable rather than spent), two runtime-layer tests (unlimited unless asked, and `0`/negative/fractional rejected before the bind), and an end-to-end CLI test over real sockets, where the kernel backlog a held connection waits in actually exists. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/`, site `api/http` + `docs/http`, `CHANGELOG`).

### D46 — Trailers: a `runtime:http` export, both versions, and `TE: trailers` on every fetch · *Proposed (2026-08-03)*
**Context:** D42 brought HTTP/2, which is what gRPC runs on — but gRPC carries the status of a call in **trailers**, the header fields that follow the body, and nothing in the runtime could send or read one. A server that speaks perfect HTTP/2 and cannot send a trailer cannot serve gRPC; a client that cannot read one cannot tell a successful call from a failed one. The Fetch API is no help: `Response.trailer` was specified and then **removed**, so there is no standard surface to implement.

**Decision (proposed after measuring the field rather than from first principles):**
- **`withTrailers(response, trailers)` and `trailersOf(response)`, exported by `runtime:http`** — not an option on `Response`. The research settled this: no runtime exposes trailers on the Fetch API. `Deno.serve` ignores an attempt to send them (terminating chunk, no trailer section) and `Bun.serve` does not even switch to chunked encoding; no runtime's `fetch` surfaces them on the way in; Node does trailers only through `node:http`/`node:http2`. So a `trailers` option on `Response` would look standard while doing nothing anywhere else, and code copied *in* would silently lose trailers here. This is the same argument that kept the peer address off `Request` (D44), and the measurement strengthened it: the "least invented" case for reviving the removed draft collapsed once it was clear nobody implements that draft either. Rejected also: Node's stream-level model (`info.sendTrailers()`), which splits a response across two objects.
- **The value may be a promise**, because that is the only useful shape — a trailer exists to carry what is not known until the body has been produced. A buffered body awaits it before responding; a streamed one sends the head first and the trailers after the last chunk.
- **Both versions carry them**, so a handler does not produce a different response depending on what the client negotiated. That required handling one wire rule for the caller: HTTP/1.1 encoders emit only the trailer fields named in the response's `Trailer` header, so that header is synthesized whenever the names are known before the head goes out. They are genuinely unknowable for a promise on a streaming body, which is documented rather than papered over.
- **Outbound `fetch` requests carry `TE: trailers`.** A conformant HTTP/1.1 server sends a trailer section only to a client that asked (RFC 9110 §10.1.4), so without it the client half could never see an HTTP/1.1 trailer — including from this runtime's own server. `trailers` is also the only value HTTP/2 permits there (RFC 9113 §8.2.2), so one spelling serves both. The cost is ~17 bytes on every outbound request.

**Consequences:** esrun becomes the only Fetch-API server that can serve native gRPC, and can also be a gRPC client. A trailered response is always chunked, since a `Content-Length` response has nowhere to put a trailer section. **Not solved here:** request trailers in either direction — a handler cannot read what a client sent, and `fetch` cannot send them; gRPC uses neither, so they stay dropped as before. Also unsolved: gRPC's "Trailers-Only" error response is expressible today only because a body-less response already ends the stream on the headers. Verified on the wire rather than through the API that produced it: an HTTP/1.1 trailer section asserted to appear *after* the head rather than among the headers, an HTTP/2 trailing `HEADERS` frame read by polling frames (`bytes_stream` drops them), an empty trailer set producing no frame at all, and two end-to-end tests under the real binary — one reading a raw socket, one doing the full round trip through `fetch`. The `TE` requirement was found by bisecting against a known-good peer: our client read Node's trailers, our server did not answer Node's client, and a raw request with and without the header isolated it. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/`, site `api/http` + `docs/http`, `CHANGELOG`).

### D47 — The WebSocket server's two bounds: HTTP's mechanisms, and a permit that outlives the handshake · *Proposed (2026-08-03)*
**Context:** three servers, one of them undefended. D43 gave `runtime:http` connection timeouts and D45 gave it a connection cap; `runtime:net` inherits the accept-loop protections. The WebSocket server had neither bound, which its own internals page said out loud ("no cap on concurrent connections and no handshake timeout, so a WebSocket server is less defended than the HTTP one. On a public port, put something in front of it."). The exposure is the cheapest kind: `accept_async` reads an HTTP request head and waits for it indefinitely, so a peer that completes the TCP handshake and then says nothing holds a task and a descriptor at a cost to itself of one syscall — and nothing bounded how many such holds one server would take.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**
- **The HTTP server's two mechanisms, unchanged and identically spelled.** `timeouts.handshake` (10s, `null` to disable) and `maxConnections` (unlimited, permit taken before `accept`, held not refused). The provider trait's `serve(host, port)` becomes `serve(WsServeOptions)`, field for field with `HttpServeOptions`. Rejected: a flatter `handshakeTimeout` on the options object, which reads better in isolation but means a guest that wrote `timeouts: { handshake: 5000 }` for its HTTP server has to learn a second spelling for the same question in the same process.
- **The handshake bound stops at the handshake.** An established WebSocket has no idle timeout, no keepalive and no maximum lifetime. Rejected: reusing HTTP's idle-connection reasoning here, which is exactly wrong — an HTTP/1.1 connection idle for 30s has nothing to say and its client will transparently reopen, while a WebSocket idle for a week is doing the thing WebSockets are for. Deciding that silence means a dead peer needs application knowledge; the host already answers ping, which is the tool for it.
- **The permit is released when the connection ends, not when its handshake completes.** This is the one place the HTTP shape could not be copied. An HTTP connection's permit lives in the task serving it and that task *is* the connection; a WebSocket connection is handed to the guest and outlives its handshake, so the permit moves into the **actor** task instead. Releasing it at handshake completion would have made the cap bound the handshake *rate* and nothing else — a cap that a server whose connections are long-lived by design would never reach.
- **Unlimited by default, for D45's reason but with more force.** The descriptor budget is still not something this crate can read. What differs is the consequence of not setting it: an HTTP server's connection count is self-limiting because requests finish, while a WebSocket server's only falls when the application closes something. So the same default carries a stronger recommendation to override it.

**Correction found after the fact:** the cap made an existing weakness into a real leak, and the first implementation shipped with it.
