The accept loop was the only one of the three servers keeping no handle for its task, relying on noticing its channel had closed — a check that sits *after* `accept()` returns. Adding the permit gave the loop a second park point *before* that, and a permit is freed by a connection ending rather than one arriving, so after `close()` the acceptor held the listening socket for as long as the connections lived. The deployment the cap exists for was exactly the one that could never get its port back. The same missing handle also left an `accept()` parked at close time unresolvable, since it holds the receiver checked out and only a dropped sender can end it. Both fixed by aborting the acceptor, which is what `runtime:net`'s `listener_tasks` already existed to do. The lesson worth keeping is narrower than "test more": adding a park point to a loop changes what can wake it, and every existing exit condition has to be re-checked against the new one.

**Consequences:** the WebSocket server is no longer the weak one of the three, and the internals page's advice to put something in front of it becomes a recommendation rather than a requirement. The visible change for existing programs is that a connection which never completes its handshake is now closed after 10s; no compliant client is affected, since a client that opened a connection intends to send the upgrade request immediately. `WsServeOptions` breaks an embedder's `serve` implementation — pre-1.0 and unstable per SPEC §14, but a real break, and the same one D43/D45 made for HTTP. **Not solved here:** a per-IP cap (one peer can still take every slot — it needs a peer identity plus a policy, the same gap D45 left); `wss:` (**amended by D55**: only the HTTP/2 form waits on RFC 8441 — a plain `wss:` server is TLS plus the ordinary HTTP/1.1 upgrade, and it ships by upgrading on the HTTP server); and backpressure a sender can feel, since `send()` is fire-and-forget by design and writing faster than a peer reads accumulates pending host-side sends with nothing bounding the total. Verified by 5 provider tests over real sockets — a connection that never sends a handshake is closed, an established one survives well past the deadline, a disabled timeout is genuinely disabled, a connection over the cap waits and is then *served* rather than refused, and a failed handshake returns its slot (without which the cap would count attempts rather than connections, letting a peer exhaust it while holding nothing) — plus 3 runtime-layer tests pinning the crossing: unset means the provider's default, an explicit `null` means off rather than default, and unusable values are rejected before the port is bound. Each was checked to fail without its fix. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/`, site `api/websocket` + `docs/internals/websockets`, `CHANGELOG`).

---

### D79 — An installed package is not a project root · *Proposed (2026-08-18)* · *amends D25, D24*

**Context:** A field report, and a blocker for anything installed from npm: `esdev node_modules/@acme/cli/src/cli.js` could not import `leftpad`, with `no node_modules/leftpad under the project root <proj>/node_modules/@acme/cli`. The root is detected as the nearest ancestor of the entry holding a `package.json` or a `node_modules` (D25), and an installed package has a `package.json` — so the detection stopped inside the dependency, and the `node_modules` walk, which stops at the root, could never reach the sibling directory the package manager hoists into. Every package manager hoists; the entry point of any installed CLI therefore could not run. No flag helped, because none of them move the loader: `--allow-read=/` widens `runtime:fs` and `--import-policy` only narrows what an already-reachable module may be.

The bug is a category error in the detection rule. `package.json` means "a package is here", and the two questions "which package is this file in" and "where does this project begin" have the same answer only outside `node_modules`.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **A directory inside `node_modules` is never a project root.** Detection skips any candidate with a `node_modules` path component and keeps walking, so an installed program anchors at the tree that installed it. The walk stays bounded without a special case: the parent of the outermost `node_modules` contains one by construction, so it always matches.

- **`--root=<dir>` names the root when detection cannot infer it** — D24's deferred "relax flag", arrived at from the other direction. It sets one thing, not two: the same directory bounds the `node_modules` walk and anchors the filesystem jail, because a project that resolves modules from one tree and reads files from another is two sandboxes with one name. A workspace whose packages are entries and whose dependencies are installed at the top is the case it exists for.

- **The root is checked before the program starts:** it must exist, be a directory, and contain the entry. A root the entry is outside of is a mistake, and left to the loader it would surface as *every* import escaping a root the user typed themselves — one mistake reported a hundred times, in a message about the wrong thing.

- **`esdev`'s watcher asks the loader's rule rather than copying it.** `watch_root` walked to the nearest `package.json` itself, with a comment claiming it matched the loader; it now calls `detect_root` and honours `--root`, so what is watched and what can be imported cannot drift apart.

**Consequences:** an npm-installed program runs under both binaries, resolving hoisted, nested and pnpm-symlinked dependencies alike; the jail moves out with it, from the package to the project, which is a wider default reach for that entry and exactly the tree the program was installed into. `--root` widens both walls at once and only from the command line, so nothing guest code does can move it. The not-found message names the flag, since "the root is above where I looked" is the remaining reason a package that is installed is not found. Verified by the reported reproduction end to end (failing before, passing after), 2 unit tests and 4 CLI tests — hoisted resolution, the jail still holding above the project, `--root` reaching a workspace top, and both up-front `--root` refusals. **Not solved here:** `data:`/`blob:` imports remain rejected, and remote modules remain out (D22) — those are what the loader may load, not where it may look.

---

### D78 — A build that fails changes nothing · *Proposed (2026-08-18)* · *amends D59's "the build owns its output"*

**Context:** `esdev build` wrote straight into `dist`, and a whole-project release build **emptied `dist` first** — the rule from D59 and the `--lib` build before it, and a rule with a real reason: output filenames are content-hashed, so `app-1a2b.js` becoming `app-9f8e.js` leaves the old one in the deployed directory for ever. Put together, those two make a failure destructive twice over, which the react static template demonstrates without any help: with no `node_modules` the browser assets, the `index.html` and the prerender bundle are all written, and then the `then: "run"` step exits non-zero. What is in `dist` at that moment is a site whose pages were never rendered, sitting where the deployment that *did* work used to be. Nothing about the directory says so, and in CI it is what gets uploaded.

The failure is not the missing dependency. It is that a build's output is treated as a place to write rather than as a thing to publish, so every intermediate state of it is visible — including the states that are wrong.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **Every output path is mirrored under one staging directory** beside the project, `.esdev-build-<pid>-<n>`, and the whole build runs there: bundles, copied assets, the document, and any `"then": "run"` step — which writes beside its own bundle and therefore writes into staging without knowing that is what it is doing. Only once every target and every step has succeeded does anything move into place. The staging directory is removed on the way out either way, by `Drop`, so "a failed build changes nothing" holds for every error path rather than for the ones somebody remembered to handle.

- **Staging sits inside the project, not in a temp directory.** The last step is a rename, and a rename is only cheap — only *atomic* — within one filesystem. A cross-device move would be a copy of the whole output, and a copy that fails halfway is the failure this decision exists to prevent. It also keeps module resolution honest: the staged bundle is still under the project root, so a `then: "run"` step resolves `node_modules` exactly as the committed one would.

- **Two ways to land, because a build owns some directories and not others.** A whole-project release build **replaces** each `outdir` — D59's up-front emptying, moved to the end where a failed build cannot benefit from it, and still leaving nothing stale. Everything else is **overlaid**: the staged files are moved into place and whatever else is in the directory is left alone. `--target=` writes one target's output into a directory another target's may share; a single entry's `--out` names one file in a directory that may hold other people's files. `--lib` replaces, since a stale module in a library's output is a module the package publishes.

- **The dev loop does not stage.** `esdev start` rebuilds into a directory a page is being served from *while that page is running*, and the hot updates it computes are written into the same directory for the page to fetch. Staging that swaps the directory out from under the running page: the patch lands where it can no longer be served, and every save becomes a full reload. This is not a guess — it is how the four HMR tests failed when staging was first applied to every build, and what they now hold. The dev loop is the one build that is not producing a deployment, and yesterday's failed dev build is one keystroke from being replaced.

**Consequences:** a failed build leaves the last working deployment byte for byte, and leaves nothing of its own; a failure also says so, because a `dist` that was left alone looks exactly like one that was rebuilt, and because the paths in whatever the failing step printed name a directory that no longer exists. Reported paths are revealed back to their real spellings, so no message names the staging directory. Verified against the report's own case and by hand for a successful react static and fullstack build, a `--target=` subset, `--lib`, a single entry, a broken single entry, a broken `--lib`, and `esdev start` rebuilding and re-serving an edit; 2 integration tests and 5 unit tests hold the rules. **Not solved here:** a build killed with SIGKILL leaves its staging directory behind (the next build in that process removes it, another process's will not); and the commit itself is a sequence of renames rather than one atomic operation, so a machine that dies between two of them leaves a directory half replaced — the same window every deployment tool has, and much shorter than the one this removes.

---

### D77 — Both binaries upgrade themselves, and a `--help` is a map, not a manual · *Proposed (2026-08-18)* · *reverses D64-era "esrun only", amends D59*

**Context:** Two findings, a day apart, with one cause. `esdev test --help` described five ambient globals — `test`, `assert`, `assertEquals`, `assertThrows`, `assertRejects` — that **D71 replaced with `runtime:test` imports a release earlier**. A file written from that help fails on its first line with `ReferenceError: test is not defined`, and nothing caught it: the templates had been updated, the site had been updated, the runtime's own suite had been updated, and the help had not. It was wrong because it was long — 93 lines for `esrun`, 118 for `esdev`, and another 328 across the four `esdev` subcommands, most of it prose that also exists on the site, where a reader can find it and where correcting it does not mean shipping a binary.

The second is `esrun upgrade` with no `esdev upgrade`. The original reasoning (`main.rs`: *"a tool that rewrites its own executable is one more thing that can go wrong on a machine where the fix is re-running the installer"*) was written when `esrun` was the binary an installer placed. `install.sh` has installed **both** since `esdev` shipped — same releases, same asset naming, `--only=` to opt out — so the asymmetry now means the development binary is the one a developer cannot update from the tool itself.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **`esdev upgrade`, and one implementation of it.** `cli_common::upgrade` lists the repository's releases, keeps the tags belonging to the binary that asked, and hands `self_update` this platform's asset. The tag filter is not an optimisation: `/releases/latest` answers with whichever binary was published most recently, so an unfiltered upgrade of either would cheerfully install the other. Only `esrun` reads the pre-0.24 bare `v<version>` tags, because only `esrun` was ever released under one.

- **A `--help` names things; it does not explain them.** What stays is the grammar, the flags, the shape of the command, and a URL. What goes is everything a page holds better: the port-selection rule, the reload channel, the `esdev.json` target keys, the argument for `--dts-bundle`, the scope-matching semantics. `esrun --help` is 62 lines and `esdev --help` 60, and each subcommand's is between 26 and 55.

- **The help is testable, so it is tested.** The claim `esdev test --help` makes is now pinned by a test that writes the file the help shows and runs it. A help text that documents an API is a contract with the same standing as a type definition, and it had been treated as prose.

**Consequences:** a developer can update either binary from the binary, and neither help text can drift as far again without a test failing. The five ambient declarations were deleted from every template's `esdev-env.d.ts` and from the runtime's own test directory, where they were worse than merely stale — they made `tsc` accept a file the runner rejects; `@opentf/esrun-types` has declared `runtime:test` since it shipped. **Not solved here:** nothing pins the *rest* of either help against the code — the flag tables are still prose that a new flag can be left out of; and `esdev upgrade` cannot tell a developer their `esrun` beside it is now a different release, because neither binary reads the other's version.

---

### D76 — A template is a scaffold, not a demo · *Proposed (2026-08-18)* · *amends D63, D64, D72*

**Context:** D64 put templates in the binary and D63 and D72 gave the react one its shape, and both were arguing about the *machinery* — targets, an entry, a permission line, which of three deployables a scaffold should produce. Nobody argued about the content, so it accumulated: a blog with three posts, a list route and a dynamic one to hang loaders off; a task manager with an in-memory store, four CRUD handlers and hand-written validation; an item list with add and remove; a `Result` type and a `retry` with backoff; and a callout box whose reason to exist was demonstrating CSS Modules.

Every line of it was written to *show* something, and all of it is documented on the site — where it can be read by somebody who wants it, and updated without shipping a new binary. In a scaffold it is somebody else's application, and the first thing anybody does with a scaffold is start writing theirs. So the content was a cost paid on every `esdev create`: read it, work out which files are the template and which are the example, delete the example, and hope nothing that stayed depended on what went.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **What a template writes is a project that runs, and one page.** The project's name, one line saying what it was built with and who it comes from, the file to edit, and three links — docs, API, GitHub. The `api` template has no page and answers the same in JSON on `GET /`. That page exists to be deleted, and says so.

- **The look is one look, shared.** `styles/theme.css` and `styles/app.css` are byte for byte the same file in the `react` and `vanilla` templates. Two copies of one page drifting apart is how a starter ends up with two.

- **What is kept is what a project needs on its first day and would otherwise assemble by hand.** The route table, the layout, the error boundary that renders a real 404, one render shared by the server and the static build, Fast Refresh, the `URLPattern` router that tells a 405 from a 404, the security headers, the `SIGTERM` drain, the JSON access log, and a permission line that is already narrow. The line between this and an example is *whether deleting it leaves the project broken* — a CRUD handler is an example, a signal handler is not.

- **One test per template stays.** `esdev test` exits non-zero when it discovers nothing, so a template with no test files scaffolds a project whose `npm test` fails on the first command. Each template keeps the smallest real test of a module it actually ships.

- **The prose is trimmed to what a file needs to be understood.** Every file keeps a header saying what it is; the essays explaining the deleted examples went with them. The two warnings that cost something to rediscover — `refresh.ts` must be imported first, `$RefreshSig$` must be the real function — stay where they are.

**Consequences:** `esdev create` writes a project somebody can start typing into rather than one they have to clear out first, and the templates stop being a second, unversioned copy of the documentation. Verified by scaffolding all four: `vanilla`, `api` and `lib` test and build; both `react` modes test, typecheck against a real install, and build — the static one prerenders its page, and the fullstack one, run under `esrun` with the grant its `esdev.json` names, answers `/` with the page, an unknown path with 404 and `/healthz` with ok; the `api` bundle answers `/` with its JSON, 404s an unknown path and returns a 405 carrying `Allow`. **Not solved here:** component testing, which still needs a bundling step `esdev test` does not have — so what each template tests is a module rather than a component, and the page itself is covered by nothing but the build.

---

### D75 — The dev channel is a WebSocket, because of what it will carry · *Proposed (2026-08-17)* · *supersedes D62's transport choice*

**Context:** D62 chose server-sent events for the reload channel and the argument was sound for what it carried: one direction, one word, and `EventSource` reconnects by specification — which matters when the far end is a build tool the developer restarts all day. A WebSocket would have meant framing on one side and a reconnect loop on the other, to deliver a string.

That reasoning is about the payload, and the payload is changing. Hot module replacement makes this channel carry **a module's source**, and the decision was revisited before the first patch went down it rather than after.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **A WebSocket at `/@esdev/hmr`,** replacing the event stream at `/@esdev/reload`. Two reasons, and neither is about today's message.

  **A hot update is multi-line JavaScript, and SSE is a line protocol.** Every patch would have to be JSON-escaped or split across `data:` lines — a re-encoding on the hot path, for ever, to fit a shape the payload does not have.

  **And SSE runs out of connections.** HTTP/1.1 caps a browser at roughly six per origin, and a stream holds one open for as long as the page is. The seventh tab of your own app stops hot updating, silently, with nothing in the console explaining it — a failure a developer would reasonably spend an afternoon blaming on their own code. HTTP/2 multiplexing would answer it, and browsers do not speak h2c, so on a local dev server it is not available.

- **What it cost is the reconnect loop**, and that is the honest side of the trade: `EventSource` reconnects for free and a dev server restarting is the *ordinary* case rather than a failure, so a client that gave up after the first drop would look broken for the rest of the session. The injected client backs off from 250 ms to a 5 s ceiling. Ten lines, written once, against a per-message encoding and a silent failure at seven tabs.

- **The handshake and the framing cost nothing here.** `--inspect` already speaks WebSocket in its server role, in this binary, on the accept loop `devserver` already borrows `read_head`/`request_path`/`respond` from. The claim in D62 that a WebSocket "would need framing here" was true when it was written and had stopped being true by the time this was decided.

- **The message is typed** — `{"type":"reload"}` rather than the bare word — and the broadcast channel carries an enum with one variant. The transport, the client's dispatch and the channel are already shaped for a message that says *what* changed, so the CSS swap and the module patch are new variants rather than a new protocol.

- **Nothing is expected from the page, and that is not an accident of the current stage.** rolldown's per-client input reads `shipped[C]`, *the server's own record of its deliveries* — so what a patch contains is decided from server-side state and a client has nothing to report. The socket is polled anyway, because that is what sends a pong and notices a close.

**Rejected: keeping SSE and adding a POST endpoint** for the client→server direction if it is ever wanted (`import.meta.hot.send`, an error overlay reporting back to the terminal). Two transports for one conversation, to avoid a handshake the binary already implements.

**Consequences:** `/@esdev/reload` is gone; a plain `GET` of `/@esdev/hmr` answers `426 Upgrade Required` rather than hanging up, since that URL is what somebody reaches for to check whether the dev server is alive. No new dependency — `tokio-tungstenite` was already linked for `--inspect`. Verified by the handshake and a text frame read off a hand-written client (`101 Switching Protocols`, `Sec-WebSocket-Accept`, opcode 1, `{"type":"reload"}` on a rebuild), and by the existing end-to-end test, which now asserts the injected client is a `WebSocket` against `/@esdev/hmr`. **Not solved here:** the injected URL carries the port the build knew, so a page open across an `esdev start` that comes back on a *different* port reconnects to nothing until it is reloaded by hand — true of the event stream before it, and worth fixing when the port stops moving as often.

---

### D74 — `--port` is the port you open, and the grant moves with it · *Proposed (2026-08-17)* · *amends D62, applies D65's rule*

**Context:** D62 gave `esdev start` a port and a rule for it — one you named is a promise and fails if it is taken, one you did not is a convenience and moves out of the way. Two things were wrong with how that landed, and they are the same mistake seen from two sides.

The rule was applied to **esdev's own endpoint** and to nothing else. The application's port never moved: it comes from the template, the template says 8080, and `esdev.json` grants `listen` on exactly that. So the second project open in the second terminal built cleanly, started, and died on a bound address — over a number nobody had chosen. Reported by an external audit of the react starter, and reproduced on the first try.

And `--port` — the name every dev server in the world uses for *the address you type* — was given to the endpoint, which is the one thing here nobody types. The first fix kept that and added `--app-port` beside it, which is backwards: it spends the good name on plumbing and makes the port a developer actually cares about the one with the qualifier.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **`--port` is the port you open**, and which process that is follows from the project. With a `run` target it is the application's; without one esdev is what is being opened, so it is esdev's listener. One flag, one meaning — *the address you type* — and the developer never has to know which process is behind it.
- **esdev's endpoint gets no flag at all.** On a project with a server of its own it carries one message to the page, the build writes its address into the page, and it takes a free port. A flag for it was written and then deleted: the two tests that appeared to need one turned out never to read it, and a second port flag on a dev command is a thing to explain, on the surface a developer meets first. Its "5173 was taken" note is gone with it — a note about a port nobody can pin is noise about plumbing.
- **The same rule for both.** `--port=3000` is a promise. An unnamed application port tries the one the project's `listen` grant names and takes a free one if that is busy, printing what it settled on. A port that was *named* is never reported as having moved, since a named port that moved would be a broken promise — it is an error instead.
- **A port is moved only for a project that has said enough for the move to be safe**, and both halves are grants that were already being written: `"listen": ["8080"]`, one port and no more, so there is a port to move; and `"env": ["PORT"]`, so the server can be told which one it got. Without the first there is nothing to rewrite. Without the second, setting `PORT` would move the grant out from under a server still binding its old number, which is a capability failure rather than a port conflict. **A project shaped any other way is left exactly as it is** — a backend that binds by another name, or several ports, or a host, gets no opinion from esdev at all.
- **The rewritten flag is the same capability with a different number.** `--allow-listen=8080` becomes `--allow-listen=8137`. This is the one place D65's property — that a dev child runs under the deployment's grant, so a capability nobody tested never appears in production — is bent, and it is worth being exact about how: the property is about *which* capabilities, and this changes none of them. It cannot widen one, because the flag it rewrites is the flag it read. The move is printed, so what is running is never on a port only esdev knows.
- **The check is a bind and a release, and the race is accepted.** Something can take the port between the probe and the child's own bind. The alternative is binding it in esdev and passing the socket down, which would make esdev part of *how the application listens* — and the whole of D62 is that it is not.

**Consequences:** two projects can be in development at once, which is an ordinary afternoon and was not previously possible. **`--port` changes meaning for a project with a `run` target** — it was the endpoint's and is now the application's — which is a break to a flag released two days earlier in 0.2.0, taken deliberately: esdev is a tool rather than a contract and its command line moves at its own pace, and the longer the wrong name sits the more places point at it. `"start": { "port": … }` moves with it, and there is no replacement key for what `"port"` used to mean there — the endpoint is not pinnable any more. A project that pins `port` but grants no `env: PORT` now gets an error saying which grant is missing, where before the key silently meant something else. Verified by 6 unit tests over the selection (a free granted port is taken as-is; a held one moves and its grant follows; a named one is never reported as a move; a named one that is held is an error; a project missing either half is untouched; naming a port such a project cannot use says which grant is missing) and by running three dev loops of one project side by side, two unpinned and one on `--port=3000`. **Not solved here:** nothing narrows `--port` to loopback for the *application* — that is the application's `serve({ hostname })`, not esdev's to override.

---

### D73 — `esdev create` asks with a menu · *Proposed (2026-08-17)* · *amends D70, reverses its "no dependency" argument*

**Context:** D70 decided that `create` asks when there is somebody to ask, and drew the questions as a numbered list read off stdin. The file said why there was no dependency: the crates that do this well draw their own UI with raw-mode terminal control, and a scaffolder that runs once does not need a redraw loop. That is true, and it is beside the point. What somebody choosing a template needs is to **see** the choices and move through them; the numbered list makes them count lines and then type a digit, and the redraw loop is the cheap part of paying for that.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **A menu, drawn with ratatui into an inline viewport.** Arrows or `j`/`k` to move, `1`–`9` to jump, Enter to take it, Esc to cancel. The marker on the selected row is a glyph as well as a colour, so it reads without colour and without colour vision.
- **Inline, never the alternate screen.** The menu is drawn where the cursor already is and is replaced *in place* by one line naming the answer. What is left in the scrollback is a transcript of what was asked and what was chosen — which is what somebody re-reading their terminal an hour later is looking for, and exactly what a full-screen TUI takes with it when it hands the terminal back empty. For a command whose entire effect is writing a project to disk, that record is not decoration.
- **Esc cancels rather than defaulting.** At "which template?" nothing has been written, so nothing is undone and the exit status is zero: changing your mind is not an error. At "install the dependencies?" it means no install, because the project is already on disk by then and cancelling the *question* is not cancelling the project.
- **Everything else about D70 is unmoved.** The gate is still a TTY on both stdin and stderr with no `CI` set, every question still has a flag, questions still go to stderr, and a terminal that will not enter raw mode still gets the numbered list — so the worst case is the behaviour this replaces.
- **Colour gets one meaning per colour, and its own module.** Green for something produced, cyan for somewhere to go or look, bold for a line to type, dim for what you skip when skimming. Nothing is red: red belongs to the error block, and a status line reaching for it competes with an actual failure. The rule — a terminal, and no `NO_COLOR` — lives in one place because written inline it becomes a condition somebody forgets once, and the result is a build log with escape codes in it, which is worse than no colour. It is asked **per stream**, so `esdev build > build.log` writes a plain log while the `esdev start` in the next terminal stays coloured.

**Consequences:** `esdev` gains `ratatui` (MIT), confined to it — `esrun` asks nothing and must carry no code that could. `prompt::select` returns `Option<usize>`, since cancel is now distinct from the default. Verified by unit tests over the frame (every choice drawn, the marker on exactly one, the default named) and the colour gate (nothing escapes into a pipe; every code is closed), and by driving the real binary through a pty that answers the cursor-position query. **Not solved here:** no filter-as-you-type, which matters at four templates about as much as it sounds like it does.

---

### D72 — The react template has two shapes, and a scaffold is one of them · *Proposed (2026-08-17)* · *supersedes D63's "three ways to ship it from one build", amends D68*

**Context:** D63 asked whether SSR, SSG and SPA are one template or three, and answered *one*, because the three differ only in when the render happens and three codebases would be three renderers to keep in agreement. That reasoning is still correct and this does not reverse it. What it got wrong is a different question, which D63 did not ask: **how many of them should a single scaffold produce?** It produced all three. Every `esdev create` wrote a server, a browser bundle and a prerendered site, and left the reader to work out which one they were shipping — three deployables in one starter, which is three things to keep working and two the project does not want. The audit that found the port collision found this too, from the other side: it could not tell what the template was *for*.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **Two modes, asked once at create time, and a scaffold is exactly one of them.** `--mode=static` has no server: `npm run build` prerenders every route, `npm run build:spa` builds the shell alone, and **nothing is granted in production because nothing runs there**. `--mode=fullstack` has `src/server.tsx` and the capability line that goes with it, and prerenders nothing. Neither carries the other's files — no server module in a static project, no prerender step in a fullstack one.
- **SSG and SPA are deliberately *one* mode.** This is where D63's reasoning still holds and is now load-bearing: both come out of the same routes, the same components and the same render with no file edited, so which of the two a site wants is a **deployment** decision that moves with the content rather than a scaffold-time one. The switch is which script you run. Which routes are prerendered is `staticPaths()` in `src/paths.ts`, and a route left out of it is rendered in the browser instead — so a mostly-static site with a few client-only routes is this same project with a shorter list, not a different template.
- **A mode is a directory, not a flag threaded through the files.** A template may hold `_mode/<name>/`, and a scaffold is everything outside it plus one mode's files with the prefix stripped; an overlay may add a file or replace a shared one. So the shared half really is shared — one `routes.tsx`, one `render.tsx`, one stylesheet — and a mode is something you can read by listing one directory.
- **The mode is named in every message.** "The react template" is two projects, and a report that does not say which one is a report nobody can check.

**Four things the same audit found, fixed with it because they are the same files.** Every template now ships `@opentf/esrun-types` and names it in `tsconfig.json` — closing D63's "not solved", since the package is published — and `api`, `lib` and `vanilla` ship `typescript` too, so `npm run typecheck` works on a fresh clone instead of depending on a global `tsc`. Those three therefore have a `devDependencies` block where they had none; their claim is unchanged and is now written as what it always meant, that **nothing they ship depends on anything**. `npm run build` minifies, because what a starter's build script produces is what people deploy (`lib` excepted — a published package is minified by whoever bundles it, if at all). And a whole-project `esdev build` now **empties each `outdir` first**: hashed filenames mean the old ones stay for ever, and beside them whatever `esdev start` wrote, which is not hashed and so was never overwritten either — so what shipped was every build the directory had ever seen. `--target=<name>` does not clear, since it may share a directory with output it will not rewrite; the dev loop does not clear, since it writes stable names and the page is being served from there; an `outdir` holding the project is refused rather than emptied.

**One finding was wrong, and is recorded because a corrected claim is worth more than a quiet one.** The audit reported that `HEAD /` and `HEAD /assets/…` returned headers and then hung. They do not, and rebuilding the template without the change confirms it: the server never writes a body for a HEAD. The change stays for the reasons it actually has — an asset response is an open file, and handing it to a server that will not read it keeps the handle alive until the collector gets to it, while a page nobody will receive is rendered for nothing — and the commit that claimed otherwise is corrected in place.

**Consequences:** `esdev create --mode` is new, `--list` names each mode and what it weighs, and the default is `static` — the smaller thing to be handed when nobody expressed a preference. Both modes were built, typechecked, tested and served end to end from a real scaffold. Verified by 5 unit tests over the overlay (each mode's own files and none of the other's, every path written once, no `_mode/` prefix escaping, every mode a whole project, an unknown mode refused) and 2 end-to-end tests through the binary. **Not solved here:** component testing, which still needs a bundling step `esdev test` does not have; and hot module replacement, so a save is still a full page load.

---

### D71 — `runtime:build`, `runtime:watch` and `runtime:test`: a binary may add to the namespace, and `esdev` is the only one that does · *Proposed (2026-08-14)*

**Context:** D59 split the binaries so that development machinery could exist without shipping to production, and D60 gave a project a file describing what it builds. Both assumed the *developer* is the one driving: a subcommand reads the config, the subcommand bundles, the subcommand watches. The next consumer is not a developer at a prompt — it is a **program**: a framework's dev server, which bundles a route on demand, holds the chunk in memory, serves it, and drops exactly the chunks a save invalidated. Every one of those is a decision only the program holding the cache can make, so what it needs from the toolchain is not a subcommand but an API.

Today it gets one by importing rolldown from npm. That is a napi addon, which this runtime does not load and is not going to (D2/D22), so a dev server written that way is a Node program by construction — which is the dependency the framework exists to remove. The bundler is already inside `esdev`; the only thing missing was a door.

Three options were considered:

1. **A subprocess protocol: pipe source in, read a bundle out.** Rejected, and the reason is specific rather than aesthetic. `transform` is one of six hooks real plugins use. `resolveId` + `load` serve **virtual modules** — a specifier that exists on no disk, whose content the plugin invents — and there is nothing to pipe. `this.addWatchFile()` declares dependencies the graph *cannot discover* (a page compiled from frontmatter depends on files it never imports), which is the input to fine-grained invalidation. `this.resolve()` asks the bundler's own resolver mid-hook. Four of the six need the plugin and the bundler in one conversation.
2. **Widen `esdev build` until the dev server is a subcommand.** Rejected: the cache, the websocket to the browser, the compile server and the route table belong to the program, and a subcommand that owned them would be the framework, written in Rust, in this repository.
3. **Two `runtime:` modules, served only by `esdev`.** Chosen.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **The `runtime:` namespace gains a host seam, and it stays deliberately narrow.** `Runtime::register_module(specifier, source)` serves an embedder's module on the same terms as a baked one: no loader, no filesystem, and no capability to import it, because the gate is always the op (D26/D38). Shadowing a built-in is **refused** rather than resolved in some order — the order would be the only thing standing between a program and a redefined `runtime:fs` — and so is a specifier outside the scheme. The namespace being identical everywhere is the property this bends, and it bends only where it cannot hold: a module that exists because the binary in front of the runtime contains a bundler cannot exist in one that does not.
- **`esrun` registers nothing, so the failure is at load.** `import "runtime:build"` under `esrun` throws *unknown built-in module*. Not a stub, not a no-op watcher that never fires — the two failure modes that would have made the split a lie.
- **An op registered after a snapshot restore needs its JS shell.** `Engine::finish_baked_ops` marks the end of the ops the snapshot baked shells for; anything registered later gets one installed. Without it an extension's handler is bound to a `__ops.<name>` that does not exist, and the symptom is a `TypeError` far from the cause.
- **The bundler runs on a thread of its own, and hooks run in the isolate.** Rolldown's graph walk is parallel; a V8 isolate has one thread, and it is the one running the dev server. So the bundler gets a dedicated thread with a multi-threaded runtime, and the hook dispatch is **inverted**: a hook posts a request and waits, and the guest's pump — an ordinary async op — resolves with it, runs the JavaScript and replies. Several are in flight at once, so the parallelism survives the crossing rather than collapsing to a queue of one. The pump is `unref`'d, because only a build *this agent started* can produce a hook call: with the loop otherwise idle there is provably nothing left to answer, and without it a program that bundled once would never exit.
- **The plugin API keeps rollup's verbs, because that is the vocabulary the JavaScript world converged on** — and the framework being ported already has six plugins written to it. **Amended below**: the *contract* is this project's, and the bundler behind it is an implementation.
- **`watchFiles` is returned, not optional.** It is every file the build read plus every file a plugin declared, and it is what lets a consumer drop three chunks out of forty instead of clearing the cache on every save — which is the same as having no cache. It is also why `runtime:watch` ships in the same increment: the two are one feature used from two sides.
- **`runtime:watch` is events, not a restart.** `esdev --watch` answers a change by `SIGTERM`ing the child, which is right for *rerun this script* and cannot serve a server that must stay up through the save. Its watch set is mutable at runtime, because which files a bundle depends on is known only after it is built. Events are debounced per path and a burst is reported as **what it adds up to**: create-then-write is a create, and the remove-then-create every editor performs on save is a modification. Last-one-wins would have reported a brand-new file as "modified", which a consumer deciding whether to add a route reads as "nothing new".

**Where the capability model holds, and where it is stated instead.** Both modules are gated on `FileRead`; `write()` also needs `FileWrite`. `runtime:watch` resolves every path through the run's own `FileSystem` view, so the root jail (D25) and `--allow-read` (D38) bound what can be watched exactly as they bound what can be read — watching a directory tells you which files exist and when they are touched, which is a read in every way that matters. `runtime:build` resolves only its `cwd` that way. What the bundler reads *from* there it reads itself, with the process's authority: a module graph's extent is not knowable before it is walked, and a check that stopped at the first symlinked package would look like a boundary without being one. That is documented on the page rather than implied by the gate.

**Consequences:** `Config` gains `extensions`, a list of host extensions registered on the **main agent only** — a worker gets the standard namespace, because handing background threads a bundler is not a power anyone asked for. `esdev` gains one linked crate it did not have (`rolldown_sourcemap`, already in the graph via rolldown, pinned with it) so that a source map a JavaScript plugin returns as JSON becomes the type rolldown's hook signatures name. **Not solved here:** no cached scan between `generate()` calls (the output options may differ per call, and a bundler carrying stale ones is a build that silently ignores what it was asked for); `renderChunk`, `generateBundle` and the other output-stage hooks are not carried, and a plugin that defines one is run without it rather than refused; and `esdev build` still has its own option translation rather than going through this module. Verified by 4 unit tests over the module seam and 10 end-to-end tests through the real binary — including one that asserts **`esrun` fails to load both modules**, and one that runs the whole shape at once: a server bundling a route on demand, serving it, and dropping only the routes that used a changed file, with an `async` plugin hook running in the same isolate that answers the requests — the moment either passes, development machinery has leaked into production. Documented per D27 (`docs/API.md`, site `api/build`, `api/watch`, `docs/internals/bundler`, `CHANGELOG`).

**Amendment (`runtime:test`, 2026-08-14).** The seam's third module, and the one that fixes something that was already wrong. `esdev test` handed a test file five **globals** — `test`, `assert`, `assertEquals`, `assertThrows`, `assertRejects` — by prepending them to the file's own source, folded onto one physical line so that the file's line 1 stayed line 1, with an epilogue appended to await the pending tests and report. It worked, and it contradicted the sentence this project opens the API reference with: host functionality is exposed as ES modules under the `runtime:` scheme, **never as ambient globals**. A test file was the one place a program was handed names it never asked for.

Two consequences of the old shape were worse than the inconsistency, and neither was visible from inside it. **Only the entry was wrapped**, so a shared `test-helpers.ts` beside the test file — the one place a suite most wants to share code — had no `assertEquals` to call. And there was **nowhere to declare the names**, so a `.ts` test file referenced five undeclared identifiers and `tsc --noEmit` failed on a suite that ran perfectly.

The API moved into `runtime:test` unchanged, and the **score moved into the host**: `test()` reports that a case started and, later, how it ended, and `esdev` prints the tally after the program reaches quiescence and picks the exit code. That is what removes the appended epilogue as well as the prepended harness, so what runs is byte for byte the file on disk — the one-line fold, the "no `//` comments in the harness" rule and the strip-then-wrap ordering all go with it. It also turns a hang into a failure: a test whose promise never settles used to sit at the epilogue's `Promise.all` forever, and is now reported as *"the test never finished"*, because a started case with no outcome is a failing case rather than an absent one. And because a test file is now an ordinary module, `esdev app.test.ts` runs one directly and reports identically.

The tally is a **thread-local**, deliberately: it belongs to the process, is read after the run by code nowhere near the ops, and threading a handle out through `Config` and the argument parser would be plumbing for a value that can only have one instance. Per-thread rather than global is what keeps it honest — extensions are registered on the main agent only, so a worker cannot import `runtime:test` and can never have a second tally.

**Breaking**, and knowingly: every test file must now import what it uses. The 13 in this repository (4 in the serialization suite, 9 across the `esdev create` templates) were updated with it.

**Amendment (the plugin contract is ours, 2026-08-14).** The first version of this exposed **rolldown's** plugin trait to the guest: rollup's hook names, rollup's `this`, rolldown's return shapes, handed straight through. The argument for it was that the hooks are not a design anyone here chose — they are the extension points the bundler actually calls, so an owned API would be a rename with a maintenance bill. That argument is right about the *verbs* and wrong about everything else, and the maintainer's objection was the correct one: rolldown is a build tool this project uses, not a dependency its published contract may be defined by.

Two things make it more than a preference. The `runtime:` namespace is **versioned** (SPEC §14): a hook renamed in a bundler's patch release would be a breaking change in a language runtime's standard library, arriving from a dependency bump. And the passthrough produced a real bug, found while writing this amendment: `esdev build` installs this project's CSS Modules pass and the guest path installed only the guest's plugins, so the same project produced a scoped `styles.button` from the subcommand and an unscoped one from the module — markup that did not match its own stylesheet, depending on which path built it. That is what follows from our own passes and the guest's plugins being two unrelated concepts glued to a third party's trait.

So there is a contract layer, and rolldown is named in exactly one file behind it. The contract states **what a backend must provide** — seven capabilities, listed in the module's own documentation — which is what makes "the bundler is replaceable" checkable rather than hoped for: rolldown has all seven, esbuild has four, and the cost of that swap is therefore legible before it is paid rather than discovered during it.

**The verbs stay rollup's**, and that is deliberate rather than lazy. The JavaScript world's plugin vocabulary *is* rollup's — Rollup, Vite, Rolldown and unplugin have all converged on `resolve`/`load`/`transform` — so renaming them would be adopting esbuild's dialect rather than inventing one, at a cost in adoption and no gain. Ownership lives in the spec and the semantics. Four of those are ours:

- **A filter is declarative**, and matched on the host's side before the call is posted. Rollup has none and does not need one: in-process, a hook that returns `null` is a function call. Here it is a round trip into a V8 isolate, so an unfiltered `transform` is one crossing *per module in the graph* — four hundred of them on a middling app to reach a plugin that wanted one `.mdx` file. This is the one primitive that matters more to this architecture than to anything it could have been copied from. A `RegExp` crosses as its source and flags; JavaScript's regular expressions are a larger language than the matcher's, and a pattern that will not compile **matches everything** rather than failing, because a plugin that mysteriously never runs is the expensive way to be wrong.
- **Dependencies are returned, not declared by a side effect.** `this.addWatchFile()` is a call you can forget, and forgetting it produces a build that serves stale output — the failure hardest to notice and worst to debug, and the exact thing the whole `watchFiles` design exists for. `dependsOn` is a field of the value a hook returns.
- **A virtual module says `virtual: true`.** Rollup marks "no file behind this id" with a leading NUL byte, a convention every descendant inherited and no author enjoys. It is the backend's notation, applied and stripped in the adapter, and a `load` filter still matches the id the plugin named.
- **The context is the last argument, not `this`.** Rollup's context-as-`this` is silently lost by an arrow function, and the failure surfaces a long way from the arrow that caused it. Every hook is *data first, context last*, with nothing positional in between — `isEntry` rides on the context rather than shifting `resolve`'s signature.

And a fifth that is ours by construction rather than by design: **a plugin is guest code under the capability model.** A plugin that reads a file needs `FileRead`, like any other program on this runtime. No other bundler's plugin API can state what a plugin is *allowed* to do, because none of them has a capability model to state it in.

**No compatibility fallback**, at the maintainer's instruction and rightly: a hook is an object carrying a `handler`, and rollup's bare-function shorthand is refused rather than accepted alongside. Accepting both would have made the filter, the order and the context argument optional extras on somebody else's design — which is the passthrough again, wearing a coat. Every rejection names what to write instead, and a misspelled hook names the one it was nearly (D60's rule, applied here). Ordering (`pre`/`post`) is surfaced at the same time; rolldown always supported it and the passthrough never exposed it.

**Amendment (one set of resolution defaults, 2026-08-15).** Two builds of one project disagreed about what it resolves to. `esdev build` asserts the `worker` condition (D59) and the `module`/`main` fields, for the reasons D60's own bug report gives; `runtime:build` asserted neither unless the caller named them, because it translated its options into the bundler's shape in a second place. So a framework's dev server, bundling the same entry the subcommand bundles, got a package's `node:` build where the subcommand got its Web build — and a package too old to have an `exports` map did not resolve at all, surviving into the output as a **bare import of something that is not there**. Nothing failed at build time in either case, which is the whole problem: this is the same silent wrong-build failure D60 recorded, reintroduced by the second implementation rather than by a wrong decision.

The fix is not to copy the constants across. `crates/dev-cli/src/resolve.rs` holds the defaults and a `Target` of our own — deliberately not the bundler's platform enum, so it survives changing what is underneath — and all three build paths (the subcommand, the client bundle it emits for a browser, and `runtime:build`) read them from it. A caller's `conditionNames` **append** to what the target asserts, matching `--conditions` (D59), so naming one cannot silently cost `worker`; `mainFields` **replace**, because there is one ordered list and a caller who writes one means it.

This is the second divergence of this class found between the two paths — the first was `runtime:build` not installing the CSS Modules pass the subcommand installs. Both were found by *looking*, which is not a way of finding bugs; it is a way of finding the ones you happen to look for. So the fix went one step further than the defaults.

**There is now one translation.** `crates/dev-cli/src/bundler.rs` owns the option types every build in this binary describes itself with — `Options`, `OutputOptions`, `External` — and is the only place in the crate that constructs the bundler's `BundlerOptions`. The `build` subcommand, the client bundle it emits for a browser, and `runtime:build` all fill in the same struct and hand it to the same `translate()`. A second translation cannot drift from the first if there is no second translation, and the property is now checkable by grep rather than by reading two functions side by side.

Two things fell out of doing it. `--lib` became a **target** (`Target::Library`) rather than a flag threaded through the option-building, which puts the library rules — assert no condition, define nothing, preserve modules, externalise everything — in the same table as the other targets instead of as `if lib` branches scattered through one long struct literal; a library build is also neutral now, where before it took the browser platform's aliasing if its target said `browser`, which would have baked the consumer's environment into a published package. And the guest's `external` predicate binds to its bridge where the options are read rather than where they are translated, which is what let the shared type stay free of anything guest-shaped.

**Amendment (the contract has two implementations, 2026-08-15).** The claim this project made when it took the plugin API off the bundler was that our own passes and a guest's plugin are the same kind of thing. It was not true. `CssModules` — the pass that scopes a `.module.css` and is the reason a component's `styles.button` matches the stylesheet the browser fetched — was written against **rolldown's** trait, while the contract it was supposed to share had exactly one implementation: the guest. A contract with one implementation always fits, which is the same as saying it has not been tested.

It is now a `contract::Pass`, and everything that follows from that is the point:

* **`contract::Pass` and `contract::Context` are traits, not a parsed declaration.** The contract used to describe only what a guest *declares*; it now describes what a pass *is* and what a hook may ask of a build while it runs. A guest plugin answers a hook by posting a message to another thread and waiting; the CSS pass answers by returning. Nothing downstream can tell which it has.
* **One adapter.** `crate::adapter` takes any `Pass` and hands back something the bundler will call — filters, ordering, the NUL-prefix notation for virtual ids, declared dependencies, and the three flavours of plugin context, all in one place. Swapping the bundler is now one file for *both* kinds of pass, where before it was one file plus every pass we had written ourselves.
* **The contract moved to the crate root.** It sat under `guest/build/` when only the guest implemented it. It is not guest-only, and a path is a claim.
* **The CSS pass declares a filter instead of checking an id.** `if !args.id.ends_with(".css") { return }` became a declared `Filter`, which is the same statement in the place the machinery can read it.

**A bug this found and fixed.** A hook returning the files it depends on is a contract feature the bundler's trait has no equivalent for — you call `this.addWatchFile()` or you forget. The CSS pass had no way to say it, so it never did: an `@import`ed stylesheet and a `composes … from` target were **invisible to every consumer of `watchFiles`**. Nothing imports either — the reference is inside the CSS, and only our own bundler follows it — so a `--watch` save to one of them rebuilt nothing and the page kept the rules it had. `css::bundle::Bundled` now reports which files it read, the `composes` recursion accumulates them across the chain, and the pass returns them as `dependsOn`. Verified end to end through `runtime:build`'s `watchFiles`, and the test fails without it.

---

### D70 — `esdev create` asks, when there is somebody to ask · *Proposed (2026-08-14)* · *amends D64's "no prompts, no install"*

**Context:** D64 gave two reasons for a scaffolder that writes files and stops, and they are not equally durable.

*"No prompts because every other command here is a flag grammar that works in a script, and a scaffolder that stops to ask cannot be one of them."* — correct, and about a **script**. It is an argument for not prompting where nobody can answer, which is not the same as an argument for never prompting.

*"No install because there is no lockfile yet to say which package manager this project uses, and guessing wrong leaves a `package-lock.json` in a bun project."* — also correct, and an argument against **guessing**. Asking is the one thing that resolves it at the root, because the person running the command is exactly who knows the answer.

There was also a practical reason not to bother: `create` had one template, so an interactive picker would have had a single option to present. D70 follows the three templates that changed that.

**Decision (maintainer, 2026-08-14):** ask on a terminal, and nowhere else.

- **The gate is strict**: stdin *and* stderr must both be a TTY, and `CI` must be unset. A prompt in a script is a script that hangs, which is the failure this is written to avoid. Two integration tests run `create` with stdin closed and assert that nothing was asked and nothing installed.
- **Every question has a flag.** `--template=api --install=bun` is the same run with nothing to type, `--no-install` and `-y`/`--yes` are the explicit refusals. The interactive path is a convenience over the scriptable one and never the only way to an answer.
- **Unattended behaviour is unchanged.** No prompt, no install, and the same report — so D64's rule still holds in exactly the case it was written for.
- **Only installed package managers are offered.** Detected by running `--version` rather than walking `PATH`, since a name on `PATH` can still be a broken shim, and a shim that fails here fails before anything has been installed.
- **The prompt is hand-written**, on `std::io::IsTerminal`, which the tree already uses. The crates that do this well draw their own UI in raw mode; a command that runs once needs a question somebody can answer and a transcript they can read afterwards, not a redraw loop.

**This is still not a package installer.** It runs *theirs* — the command they would have typed — and everything about resolution, the registry, the lockfile and the network belongs to it.

**Consequences:** `CreateConfig` carries `Option`s where it carried values, because "not said" and "said" now differ: unset means ask-or-default, set means an answer. The report is printed *before* the install rather than returned at the end, so the transcript reads in the order things happened rather than putting the installer's output above the line announcing the project it installed into. Next steps are per-template — a library has nothing to `run dev`. Verified over a real PTY: choosing by number and by name, the default on Enter, `skip` leaving no `node_modules`, and an unknown manager named as such before anything runs.

---

### D69 — CSS Modules: a stylesheet the JavaScript imports · *Proposed (2026-08-14)* · *builds on D67, closes its last "not done"*

**Context:** D67 listed CSS Modules as deliberately out of scope, on the grounds that it is "a bundler change rather than a CSS one". That was the right description and the wrong conclusion. The maintainer's answer (2026-08-14) was that it is the highest-priority remaining gap for a framework-grade React template — which is correct, because CSS has exactly one global namespace and a component tree does not. Every real React project reaches this problem on its second component, and the alternatives are a naming convention nobody enforces or a runtime CSS-in-JS library, which is a dependency and a per-render cost.

**Decision (maintainer, 2026-08-14):** implement it, in two halves that meet at a name.

`crates/dev-cli/src/css/modules.rs` is the **CSS half**: an AST pass that renames class selectors, id selectors and `@keyframes` — *and* the `animation` / `animation-name` values that refer to them, because a renamed `@keyframes` with an un-renamed reference is an animation that silently stops running. It reports the mapping. `:global(…)` is the opt-out and is **unwrapped**, not merely skipped: `:global` is a convention of this build, and a browser handed it matches nothing.

`crates/dev-cli/src/cssmodules.rs` is the **bundler half**: a rolldown plugin on the `transform` hook. A `.module.css` module's source is replaced with a frozen object literal, so the graph sees an ordinary module and the component gets `styles.button` with no runtime. The scoped CSS has nowhere to go in a JavaScript bundle, so it is collected and `html.rs` writes it as one hashed stylesheet with a `<link>` in the head.

**The scoped name is derived from the file's path**, not its contents and not a counter. Three things follow, and the first is the one that makes SSR work at all: the **server build and the browser build arrive at the same name independently**, with no shared state and no ordering between them, so the markup the server renders matches the stylesheet the browser fetched. Two machines building one commit agree. And editing a component does not rename its classes, so a rebuild does not invalidate every reference to a name.

**Linked, not injected.** The common alternative is emitting a `<style>` from JavaScript at runtime. That costs a flash of unstyled content on first paint, puts styling behind script execution, and requires `style-src 'unsafe-inline'` — which D68's template deliberately does not grant. A `<link>` is fetched in parallel with the bundle and blocks rendering exactly as a stylesheet should.

**`composes` is supported in all three forms** — `composes: a b` (this file), `composes: a from "./x.module.css"` (another module), `composes: a from global` (a name nothing scopes). The mapping's value becomes a list of class names and the element carries all of them; the declaration is removed, since a browser handed `composes:` skips it as an unknown property and would look like it worked.

It is **transitive**, and that was a bug before it was a feature: the first implementation gave `.big` only `.button` when `.button` itself composed `.rounded`, so a `.big` element had none of `.rounded`'s rules. A class only styles an element that actually carries it, so the chain has to be followed all the way down. Cycles are refused rather than followed. A module named only by a `composes` has its rules emitted too — without that, composition hands out a class name that styles nothing — and `Collected` dedupes by path, since a module can be both imported and composed and its rules must appear once.

**A plain `import "./x.css"`** — anything not `.module.css` — becomes an empty module and its CSS is emitted unscoped. This is not a convenience: a third-party stylesheet *cannot* be a CSS Module, because the library's own JavaScript emits its class names as hardcoded strings, so scoping them would rename half of a contract the library has with itself. The alternative for a user is copying the file out of `node_modules` and `<link>`ing it, which goes stale on the next upgrade with nothing to report it. The module is empty rather than absent because the import is what put the stylesheet in the build, and an edge with no exports is what stops tree-shaking deciding it was unused.

**Consequences:** the plugin runs in **both** builds — the browser one, which writes the CSS, and the server one, which needs the identical mapping and discards it. `rolldown_common` and `anyhow` become named dependencies; both were already in the graph via `rolldown`, so neither adds a crate, and a plugin cannot be written against re-exports that do not exist. The React template gains `src/app/Callout.module.css` and `Callout.tsx` as the worked example, and `*.module.css` gets a `declare module` in `esdev-env.d.ts` — typed as `Readonly<Record<string, string>>` rather than a per-file union, since generating that would mean running the CSS pipeline from the type checker.

Verified by 22 unit tests over the scoping pass, 4 over the generated module (including that a class name cannot break out of the JavaScript string literal it lands in), and an end-to-end test asserting the property the unit tests cannot reach: that two files declaring the same `.button` produce two names, that both appear in the CSS, and that the *bundle* uses those exact strings — the failure mode otherwise being markup naming a class the stylesheet never declared. In a browser: two components' `.button` resolving to different colours; and in the template, a scoped `<aside>` with `var(--accent)` resolving from the global theme, its nested `code` rule applying, and hydration clean.

**Not solved here:** per-file typed class names, which would mean running the CSS pipeline from the type checker.

---

### D68 — The React template is a real app, not a demonstration · *Proposed (2026-08-14)* · *supersedes D64's template, amends its "not solved" list*

**Context:** D64 shipped a template that worked and that nobody would start a project from. Its route table was three fields and a `match()` that compared strings for equality, so there were no dynamic segments and no client-side navigation; every route shared one flat `RouteData = { title, body }`, which is the shape that stops being usable on the second real page. Reviewing it against what it claimed turned up four defects, each silent:

`src/server.tsx` read `globalThis.process?.env?.PORT` — **there is no `process` global on this runtime**, so the value was `undefined` forever and the file taught a Node idiom in place of `runtime:process`. A 404 returned a bare `<h1>404</h1>` *with the client bundle still attached*, so `entry.client.tsx` threw `nothing to render at …` into the console of every 404. `render.tsx` awaited `renderToReadableStream` before constructing its stream and then claimed in a comment that the head was on the wire first, which it was not. The README pointed at `src/app/routes.test.ts`, a file that did not exist, and told the reader to run `<pm> install` — a placeholder nothing substitutes, because `{{name}}` is the only one there is.

**Decision (maintainer, 2026-08-14):** rebuild it on **react-router 8 in data mode**, and hold it to what a service actually deploys.

- **One route table, three consumers.** `createStaticHandler().query()` on the server, `createBrowserRouter()` in the browser, and the same table walked by the prerender step. Dynamic segments, nested layouts, per-route loaders and per-route `ErrorBoundary` come with it, and `context.statusCode` makes a thrown `Response` a **real** 404 rather than a soft one that returns 200.
- **`serialize.ts` is deleted.** `<StaticRouterProvider>` emits the hydration script itself, correctly escaped, and takes a `nonce`. Hand-rolling that escaping was the template's most security-sensitive code and it is now the library's.
- **The server does what a deployed one has to.** A CSP with a per-response nonce (the one inline script is admitted by nonce, never `'unsafe-inline'`), `nosniff`, `referrer-policy`, `permissions-policy`; `SIGTERM` closes the listener and drains before exiting; immutable caching keyed on `NODE_ENV` rather than guessed from a filename; a `/healthz` that touches neither the router nor the data source; one JSON log line per request; 405 on a method the app does not answer.
- **`handle.meta` gives each route its own `<title>`**, derived from its own loader data, spliced into a `<!--head-->…<!--/head-->` region that a single-page build keeps as written.

**What testing the template can and cannot reach.** `esdev test` runs each file unbundled and React ships CommonJS, so a test that imports a component is still refused — D64's finding, unchanged. The response is to factor the parts worth testing so they have no imports: `src/http/` holds the content-type table, the asset-name check that refuses a traversal rather than sanitising one, the HTML escaping, and the CSP. Fourteen tests over those, all reachable. Component testing needs a bundling step in `esdev test` and is **not** solved here.

**Consequences:** the template gains one dependency (`react-router`) and a `src/esdev-env.d.ts` — because `esdev test`'s injected globals and the `process.env.NODE_ENV` that `esdev build` defines are the *tooling's*, and `esdev --install-types` installs the *runtime's*; a project needs both and only one had declarations. `esdev.json` grants `env` and `signals`, both found by running the thing: `onSignal` threw `capability denied: Signals` on first start, and after the drain the process sat idle until killed, because a registered handler is itself something the runtime keeps alive — hence an explicit `exit(0)`. Verified by building and driving it: correct statuses across five routes (including a loader-thrown 404 that first returned 500, because `handle.meta` was called for a route whose data never arrived), the nonce reaching the hydration script, immutable headers on a hashed asset, four spellings of path traversal refused, `SIGTERM` draining and exiting, and a static build of five pages plus a `404.html`.

**Then verified in a browser, which is the only place the rest of it is true or false.** Headless Chrome over CDP, watching the console across four routes and a client-side navigation. It found three things nothing else could. Hydration is clean — no mismatch on any route, which was the open question, since `<StaticRouterProvider>` renders its hydration `<script>` *inside* the container being hydrated and gets away with it only because the element carries `suppressHydrationWarning`. `document.title` did not follow a client-side navigation at all: the server writes the head and a client-side navigation never touches it, so the tab kept the name of whichever page the visitor first landed on. And once a `useMatches()`-driven title fixed that, every data-bearing title silently fell back to the app name — because in react-router 8 the field is **`match.loaderData`**, not `match.data`. `tsc` would have caught that; it did not, because the typecheck had been run before the hook was written. It is caught now.

**Found by running it, after the fact.** Three things no test caught and a browser did. The Content-Security-Policy blocked `esdev start`'s **own live-reload client** — inline script under a nonce policy, then its endpoint under `connect-src 'self'` — so the page never updated on a save and looked entirely healthy doing it; a development build now allows inline script (dropping the nonce, since a policy carrying one ignores `'unsafe-inline'` outright) and admits loopback on any port. The error page rendered with no shell and no masthead, because an `ErrorBoundary` replaces its own route's element and this one is on the layout. And the post bodies contained markdown backticks that rendered literally, there being no markdown renderer. The first of those also moved the dev/production flag out of `src/http/` and into a parameter: those modules have no imports so `esdev test` can run them unbundled, and reaching for `process.env.NODE_ENV` inside one threw `process is not defined` the moment a test touched it.

**Not solved here:** component tests; a client-side router was D64's stated non-goal and is now the opposite, so that line of D64 is superseded rather than amended.

---

### D67 — A stylesheet is an entry, and the CSS pipeline is ours · *Proposed (2026-08-14)* · *closes a gap left by D61*

**Context:** D61 made an `index.html` a build target and *copied* what it referenced — and said in its own "not solved" list that a stylesheet was copied rather than bundled, so `@import` and `url()` inside one were not followed. `html.rs` repeated the point, because the failure is silent in the worst way: the build succeeds, the file lands in `assets/`, and the browser then resolves the stylesheet's relative URLs against *where it ended up* rather than where it was written. A three-file stylesheet arrives with two thirds missing, and a `url(./bg.png)` arrives as a 404.

**The first attempt was lightningcss, and it was withdrawn on licensing.** It works, and it is **MPL-2.0** — as is the `cssparser` subtree it is built on, seven crates in all. MPL's copyleft is per-file and does not reach an Apache-2.0 work that links it, `esrun` never contained any of it, and Tailwind v4 and Parcel already put it in the CI of most commercial frontend teams. It was, on the merits, fine. It was withdrawn anyway (maintainer, 2026-08-14: "we don't want to be in licensing hell at any layer"), and the reason is not legal but practical: `deny.toml` opens by saying copyleft fails the gate, so this would have been a standing seven-crate exception to re-explain to every downstream commercial user whose scanner flags `esdev`. A build tool is not worth that conversation.

Apache-2.0 alternatives were measured rather than assumed. **swc's CSS crates** are Apache-2.0, actively released, and were verified by spike to parse nested CSS and minify correctly, with **zero copyleft across all 121 transitive crates**. **Biome's** are MIT but were last published in December 2024. Both were rejected on scope rather than licence: they buy syntax lowering, prefixing and value minification, all of which are deliberately not wanted here.

**A first implementation spliced byte spans and never parsed.** It worked, it was a few hundred lines, and it was rejected by the maintainer (2026-08-14: "minimal does not mean without proper foundation") — correctly. A splicer is safe and cannot grow: every future pass that has to *move* a rule, wrap one, or read a value has no structure to do it with, and would have started by writing the parser anyway.

**Decision (maintainer, 2026-08-14):** implement [CSS Syntax Level 3][css3] properly — tokenizer, syntax tree, parser, printer — and put the two passes needed today on top of it.

The insight that keeps this small is the **spec's own two-layer design**. CSS has a *generic* grammar every rule obeys — a prelude, then a block of declarations or nested rules — and a *per-property* grammar that says what `grid-template-areas` accepts. The generic layer is closed and already complete; the per-property layer is unbounded and grows with every new specification. So the tree has no `Selector` type and no `MediaQuery` type: a prelude is a list of component values, which is what a selector *is* until something chooses to interpret it. That is what lets the tree hold `@property`, `@container`, `unicode-range: U+4??` and whatever ships next year without knowing anything about them.

**Lossless by construction.** Every token carries its verbatim text and every token is kept — whitespace, comments, unclosed blocks, bad strings — so `print(parse(x)) == x` for *any* input, valid CSS or not, and printing is concatenation. This is the property that makes a tree as safe as the splicer it replaced: the frightening failure mode in CSS tooling is a printer meeting a construct it cannot represent and emitting something else instead, silently, in a build. Here a pass that does not touch something cannot change it. The parser has no `Result` for the same reason a browser has no parse error that stops rendering: malformed input is *represented* (`Block { closed: false }`, `Item::Dangling`), never rejected.

Five modules along the axis a CSS pipeline grows: `token` (§4, the spec's full token set), `ast`, `parse` (§5), `print` (lossless or minified), `bundle` (the `@import` and `url()` passes). A value minifier, a syntax lowerer, a prefixer or CSS modules is a new module beside `bundle` over the same tree.

**Deliberately out of scope:** syntax lowering and vendor prefixing, since nesting and `color-mix()` are supported by every browser in the target range and lowering them today produces a larger file and changes nothing; value-level minification, which needs the per-property layer; and CSS modules, which is a bundler change rather than a CSS one.

**Consequences:** `esdev` gains a CSS pipeline and **no dependency at all**, and `deny.toml` returns to `exceptions = []`. `scripts/copyleft-confined.sh` is kept even though nothing now needs confining — the gate is cheap and the next copyleft crate should have to argue for itself. `xxhash-rust` (BSL-1.0 — the Boost licence, not the Business Source one) stays in the allow list as the plainly permissive licence it is; D61 had noted that failure as pre-existing and left it.

Verified by 45 tests. Six were failures in the first draft that the round-trip corpus caught: an unclosed `{` printing a brace the parser invented; a blockless at-rule printing a `;` that was never there; an unclosed `[` swallowing a rule and *discarding* it (which is what `Item::Dangling` now exists for); `@media` losing the space before its prelude, because the printer tracked no token across a call boundary; `and (min-width:0)` minifying to `and(…)`, a function where there had been a keyword and a block; and `\49 mport` tokenizing as `\49` because the scanner stopped at the whitespace that is *part of* a hex escape — which is exactly how an obfuscated `@\49 mport` would slip past a check matching only the plain spelling. That last one is the argument for implementing §4 rather than approximating it.

The whitespace rules ended up structural, which is what the tree buys over a token stream: in a **selector** whitespace is the descendant combinator, so `main a`, `main :hover` and `& a` all tokenize identically closed up and all mean something else — every space is kept. In a **value** only token-merging matters. Inside `calc`, `min`, `max` and `clamp` the spaces around `+` and `-` are grammar. A parenthesised group in an at-rule prelude is a media feature and prints as a value, while one in a selector is an attribute selector and keeps its spaces.

Round-tripping is asserted over a corpus of exotic at-rules, over the template's own stylesheets, and **exhaustively over every string up to four characters** on the alphabet that drives the tokenizer — roughly 54,000 inputs, which reaches the interleavings nobody thinks to write down.
