End to end, the template's stylesheet was built both ways and *measured in a browser*: one sheet, 21 rules, `#fbfbfd` resolved from the `@import`ed theme file, and the nested `.card h3` and `&[aria-current="page"]` rules applying — with **identical computed styles from the minified and unminified builds**, which is the only evidence that the printer reconstructs valid CSS rather than plausible text. Minification takes it from 4867 to 2863 bytes.

[css3]: https://www.w3.org/TR/css-syntax-3/

---

### D66 — The goal is a server runtime; the embedding API is not offered · *Proposed (2026-08-14)* · *narrows the scope set in D1–D11*

**Context:** this repo opened as "Layer A" — an embeddable runtime built so that a future actor-model VM ("Layer B", a separate repo) could embed it by supplying its scheduler as the I/O provider. That framing shaped the architecture and it earned its keep: the provider seam, the driven loop that owns no thread, the engine abstraction that names no V8 type. It also shaped the *story*, and the story has stopped matching the work.

Three things moved. **The binary became the product.** D31 and D42–D47 put an HTTP/2 and WebSocket server in it, D56 a database module, D58 UDP, D59 a second binary for the development toolchain, D60–D64 a build system and a scaffolder, D65 a deny-by-default deploy line. None of that is Layer A work; all of it is what a service needs. **The embedding API never shipped.** No crate here is published to crates.io — not `es-runtime`, not `es-runtime-cli`. The site's `/docs/embed` page said "the Rust embedding API is still being shaped" and had said so for months, which is a promise with no delivery date attached to it. **Nobody has asked.** There is no issue, no user, and no requirement to design against.

Meanwhile the two-shapes story cost something real. Every doc had to explain which shape it was talking about; the security page opened by splitting itself in two; the home page spent half its call-to-action on a product that does not exist. A reader arriving to evaluate a server runtime had to work out which half applied to them.

**Decision (maintainer, 2026-08-14):** Narrow the goal to a **secure server runtime**, and stop presenting an embedding API.

- **One product, two binaries.** `esrun` runs a service; `esdev` is the development toolchain (D59) and is not a deployment target. That is the whole surface.
- **The embedding API is not offered** — not deprecated, not deferred with a date, simply not on offer. Nothing is withdrawn: no crate was ever published, so there is no consumer to break. If somebody asks, they will arrive with a use case, and an API designed against a real requirement beats one designed against an imagined embedder. Until then, shipping it would fix its shape before its requirements are known.
- **The actor-model VM is a separate project with different goals**, and is no longer described here as a layer of this one. The non-goal it implies — no process model, scheduler, preemption, mailboxes or supervisors — stands unchanged; only its framing does, from "later, in Layer B" to "not this project".
- **The architecture does not change.** The provider seam, the driven loop, the engine abstraction and the capability model all stay exactly as they are. They were justified on their own merits — testability, determinism, a security boundary in Rust rather than in JavaScript — and those hold whether or not anyone embeds this. What changes is what the documentation claims is *on offer*, not what the code does.

**Deliberately left open — the status of the library crates.** Whether `es-runtime` and its siblings become "internal to esrun, no stability promise", and what that does to SPEC §14's definition of the public API and to the provider seam's framing in ARCHITECTURE, is **not settled here**. The maintainer asked to take it separately (2026-08-14). Until it is, the crates keep their current documentation and the workspace is unchanged.

**Consequences:** `/docs/embed` is deleted along with the home page's "Embeddable Engine" call-to-action; the docs landing page, the security model, the API overview, the comparison table and the scope page each present one product; "Layer A" / "Layer B" is gone from `README.md`, `SECURITY.md`, `ARCHITECTURE.md`, `SPEC.md`, `SECURITY-REVIEW.md`, `Cargo.toml` and five crate rustdocs. The comparison table drops its "Embeddable as a library" row — a row this project no longer competes on. **Not rewritten:** the locked entries above, which record what was decided when it was decided; D1's "critical for Layer B", D4's scheduler seam and the rest stay as written, because an ADR that edits its own history is not a record. Read them as the reasoning that produced the architecture, which it did.

---

### D65 — `esrun` is deny-by-default; `esdev` is not · *Proposed (2026-08-14)* · *supersedes D38's default, amends D38's rules*

**Context:** D38 kept `esrun` permissive by default and made restriction opt-in, and it argued the case honestly: the binary runs a local script the user named, and flipping the default would have been a breaking change to every existing command line. Three things have since made that the wrong answer.

The first is that `esrun` stopped being a script runner. D42–D47, D55–D58 and D62–D64 turned it into the binary a **service** is deployed with, and a service's grant is not a preference — it is the thing an auditor reads. A default of "everything" means the safe configuration is the one you have to remember, and the unsafe one is what you get by typing nothing.

The second is that D64 wrote the failure down while solving a different problem: *"a developer assembling this by hand reaches a demo fastest by running with everything granted, and the narrow line is then a thing to add later, which is a thing never added."* D64's answer was a scaffolder that starts narrow. That is the right fix for a project created by `esdev create`, and no fix at all for a project that was not.

The third is that D59's split removed the reason to compromise. When `esrun` was the only binary, its default had to serve both a developer editing a file and a deployment serving traffic, and permissive was the only default that served the first. There are two binaries now, and each can have the default its job wants.

**Decision (maintainer, 2026-08-14):** Flip `esrun` to **deny-by-default**. `esrun app.js` grants nothing host-facing: it computes, and reaches nothing. `esdev` keeps the permissive default and today's rules, unchanged.

- **The baseline is the only difference between the binaries.** `Permissions::new(Baseline::Nothing | Baseline::Everything)` — one enum, threaded from each `main`. The vocabulary, the scope grammar, the validation and the error text are one implementation in `cli-common`, as D59 requires. Enforcement is untouched: this changes what a command line with no permission flag *starts from*, and nothing about what a capability check does.
- **The rules are the same rules, mirrored — not a second rule set.** A command line is in one of two **modes**: restrictive (start from nothing, `--allow-<name>` adds) or permissive (start from everything, `--deny-<name>` subtracts). `--deny-all` and `--allow-all` name a mode outright; absent both, the binary's baseline names it. Then:
  1. `--allow-all` and `--deny-all` are mutually exclusive.
  2. A flag may only move in its mode's direction; the other one is an **error** naming the `--all` flag that would give it something to move.
  3. Each mode therefore has exactly one direction, from either baseline.

  This is D38's three rules with the baseline lifted out of them. The property D38 was written to protect — **no flag ever overrides another**, so there is no precedence rule anywhere — survives intact, and now holds for `esdev` and `esrun` with one implementation rather than by agreement.
- **`--allow-all` / `-A` becomes real.** D38 rejected it with "there is no --allow-all"; it is now the flag that says *unsandboxed*, and the only way to ask for what used to be the default.
- **`--deny-all` is kept, and is a no-op on `esrun`.** Every existing `esrun --deny-all --allow-net=host app.js` keeps working and means exactly what it did — the flip breaks no command line that was already narrow, only the ones that were relying on the wide default. It is not merely tolerated but recommended on a deploy line: a reader should not have to know which way a binary defaults to know what a service was granted. The mirror holds on `esdev`, where `--allow-all` is the no-op.
- **`process_args` is ungated**, where D38 had it requiring `Env` alongside `process_env` and `process_cwd`. The arguments are the command line that started this program — the same line the permission flags are on — not a read of host state the flags exist to withhold, which is D38's own reasoning for ungating `platform`/`arch`. Under a permissive default the gate cost nothing. Under this one it would mean every script reading its own argv had to be handed `--allow-env`, and with it the whole environment: a strictly *worse* outcome than before the flip, arrived at by leaving something alone. Node and Deno both leave argv ungated.
- **`esdev.json`'s `permissions` is validated against `Baseline::Nothing`** and pinned to its mode with an explicit `--deny-all`/`--allow-all` on the way out. The block states the grant the *deployed* program runs under, so it is read as an `esrun` line even though `esdev start` is what spawns the child — and pinning it means the same list means the same thing whichever binary is handed it.

**Rejected: making `esdev` match.** It was considered, and the argument for it is real: `esdev app.js` running tells you nothing about `esrun app.js`, `esdev test` never exercises the production grant, and the scoped forms — `--allow-net=host` across redirect hops, `--allow-read=./data` narrowing the D25 jail — are the ones people get wrong and the ones dev would still never touch. It was turned down because D59's premise is that the inner loop's cost lands on `esdev`: a dev loop that dies on an unnamed capability at every `--watch` save is precisely the friction that decision exists to absorb, and it would be paid on every save to catch a class of problem that is caught once, by running one command. The drift is accepted, and three things bound it: `esdev --trace-permissions` prints the `esrun` line a run actually needs (now without the `--deny-all` prefix, since there is nothing to prefix); `esdev start` spawns its child under `esdev.json`'s `permissions`, so the loop for a real project already runs under the production grant; and `esdev create` scaffolds that block from the first commit.

**Rejected: a prompt.** Unchanged from D38, and more clearly right now: a deployment has no terminal to answer one.

**Consequences:** `esrun app.js` with no flags is now a single-file, reaching-nothing run — including no module loader, so a multi-file program needs at least `--allow-imports`. That is the breaking change, it is the intended one, and its error message names the flag to add. The **`--allow-all` escape hatch is one flag**, which is the honest migration for anyone who was relying on the old default and is not ready to narrow. The eight-name vocabulary, the scope lists, `permissions.has()`, the worker inheritance rules and every capability check are untouched. Peer position: this puts `esrun` where Deno has been from the start, and where Node is heading with `--permission`; Bun still has nothing.

Verified by 74 end-to-end permission tests against the real binary (the new default, both `--all` flags, rule 1, rule 2 from both baselines and both directions, and `--deny-all` proven equal to the bare default), 5 `cli-common` unit tests over the rule engine itself — including that the baseline is the *only* difference between the binaries, and that the error a wrong-direction flag gets depends on whether a flag or a baseline put it in that mode — and the rest of the workspace's 1,205 tests updated where a fixture needed a grant it used to be handed for free. Documented per D27 (`SPEC.md`, `API.md`, `SECURITY.md`, `README.md`, the site's CLI/security/permissions pages, `CHANGELOG`).

---

### D64 — `esdev create`: templates in the binary, and a scaffolder that owns nothing · *Proposed (2026-08-13)*

**Context:** D60–D63 produced a toolchain and a template, and no way to get from one to the other. The gap is not that the template is hard to write — it is that writing it *correctly* is tedious in a way that produces a specific failure: the permission line. A developer assembling this by hand reaches a demo fastest by running with everything granted, and the narrow line is then a thing to add later, which is a thing never added. A scaffolded project starts narrow.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **The templates are embedded at build time**, by a build script that walks `crates/dev-cli/templates` and emits an `include_bytes!` table. `create` therefore works offline, on a machine with no registry access, and always writes a project the `esdev` that wrote it can build — a fetching scaffolder can hand you something the binary was never tested against, and this project's non-goal on remote module imports is the same argument in a different place. Bytes rather than strings so a template may hold a favicon or a font; the `{{name}}` substitution is attempted only on files that are valid UTF-8. What a *running* template leaves behind (`node_modules`, `dist`, a lockfile) is skipped by the build script and asserted absent by a test: embedding an installed `node_modules` would put tens of megabytes of somebody else's code in this binary, and nothing about the build would complain.
- **It writes files and stops** — no install, no `git init`, no prompts. There is no lockfile yet to say which package manager the project uses, so an install would be a guess that leaves a `package-lock.json` in a bun project; a package installer is a stated non-goal besides. No prompts because every other command here is a flag grammar that works in a script, and a scaffolder that stops to ask cannot be one.
- **It never overwrites, and `--force` does not mean overwrite.** `esdev build --lib` empties its output directory because the build owns it (D59); this owns nothing — the directory it writes into may be a project somebody has worked in for a year. So a non-empty target is refused, and `--force` writes *among* what is there while still leaving every existing file alone, reporting which ones it left.
- **`_gitignore` is written as `.gitignore`.** Not cosmetic: as itself it would apply to the template *in this repository*, which would untrack the file it means to ship. npm's packaging has the same problem and the same fix.
- **The name comes from the directory**, into `package.json` and the document's `<title>`, sanitised — a directory called `My App` would otherwise produce a manifest every package manager rejects, on the first command the user runs.

**Consequences:** `esdev create my-app && cd my-app && npm install && npm run dev` is the whole path from nothing to a running server-rendered React app, and the deploy line at the end of it is `--deny-all --allow-read=./dist --allow-listen=8080`. Verified by scaffolding into a scratch directory and then installing, testing, building and running what came out — including the static build and the hashed assets. **Not solved here:** more templates (hono, elysia and a Remix-style one are the reason the `--template` flag exists at all); a `--pm` flag or lockfile detection, since there is nothing yet to detect; and `create` cannot tell a user their `esdev` is older than the template they want, because there is only one source of both.

---

### D63 — A React template that is a real project, and the three ways to ship it from one build · *Proposed (2026-08-13)*

**Context:** D60–D62 built the machinery — targets, an HTML entry, a dev loop — and the thing they were for did not exist. The stack asked for was "a generic React fullstack app with SSR, SSG and SPA builds", and the question that had to be answered first is whether that is one template or three. It is one, and the reason is mechanical rather than aesthetic: the three differ only in **when** the render happens (per request, at build time, in the browser), and a project that expressed that as three codebases would have three renderers to keep in agreement.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **One render function, called from two places.** `src/render.tsx` streams a whole document for a route; `src/server.tsx` calls it per request and `src/prerender.tsx` calls it per route at build time. A page cannot come out one way live and another way static, because there is one way.
- **One client bundle for all three, by looking at what it finds.** `hydrateRoot` when the root has children, `createRoot` when it does not. That is what lets the single-page build ship the same `index.html` the server uses as a template, with an empty root, and the same bundle that hydrates a prerendered page.
- **The prerender writes to `dist/static/`, not over `dist/`.** `dist/index.html` is the *template* the server splices into, and a rendered page is not a template — writing one over the other breaks SSR in a way that shows up as a blank page. The static build copies `assets/` in beside its pages, so `dist/static` is a whole deployment.
- **The route table is data, not a router.** A path, a component, and a loader that runs on the server. Three fields is not a library, and keeping it as data is what lets the same table be read by the server (to render), the client (to hydrate) and the prerender step (to know what pages exist). Navigation is `<a href>` — a full page load, invisible on a fast server, and a client-side router is left as the user's choice rather than made for them.
- **The loader's result reaches the browser in a `<script>` that escapes `<`.** Without it the client would fetch everything again and render a tree that does not match the markup, and React would throw the server's HTML away. The escape is not incidental: `{"body": "</script>…"}` is the oldest injection there is and `JSON.stringify` does nothing about it. It lives in a module that imports nothing, which is the only reason it can be tested (below).

**Two of `esdev`'s own limits are worked within rather than around, and both are in the template's README as limits.** There is **no CSS pipeline**, so `styles.css` is linked from `index.html` and copied by the build rather than imported from JavaScript. And **component tests cannot run**: `esdev test` runs each file unbundled, React ships CommonJS, and the runtime loads ES modules only — so a test that imports a component is refused, and so is a test that imports the route table, because the table holds components. The first attempt shipped a route-table test that failed for exactly that reason; what ships instead tests `serialize()`, which imports nothing. A template whose test suite fails on `create` would be worse than one with a smaller suite.

**Consequences:** the template is a real project in the tree (`crates/dev-cli/templates/react`), developed and run in place rather than written as text and hoped over — every claim above was checked by building it and driving it: SSR streaming under `--deny-all --allow-read=./dist --allow-listen=8080`, `/api/time`, immutable caching on hashed assets, a 404, the two prerendered pages, and an edit reaching the browser through `esdev start`. Its build artifacts and lockfile are gitignored, since a scaffolded project resolves its own. `{{name}}` is the one placeholder, and it survives `bun install` — a private package's name is not validated, so the template stays installable with the placeholder in it. **Not solved here:** the template cannot declare `@opentf/esrun-types` as a dependency because it is not published yet (npm 404), so `tsconfig.json` omits `types` and the README points at `esdev --install-types`, which is the command that exists for this; a client-side router; and streaming the *shell* before the loader resolves, which needs Suspense boundaries the template does not have.

---

### D62 — `esdev start`: the dev loop is the build on a loop, and the server is the application's · *Proposed (2026-08-13)*

**Context:** D60 and D61 made a project describable — targets, an HTML entry, hashed assets — and left it with no way to *develop* against. The measured facts that shaped the answer were taken before any of it was written: a full React SSR rebuild is **43 ms** and `esrun` reaches "listening" in **14 ms**, so the loop a coarse tool can offer is ~60 ms end to end. That is the number that makes the Vite question answerable: their dev server exists to avoid a bundle that costs seconds, and here it costs milliseconds, so what a module-graph dev server would buy is not speed but *state across an edit* — which is HMR, which needs a transform callable from JavaScript, which esdev does not have (and which is the same trigger that would reopen the `.ts`-config question, D60).

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **It is `esdev build` on a loop.** A dev build differs in exactly two ways: `NODE_ENV` is `"development"` and nothing is content-hashed. Not the conditions, not the platform, not the resolution — a dev and prod that disagree about which file a specifier names is the failure the whole toolchain is arranged around, and a dev-only difference in *resolution* would reintroduce it at the last place anyone would look.
- **The server is the application's.** `start.run` names the target whose output is the server; esdev runs that output as a child process under the config's `permissions` and restarts it with `SIGTERM` — `--watch`'s policy, for `--watch`'s reasons (D59): a fresh process carries nothing forward, and the graceful stop is the one production gives, so a request in flight when a file is saved is answered rather than dropped. **The file that runs in development is the file that runs in production.** Nothing stands in for it, nothing wraps it, and there is no second code path that exists only on a developer's machine — which is the property Vite's middleware mode and Next's `next dev` both give up.
- **esdev serves files only when there is nothing to run.** A static site or SPA has no server of its own, and telling somebody to write one before they can look at their page is not parity with anything. So: files, an `index.html` fallback for paths that look like routes (not for a missing `.js`, which answered with HTML is a syntax error three steps from its cause), and nothing else. No module graph, no transform, no middleware — each of those would be a second, different way to run the app.
- **A failed build changes nothing, and the rebuild happens first.** The initial implementation stopped the child on the change and then rebuilt, which meant a syntax error mid-edit — the most ordinary event in a dev loop — left a dead port and a browser that could not load the page that would explain it. Rebuilding *before* stopping anything also keeps the old server answering during the build.
- **Reload is server-sent events on esdev's own endpoint**, loopback, and injected into the built document rather than the source. One direction, one word, and `EventSource` reconnects by specification — which is the property that matters when the thing on the other end is a build tool the developer will restart; a WebSocket would need framing here and a reconnect loop there to carry a string. *(Superseded by D75: the reasoning held for a channel carrying one word and stopped holding when it began carrying module source.)* It is esdev's rather than the app's so no template ships dev-only code, and it is sent *after* the restart, because a page told to reload while the server is still coming back gets a connection refused and stays blank.
- **A hand-written HTTP responder rather than a new dependency.** `GET`, a file, a content type, a 404 — over the same `TcpStream` idiom `--inspect` already serves its discovery endpoints with (D59). The alternative was a server framework in the binary that runs no servers.

**Two bugs it found, both older than it.** A bundler failure printed its `Debug` representation (`BatchedBuildDiagnostic([BuildDiagnostic { kind: "PARSE_ERROR", … }])`) — unreadable, and the most-seen message in a dev loop; it now names the module and its message. And the watcher matched its ignored directory names **anywhere in the path**, so a project living in a directory called `target`, or a test fixture under `target/tmp`, had every one of its files ignored: the watcher started, reported what it was watching, and then reacted to nothing. Matched below the watch root now. The second was found by the tests failing for a reason that had nothing to do with what they were testing, which is the argument for fixtures that live where real projects live.

**Consequences:** a project with an `esdev.json` is now developable and deployable with two commands and no scripts between them. `esdev` binds a port for the first time, which is a real change to what the binary does — bounded to `start`, loopback only, and unlike `--inspect` it is not a way to run code in a process (it hands out files from a directory the developer named and says one word), so it is not gated behind a build switch. **Not solved here:** HMR; source maps, so a dev stack trace still points into a bundle; and the child outliving a `SIGKILL`ed supervisor, which `--watch` shares and which needs `PR_SET_PDEATHSIG` or an equivalent to fix properly. Verified by 3 unit tests over the watch/ignore rules and 3 end-to-end tests driving the real binary over HTTP: a frontend project served with an SPA fallback and a 404 for a missing script, a rebuild landing in what is served, and a broken build leaving a running server answering — then recovering when it is fixed. Documented per D27 (site `docs/esdev/start`, `CHANGELOG`).

---

### D61 — The document is the entry: an `index.html` target, spliced by byte span · *Proposed (2026-08-13)*

**Context:** D60 gave a project named targets, and the browser half of one was still described the wrong way round. A server bundle's entry is a module because the runtime starts at one; the browser starts at a **document**, and the module is something the document happens to reference. Written as a config target, that means the client entry is named twice — its source path in `esdev.json`, its built URL in the `<script src>` — two places one rename apart from disagreeing, with nothing to catch it. Every other build tool treats HTML as an entry point for exactly this reason, and the earlier plan for this project explicitly deferred it; the maintainer's instruction was that `esdev` exists to fill the gaps a server-only runtime leaves and should not shy away from an HTML parser to do it.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **A target whose `entry` ends in `.html` is a different kind of build, not a setting on the same one.** Its inputs are the tags in the document: a `<script type="module">` is an entry (it and everything it imports become one browser bundle, under the browser conditions D60 introduced), and anything else a relative reference names — a stylesheet, a favicon, an image, a classic script — is copied. Both are content-hashed into `<outdir>/assets`, so a deployment can cache that whole directory immutably, and the document is written out pointing at the results. The keys a document already answers are **refused** on such a target rather than ignored: `out` (its output is a directory however you look at it), `platform` (its scripts run in a browser, that is not in question) and `then: "run"` (there is nothing to execute).
- **A relative path is an input; anything else is a URL.** `./src/entry.client.tsx` names a file in the project; `/assets/vendor.js`, `https://…`, `//cdn…` and `data:` name things this build does not control and are left exactly as written. One line of rule, and it doubles as the escape hatch. A relative path naming nothing **stops the build** — the alternative is finding it in a browser.
- **Everything else in the file is untouched, byte for byte.** The title, the meta tags, the Open Graph block, the inline analytics snippet and the favicon are the author's. This is the property the whole feature is for: a tool that *generated* the document would own all of them.
- **Entry filenames are hashed by renaming after the bundle is written**, rather than by reading the bundler's report of what it called things. Nothing imports an entry — a chunk is imported *by* one, never the other way round — so the rename breaks no reference, and it keeps `rolldown_common`'s output types out of this crate's dependency list for one field.
- **`runtime:*` is not external in a browser bundle.** In a server bundle it is left for the runtime to serve; in a browser nothing serves it, so external would emit a bundle whose first import fails in somebody's browser. Unresolved, it fails at build time naming the module and the importer.

**The dependency, and the correction that got to it.** What this needs is to find the tags that name build inputs and point them at what was built, without touching anything else in a file the developer owns. The first implementation used `lol-html` (Cloudflare's streaming rewriter), which does that well — and was **wrong to add**: its selector engine brings four MPL-2.0 crates (Servo's `cssparser`, `cssparser-macros`, `selectors`, `dtoa-short`), and `deny.toml` fails copyleft by policy (D10). It was committed with a per-crate exception and reported afterwards; it should have been a question asked before any of it was written, and the maintainer said so.

The replacement is better on the merits, which is the part worth recording. **`html5gum`** (MIT, one transitive dependency: `jetscii`, MIT OR Apache-2.0) is a WHATWG-compliant tokenizer that reports the **byte span** of every attribute it emits. With a span, the rewrite is a *splice into the original text* rather than a re-serialisation: the doctype's casing, the author's single quotes, their `&mdash;`, their stray whitespace inside a tag — none of it is even read, let alone rewritten. A tree-based parser (`html5ever`, `swc_html`) would have to print the document back and would normalise all of it; `lol-html` re-emits the tags it touches. Only the attributes being changed move.

Two details the tokenizer earns its keep on, both under test. **Raw-text state switching** (`naively_switch_states`) is what keeps a URL inside a `<script>` string, a CSS comment in a `<style>`, or a `<textarea>` from being read as a reference — each looks exactly like markup, and treating one as such would fail the build on a page that is perfectly correct. And an **unquoted** value's span runs to whatever ended it (the `>`, or the space before the next attribute), because that is the byte the tokenizer learned it was over on; trimming that terminator is the difference between a correct splice and two tags welded together.

**Consequences:** a frontend stack is now describable end to end — `esdev build` turns an `index.html` into a deployable `dist/` with hashed assets — and the fullstack case gets the same file as the template its server splices into, so `<head>` is authored in one place whichever of SSR, SSG or SPA is being built. Multi-page apps need no new concept: a second document is a second target. **Not solved here:** there is no CSS pipeline — a stylesheet is copied, not bundled, so `@import` and `url()` inside it are not followed and `import "./x.css"` from JavaScript is still unsupported; assets referenced only from JavaScript are not discovered; and the asset URL is rooted at the deployment (`/assets/…`), so a site served under a path prefix needs a `base` key that does not exist yet. Also unresolved and **pre-existing**: `cargo deny check licenses` already failed before this change on `xxhash-rust` (BSL-1.0, reached through rolldown), which is permissive and OSI-approved but absent from the allow list — left alone here rather than fixed in passing, since it is a separate policy call. Verified by 8 unit tests over the discovery and splice passes and 4 end-to-end tests through the real binary: a React entry with a dynamic import (the chunk is emitted beside the hashed entry and still imported correctly after the rename), the four kinds of URL-that-is-text, an unquoted attribute in three positions, and a `diff` of the built document against the source with the rewritten attributes masked — identical. Documented per D27 (site `docs/esdev/build`, `CHANGELOG`, `NOTICE`, `docs/LICENSING.md`).

---

### D60 — `esdev.json`: what a project builds is a property of the project · *Proposed (2026-08-13)*

**Context:** every knob in this toolchain has been a flag, and for a *run* that is right — a flag is typed by a person, in view, once, and D38's whole argument is that the grant a service runs under should be visible on the line that started it. A **build** is not that. The stack this is being aimed at next is a React application that renders on the server and hydrates in the browser: two bundles, from two entries, with two shapes of output (one file; a directory, because a dynamic `import()` emits a chunk beside its entry), and if it prerenders, a third that has to *run* after the others are built. `esdev build` takes one entry and writes one file, so describing that project means three command lines — which in practice means three `package.json` scripts, written twice, once for the dev loop and once for the release, drifting apart from the day they are written. The dev/release drift is the failure that matters: it is the same class as the one D59's crate split exists to prevent, one level up.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **A file of named targets, read by `esdev build` when no entry is named.** `esdev build` builds all of them, `--target=<name>` builds one, `esdev build <entry>` ignores the file entirely — so a project that has a config can still bundle a scratch file. A flag beats the file (`--minify` takes a release build of a project whose day to day is unminified), which is the same precedence `--define` already had over the built-in `NODE_ENV`.
- **Four keys carry every difference between stacks, and nothing about the stacks is named.** `out` vs `outdir` (one file, or a directory); `platform` (`server`/`browser`); `assets` (copied into the output); `then: "run"` (execute the output once built). A backend is one `out`; a frontend is a browser `outdir` plus a prerender target; a fullstack app is both. **`esdev` does not learn what SSR, SSG or SPA are** — `then: "run"` is the whole of static-site generation from this side: the bundle runs, and what it writes is the build's real output. That is what keeps the same three keys serving hono, elysia or anything else without a second concept.
- **JSON, not `esdev.config.ts`.** Vite and Next both take an executable config and both are right to: theirs carry **plugins**, and a plugin is a function. `esdev` has no plugin API, no resolver hooks and no transform pipeline, so a `.ts` config here would be a program whose entire content is data. There is also an ordering problem specific to this project: the file carries `permissions`, so executing it to learn what a run may do means running guest code before that has been decided — a hole in the one property the runtime is built around, and one Vite never has to think about because it has no capability model. The key names are chosen so a future `esdev.config.ts` can export the same shape and leave every existing `esdev.json` valid. **The trigger to revisit is concrete**: the day `esdev` exposes a hook that takes a function — which is the same day HMR becomes possible — a plugin becomes expressible and this decision should be re-opened, not before.
- **`esrun` never reads it, and this is the line the design holds.** A production binary that picks up a checked-in file granting itself capabilities is precisely what the capability model exists to prevent; the grant a service runs under belongs on the command that deployed it, not in a file that travelled with the source. `permissions` in the file therefore shapes only the child a developer's machine runs, which is how you develop *under* production's grants without being able to ship them by accident. It is validated by **translating it into the flags it stands for** and handing them to the parser `esrun` uses — one model, not two dialects of what `read` means, and the one that would have drifted is the one granting capabilities.
- **A mistyped key is an error that names the key it was nearly.** `outDir` → "unknown key `outDir`. Did you mean `outdir`?", by case-insensitive edit distance ≤ 2. A tolerated unknown key is a setting that silently does nothing, which for `minify` is a slow bundle and for `platform` is the wrong build of a dependency shipped to a browser.

**A bug this found, which was silent and is now fixed.** An application build asserts the `worker` condition (D59) — how `react-dom/server` hands over its Web Streams implementation instead of its `node:stream` one. That is correct for a server bundle and wrong for a client one, which wants `browser`. Conditions match in the order the **package author** wrote them (D40), so `worker` being asserted at all was enough to win, and nothing failed at build time: the failure was a worker-targeted build of a dependency running in somebody's browser. `platform: "browser"` now asserts `browser` *instead of* `worker` — alternatives, not additions — and takes rolldown's browser platform with it, for the `browser` field, which predates `exports` and is still how a good deal of the registry redirects away from `node:` builtins. Verified with a synthetic package whose `exports` lists `worker` before `browser`: the server target inlines the worker build, the browser target the browser one.

**Consequences:** `esdev build` gains `--config` and `--target`; `BuildConfig` gains a directory output, a platform and assets, all of which the command line leaves at their old values, so every existing invocation behaves exactly as before (verified by the existing 60-test suite passing untouched). Targets are **sorted by name** rather than taken in file order — `serde_json` preserves insertion order only when a feature enables it, and that feature is currently on because something else in the workspace asked for it, which is an ordering that would change under an unrelated dependency edit. A `then: "run"` target runs in a **child process**, after every target is built: it is somebody's program, so one that wedges or calls `exit()` should take only itself down, and a prerender that emits HTML referencing the browser bundle must not run before that bundle exists. Assets are copied **a file by name, a directory by its contents**, so `public/styles.css` lands at the output root and is served at `/styles.css` with nothing rewriting an href — and since the runtime resolves a relative path against the *entry module's* directory, a server bundle in `dist/` reading `index.html` reads `dist/index.html`, which is what makes `dist/` the entire deployment. **Not solved here:** `start` and `permissions` are parsed and validated but nothing consumes them yet — they belong to `esdev start`, and validating them now means a typo is reported by the build that read the file rather than held until the other command exists. Also unsolved: an HTML target type (the entry is still a module, and the script tag in an `index.html` is a URL rather than a source path), content hashing of entry files, source maps, and any per-target `lib`. Verified by 13 unit tests over the grammar and 8 end-to-end tests through the real binary, including the three-target project reaching disk from one `esdev build`. Documented per D27 (site `docs/esdev/build`, `CHANGELOG`).

---

### D59 — `esdev`: a second binary, because the production one earns its narrowness · *Proposed (2026-08-11)*

**Context:** `esrun` grants every capability by default and can be narrowed to nothing (D38), loads ES modules only (D22), and has no development surface whatsoever — no inspector, no REPL, no watch, no test runner, no transform. That is not an oversight; it is what makes the deploy line (`--deny-all --allow-listen=8080`) a statement rather than a hope. An inspector port in particular is a total bypass of the capability model: attach and you own the isolate regardless of every flag.

But the bill for that narrowness lands entirely on the developer's inner loop, and it is large. Measured against a real project this session: React ships pure CJS, so `import "react-dom/server.edge"` is a hard stop; there is no debugger, so `console.log` is the whole toolkit; there is no watch, so every change is a manual restart; and there is no tool that helps a developer arrive at the right capability flags, which means in practice they ship the default and the differentiator evaporates in the last mile.

Three options were considered:

1. **Add the development features to `esrun` behind flags.** Rejected: it puts an inspector, a watcher and test discovery in the binary that runs in production, and makes "esrun has no way to undo its permission model" false.
2. **Ship no development binary; document external tooling.** Rejected: the CJS wall and the missing capability-discovery tool are not things an external tool can solve, because both need the runtime's own resolver and its own capability vocabulary.
3. **A second binary, `esdev`.** Chosen.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **`esdev` may change what happens *around* a run — never what the JS sees.** Watching, restarting, attaching, discovering, reporting, building are its scope. Same prelude, same snapshot, same providers, same capability enforcement. The one sanctioned exception is a future `esdev test`, since a test file is never a production artifact.
- **The shared half is a crate, not a convention.** Everything that decides how a run behaves moved into `es-runtime-cli-common`: the baked prelude snapshot, the D38 permission grammar, the provider wiring, the drive loop, graceful shutdown, and the error block. Neither binary keeps a second copy of any of it. A convention ("keep them in sync") would have drifted within two releases, and the failure mode is the worst available — a permission flag that means something different in the binary you test with than in the one you deploy.
- **The dependency order absorbs it cleanly**: `common → engine → providers → runtime → default-providers → cli-common → {runtime-cli, dev-cli}`. `cli-common` sits above `default-providers`, so `dev-cli` can later pull development-only dependencies (a file watcher, a line editor, a transformer) without any of them touching the library or the production binary. That is what makes the split cheap rather than merely tidy.
- **The snapshot is built once**, in `cli-common`, and shared. A second binary therefore costs no second V8 snapshot build.
- **`esdev` is not a deployment target**, and its `--help` says so rather than leaving it to documentation.

**Amendment (the TS/JSX transform, 2026-08-11).** The first of the deferred increments has landed, and the seam it needed is worth recording because it is not the obvious one. A transform that wrapped the `ModuleLoader` would be the natural design and would be **wrong**: the entry file is read directly in `run()`, before a loader exists, so wrapping the loader strips every file a program imports and chokes on the one the user named. The seam is therefore a `SourceTransform` on `Config`, applied in two places — to the entry source, and through a `TransformingLoader` that delegates resolution untouched (including `resolve_sync`, so `import.meta.resolve` and `import()` cannot disagree — D41) and rewrites only `ModuleSource::Text`. `esrun` passes `None` and gains neither the dependency nor the behaviour, which is the payoff the crate split was for.

Three rules fix the shape:

- **Types are erased, never checked.** Node's `--experimental-strip-types` and Bun make the same call. A typechecker on the critical path of every run costs a second or more per launch for a diagnostic the editor already showed, and it would make `esdev` refuse programs `esrun` runs.
- **Import specifiers are left exactly as written.** No extension guessing; `./app.js` does not resolve to `app.ts`. Resolution is the loader's contract (D21/D40), and a transform that widened it would make the two binaries disagree about which file a program is — the one failure this whole design exists to prevent.
- **A `.js` file is passed through, not reprinted.** Round-tripping it through parser and printer would move every line in its stack traces for no gain.

JSX compiles to the automatic runtime (`react/jsx-runtime` by default), redirected per file by `/** @jsxImportSource … */`. That pragma is what makes a non-React framework work without a flag: a Remix v3 SSR app written as real `.tsx` targets `remix/ui` with one comment and no configuration, which was checked end-to-end against the published `remix@3.0.0-beta.5` before this landed.

**Found by running it, not by reading it:** `SemanticBuilder::with_enum_eval(true)` is mandatory, not a tuning knob — a TypeScript `enum` compiles to an IIFE whose members may refer to earlier members, and without evaluated constants the transformer *panics*. A one-line `enum` in a user's file was a crash. The unit tests over the stripper's output did not catch it; the first end-to-end run of a real `.ts` file did. There are now tests at both levels for `enum`, parameter properties and `namespace` — the constructs that emit code rather than vanishing.

Verified by 13 unit tests over the emitted source and 10 end-to-end tests driving the real binary, including one that asserts **`esrun` still refuses the TypeScript `esdev` runs** — the moment it passes, the transform has leaked into production.

**Amendment (`esdev build`, 2026-08-11).** The second increment, and the one that pays for the whole design. `rolldown` (MIT, on crates.io, the same bundler Vite 8 uses and on the `oxc` generation already in the tree) is confined to `dev-cli` exactly as `oxc` is.

**What it buys is not convenience, it is reach without compromise.** D22 says ES module packages only, and a large share of npm — React included — still ships CommonJS. The options were to teach the runtime `require` (breaking the non-goal, and the property that a deployed artifact is the text that was reviewed) or to convert on the developer's machine. The bundler absorbs CJS→ESM at build time and `esrun` receives ordinary ESM, so the non-goal holds *completely* rather than being narrowed again.

**A second effect, not anticipated when D59 was written and worth stating plainly: bundling removes a capability from production.** An unbundled program needs `--allow-imports`, because the loader walks `node_modules` at runtime; a bundle has no imports left to resolve, so `esrun --deny-all --allow-listen=8080 dist/app.js` is the whole grant. The build is a security step, not only a packaging one. It was found by running the two side by side, not by reasoning about them.

Four settings are the actual product — each is silent when wrong, which is why this is a command rather than documentation telling people to run a bundler:

- **`runtime:*` stays external.** It is served by the runtime and has no file behind it; inlining it produces a bundle that fails at its first import. This is the one a hand-written config gets wrong.
- **ESM output.** The runtime has no other module system.
- **`process.env.NODE_ENV` is defined**, because packages branch on it before doing anything and there is no `process` global here — undefined is a crash, not a missed optimisation.
- **The `worker` condition is asserted.** It is how a package with an `exports` map hands over its Web-API build: `react-dom/server` resolves to a Web Streams implementation under it and to a `node:stream` one without it. Verified against the real package rather than assumed.

`--conditions` **adds** to the defaults rather than replacing them, so asking for one more cannot silently cost `worker`. `--define` overrides or extends. The **runtime's** condition set stays standards-only (D40) and this does not change it: the escape hatch belongs to the bundler, where a developer chose to run it, not to a server resolving imports under load. That closes the `--conditions=<list>` question D40 left open — as a build flag, not a runtime one.

`Platform::Neutral` is deliberate over `Browser` or `Node`: this runtime is neither, and either would pull that platform's `main` fields and aliases in by implication. `main_fields` is then set explicitly to `["module", "main"]`, because Neutral leaves it empty and that breaks any package old enough to have no `exports` map.

Verified by 2 unit tests and 9 end-to-end tests driving the real binary over real `node_modules` trees — including a CommonJS package that `esrun` refuses unbundled and runs bundled, a two-branch `exports` map proving the `worker` condition is taken, a three-branch one proving `--conditions` adds rather than replaces, and a bundle that runs under `--deny-all` where the unbundled program cannot. End-to-end beyond the suite: React 19 streaming SSR behind Hono, from CJS packages, served by `esrun --deny-all --allow-listen`.

**Not in this increment:** watching a build, source maps, code splitting, and any output format but ESM. Sourcemaps are the next real gap, since a minified bundle's stack traces are currently unmapped.

**The lesson this increment actually taught, and it is not about bundling: a dependency added to *any* workspace member can change how the runtime behaves, because Cargo unifies features across everything built together.** Rolldown did it twice. Once through `serde_json/arbitrary_precision`, which changed what `YAML.build` emitted (fixed above by not transcoding through a foreign `Value`). Once through `tracing-subscriber`, whose `log` bridge turns a dependency's `log::trace!` into a tracing event — and since those are emitted on our tasks they inherit our spans, so `mio::poll` announcing a deregistration landed inside an HTTP connection span carrying its `peer` field and failed a test asserting that a healthy connection logs nothing. The capture layer now ignores bridged records, which is what it always meant.

Both were invisible to `cargo test -p <crate>` and only appeared under `cargo test --workspace`. Neither was a bug in the new dependency. The general defence is the same in both cases and is worth stating as a rule: **code that can be reached from the runtime must not depend on the ambient configuration of a shared crate** — not its optional features, not a globally installed bridge. Where that is not achievable, the workspace-wide test run is the thing that catches it, so it must stay the gate rather than per-crate runs.

**Amendment (`--watch`, 2026-08-11).** Two choices, and both were made for the same reason — the runtime already knows how to do the hard part.

**The program runs in a child process.** The alternative is tearing down the `Runtime` in place and building another, which means recovering every piece of host state: listening sockets, worker threads, the isolate, the signal registry. One missed handle poisons every later run and the failure looks like the user's bug. A fresh process cannot carry anything forward, and the snapshot makes starting one cheap. Node's `--watch` and nodemon land here too.

**A restart is a `SIGTERM`, not a kill** — which means **a restart drains**. The Phase 14 shutdown watcher already stops accepting, lets in-flight HTTP requests answer, and exits `128+signal`; the supervisor just sends the signal and waits. Saving a file while a request is open therefore does not drop that request, which is the difference between a watcher you can leave running while you use the app and one you cannot. Verified with a two-second handler edited mid-request: the response still arrived. `--shutdown-grace` is the backstop for a program that ignores the signal (a guest with its own handler, or a wedged loop), and it is the same number production uses.

**Watched: a directory tree, not the module graph.** The graph is more precise and the runtime knows it — but it knows it *in the child*, and shipping it back is a protocol between two processes for a gain that shows only on large projects. The tree is the project root (nearest `package.json` — the same root the loader detects, so what is watched and what can be imported agree) or the entry's directory, minus the directories nobody edits by hand.

**The bug worth recording, because it is not obvious and it is silent:** `inotify` reports *reads*. A child process opening its entry file to load it raises `Access(Open)` on a watched path, so a watcher that treats every event as a change **restarts because it restarted** — an infinite loop with nobody touching anything. Only modifying events (`Create`, `Remove`, `Modify(Data|Name)`) count; `Access` and `Modify(Metadata)` — an access-time bump is not an edit — do not. It was found by running the thing, not by reading it, and it took a debug print to see: from the outside it looked like a debounce problem.

A second, smaller trap found the same way: the first end-to-end test staged its files under `CARGO_TARGET_TMPDIR`, which lives inside `target/` — a directory the watcher ignores by design. The test watched nothing and would have passed for the wrong reason had the assertion been weaker.

Verified by 6 unit tests (both filters, including the read-is-not-a-change regression, and root detection with and without a `package.json`) and 3 end-to-end tests driving the real binary — a change reruns the program, a run with **no** edit stays at exactly one run, and `--watch` with `-e` is refused since there is nothing to watch.

**Windows caveat, stated rather than pretended:** there is no `SIGTERM` and no console-independent way to ask another process to stop cleanly, so a restart there kills rather than drains.

**Amendment (`esdev test`, 2026-08-11).** Two decisions, and the second one was forced rather than chosen.

**One process per file.** A test suite is where isolation matters most: a file that wedges, exhausts its heap, or calls `process.exit()` must not decide the fate of the others, and a global left behind by one must not be visible to the next. The same mechanism `--watch` uses — `esdev` re-executing itself — gives all of it, and the snapshot makes it cheap. `--file=<path>` is what a child is invoked with, and is equally a supported way to run one file by hand, so the parent/child protocol is public surface rather than a hidden flag.

**The test file is the entry, and the harness is prepended to its own source.** The obvious design — generate a driver module that imports the test file — *cannot work here*, and the reason is one of this project's own rules: module resolution is jailed to the project root detected from the entry's directory (D25), so a driver written to a temp directory could not import a test file in the project at all. Prepending through the existing `SourceTransform` seam (D59's transform amendment) means the file keeps its path, its jail, its relative imports and its TypeScript, and simply arrives with `test()` already defined. The seam built for type-stripping turned out to be the seam a test runner needed, which is the argument for having put it on `Config` rather than in the loader.

The globals are the ones this repository's own conformance suite uses (`test`, `assert`, `assertEquals`, `assertThrows`, `assertRejects`) rather than a new vocabulary: someone reading the runtime's tests and writing their own should not learn two.

**The harness is injected as a single physical line.** Not a micro-optimisation — a failing assertion has to name the line the developer wrote, and thirty lines of prepended harness moves every line number in the file. The first run of this proved it: an assertion on line 2 of a five-line file reported line 41. The harness therefore carries no `//` comments, since one would comment out the file once the newlines are gone, and a `debug_assert` plus a test enforces that. Only line 1's *columns* shift.

Verified by 8 unit tests (suffix recognition, entry-only wrapping, TypeScript still stripped, line numbers preserved, no line comments, discovery skipping machine-written directories, filters) and 7 end-to-end tests driving the real binary — a mixed suite exits non-zero and names both files, a passing suite exits zero, a `.test.ts` runs with its relative `.ts` import, a failure names the developer's line, a file that calls `exit()` does not end the run, a filter selects by path, and an empty run is an error rather than a silent pass.

**Known cosmetic:** a reported stack still includes the harness's own frames (all at line 1). They are noise rather than wrong, and filtering them needs a rule for telling harness frames from user code on line 1 — deferred rather than guessed at. **Not in this increment:** parallelism across files, `describe` nesting, per-file timeouts, and a machine-readable reporter — the last is what `tsr` would want, and is the first thing to add.

**Correction to this decision's own plan:** D59 listed `--trace-permissions` as self-contained. It is not. Capabilities are enforced at op dispatch in `engine` (`OpDecl::requires`, checked in `op.rs`), so recording what a run *used* needs an observer hook there and pass-through in `runtime` and `cli-common` — four crates, including the production core, for a development feature. It is still worth building, but it is a cross-cutting change like `--inspect` and should be decided as one rather than slipped in. Note also that a cargo feature is **not** a safe way to keep either out of `esrun`: Cargo unifies features across a workspace build, which is exactly how rolldown twice changed the runtime's behaviour (recorded above). A runtime-configurable hook that defaults to off is the honest mechanism.

**Amendment (`esdev test` assertions, 2026-08-14).** Three defects, all found by pointing the runner at the largest hand-written JS in this repository — `crates/runtime/js`, the source of `runtime:serialization` — which until now was tested by `bun test` and so had never exercised our own runner.

**`assertEquals` compared through `JSON.stringify`.** That is wrong here in a way it would not be elsewhere: `JSON.stringify` *throws* on a `BigInt`, so on a runtime whose Protobuf implementation is built on int64 the one assertion those tests most need could not be written at all. It also rendered a `Uint8Array` as `{"0":1,"1":2}` rather than comparing bytes, and was sensitive to object key *order*, which no equality assertion wants. It now walks the values — `Object.is` for `BigInt` and `NaN`, bytes for typed arrays and `ArrayBuffer`, contents for `Map`/`Set`, key sets for objects — and remembers the pairs it is mid-comparison on, so a cycle terminates. The published type already said "deep-equals"; the code now agrees with it.

**`assertThrows(fn, x)` treated `x` as a failure label, so it asserted nothing.** The natural line to write — `assertThrows(fn, "TypeError")` — passed on *any* throw, including the wrong one. Every call site in this repository was already written that way and every one of them was asserting less than its author believed. The second argument is now the expectation (a name or message substring, a `RegExp` over the message, or a constructor for `instanceof`) and the failure label moved to the third. This is the one intentionally breaking change; it was made because the compatible reading is the one nobody was using.

**The single-physical-line invariant did not survive the type stripper.** The harness was prepended and the result handed to `TypeStripper`, which re-prints through oxc's codegen — and codegen does not preserve line positions, so the folded harness came back out as one statement per line. Every line number in a `.ts` stack was pushed down by the length of the harness: with the old harness an assertion on line 2 reported line 44, and the larger one here would have made it 151. The order is now stripped-then-wrapped, so the harness never reaches the printer. `.js` was always correct, which is why this survived — the invariant held for exactly the files that did not need it.

**Amendment (`--inspect`, 2026-08-12).** The cross-cutting one, and the switch it needed is the part worth recording.

**An environment variable read by a build script, not a Cargo feature.** D59's own correction said a feature would not work — Cargo unifies features across everything built in one invocation, so a feature declared by `dev-cli` is also on in the `esrun` built beside it, which is how rolldown twice changed the runtime's behaviour. `engine/build.rs` therefore emits `--cfg inspector` when `ES_RUNTIME_INSPECTOR=1` is set for the invocation, and the V8 half of `engine::inspector` is compiled only then. The difference that matters is not mechanical but social: **a feature is declared, and so is on for ever; a variable exists only when a human types it.**

That still leaves one invocation able to set it for both binaries, so the separation is made something a build **fails on**: `runtime-cli` gained a build script whose only job is to refuse while the variable is set, naming the command that is correct instead (`ES_RUNTIME_INSPECTOR=1 cargo build --release -p es-runtime-dev-cli`). One invocation can now never produce both an `esdev` with an inspector and an `esrun` at all. The runtime-configurable hook D59 asked for is there too, underneath: `attach_inspector` is `Option` on `Config`, `esrun` passes `None`, and a build without the switch returns `Error::Unsupported` — so a binary lacking an inspector *says so* rather than accepting `--inspect` and listening on nothing. Three locks, and the compile-time one is the only one that removes the code.

**The seam is `Config`, again.** The same one the TS transform and the test harness took: `Config { inspector: Option<Inspector> }`, carrying a transport and a flag. What crosses into `engine` is `InspectorTransport` — four methods over JSON text, no V8 types, no transport dependency — so the WebSocket server, `tokio-tungstenite` and the CDP discovery endpoints live in `dev-cli` exactly as `oxc` and `rolldown` do. D2/D3 hold: `engine` is still the only crate that names the `v8` crate.

**The server runs on its own thread, because a paused program is a stopped one.** When V8 stops at a breakpoint it hands the isolate's thread to the embedder and does not take it back until told to resume: no timers, no I/O, and no socket read that could deliver the `Debugger.resume` being waited for. So the socket lives on a second thread with its own tokio runtime, and the halves meet at two queues — a `std::sync::mpsc` the isolate's thread *blocks* on (that block **is** the pause) and a tokio channel the server drains. The engine is otherwise pumped once per `tick`, and rings the driver's existing waker (`set_async_waker`, D4) from the server thread, which is what makes setting a breakpoint on an idle server take effect immediately rather than at the next request — measured at 0 ms against a program whose only pending work was a 60-second timer.

**Found by running it, and it is an ordering bug of the kind reading does not catch:** a client attaches and sends its first message in one breath, so "has a new client attached?" asked *before* the receive is always one message too early. Under `--inspect-brk` that meant the `Debugger.enable` opening the session was dispatched to no session at all, and both sides then waited for the other for ever. The check now happens after the receive and before every dispatch. A fresh session per client is itself required, not tidiness: V8 replays `Debugger.scriptParsed` for the scripts it already knows only on a session's *first* `Debugger.enable`, so a reattached DevTools handed the old session opens on an empty Sources pane.

Three smaller decisions, each made once: `ensure_default_context_in_group` is implemented (it is the difference between `Runtime.evaluate` working and answering "Cannot find default execution context" to every client that does not name a context — which is most debug consoles); the endpoint binds loopback by default and *warns* rather than refuses when told otherwise, since a debugger port is a way to run code in a process regardless of what it was denied; and the port is bound with `SO_REUSEADDR`, without which `esdev --watch --inspect` fails on the first restart — a debugger attached across a restart guarantees connections in `TIME_WAIT` on the port being rebound.

**Known limitation, stated rather than left to be discovered:** `console.log` output does not appear in the debugger's console. V8 generates `Runtime.consoleAPICalled` for *its* built-in console, and this runtime's prelude replaces that with one that goes through the `Console` provider. Output is in the terminal, where it has always been; wiring it to both is its own change.

Verified by 8 unit tests over the address grammar and the HTTP/target plumbing, and 6 end-to-end tests that drive the real binary the way a debugger does — attach over the real WebSocket, speak real CDP: the discovery endpoints describe the target, a breakpoint set by URL stops the program at the line and a local is readable through `evaluateOnCallFrame`, an idle program answers `Runtime.evaluate` without waiting for its timer, `--inspect-brk` really has not run a statement when the client arrives, a debugger that disconnects without releasing does not leave the program held, and **`esrun` does not know the flag at all**. The whole file adapts to the build: without the inspector each test asserts the one thing left to assert — that `--inspect` fails with the line telling you how to get one.

**Amendment (`--trace-permissions`, 2026-08-12).** The last deferred increment, and the one D59's context paragraph was actually about: there was no tool that helped a developer arrive at the right capability flags, so in practice they shipped the default and the differentiator evaporated in the last mile.

**The observation point is the capability check, not the providers.** `OpDecl::requires`, checked in `op.rs`, is the only place that knows what a program *reached for* rather than what it was handed — and it sees a denial as clearly as a grant, which is why the observer is called for every capability an op names *before* the first missing one is refused. Reporting only the first would hide the rest of what the op wanted, and a denial is the most interesting thing a trace can record.

**A runtime hook, not a build-time switch.** The opposite call from `--inspect`, for a reason worth stating: an inspector *acts* and an observer cannot, so there is nothing here to keep out of `esrun` — only a branch to keep cheap. It is one `Option` read per dispatch, `None` in every production run. (D59's correction that a Cargo feature would not have kept it out either still holds; it is simply not needed.)

**One check lives above the op boundary and had to be traced separately.** Whether a module may be loaded is decided in `runtime`, not at an op — so an op-only observer would print a deploy line with no `--allow-imports`, which is a line that does not run. `Runtime` therefore holds the observer as well and records the loader's check as `import`. This was the one way the feature could have been *confidently wrong*, which is worse than absent.

**Workers are traced into the same report**, which is what makes the observer `Send + Sync`: each worker is its own thread and its own isolate, and a worker's grants are set at the spawn — the place they are hardest to get right, and the one a report that stopped at the main agent would say nothing about.

**The report is printed however the run ended.** `run()` is now a wrapper around the run itself so that every early `return Err` reports too — the run a developer most wants a report from is the one that *failed for want of a permission* — and the three paths that leave through `process::exit` (a guest `exit()`, a `^C` drain) call it explicitly, since no wrapper catches those and for a server that is every run.

**What it does not do, said in the report itself:** scopes. The line grants each capability unnarrowed, because a scope is enforced provider-side rather than at the capability bit, and narrowing it (`--allow-read=./data`) is still the developer's. A trace that implied otherwise would be worse than one that admits it.

Verified by 7 unit tests over the report and 4 end-to-end tests that make the claim checkable rather than plausible: the printed line is **run under `esrun`**, and then each grant is dropped from it in turn and the same program must fail. Sufficiency alone would pass for a trace that printed every flag there is; minimality is the property worth having.

**Deferred to their own increments, in this order:** ☑ the TS/JSX transform; ☑ `build`; ☑ `--watch`; ☑ `test`; ☑ `--inspect`; ☑ `--trace-permissions` — all landed, each amended above. **D59 is complete.**

**Amendment (`types` moves out of `esrun`, 2026-08-12).** The reverse direction of the same principle, and it should have been obvious sooner: `esrun types --install`'s entire effect was to write into `node_modules` and rewrite a `tsconfig.json`. That is development tooling, in the binary whose whole claim is that it has none — and it cost the production binary `serde_json` and every `.d.ts` baked in by `include_str!`.

It is now `esdev --install-types`, and rewritten rather than moved: the definitions are published to npm as `@opentf/esrun-types`, so nothing fabricates a package under `node_modules/@opentf/esrun` any more. The command adds the real package with the manager the lockfile names, and registers it as a `compilerOptions.types` entry — the form the package's own `index.d.ts` documents, so there is one answer rather than two. A JSONC `tsconfig.json` is left untouched with the lines to add printed, because re-emitting it from a JSON value would silently delete the comments. `esrun types` is kept as a *recognised* word solely so it can name its replacement; without that the parser would take it for a script and answer "cannot read types".

**`upgrade` stays in `esrun`, deliberately.** The same audit asked the same question of it, and the answer is different: it is reachable only by explicitly typing `esrun upgrade`, never from guest JS, never at startup, and it does nothing on any ordinary run. It is how an installed `esrun` updates itself, and `esdev` is not published — moving it would leave the shipped binary with no update path to solve a risk that is not there.

**Not moved, and worth recording so it is not asked again: the console.** `console.log`'s formatting is a WinterTC surface implemented in the prelude and baked into the shared snapshot. Moving or trimming it in one binary would change what the JS sees, which is the one thing D59 forbids.

**Rejected, not deferred:** a formatter and a linter. Neither `oxc_formatter` nor `oxc_linter` is published as a usable library crate (the former's versions are yanked, the latter has never been published), so embedding either means a git dependency — which would be the first exception to D7's pinned-crates-io supply-chain rule, in the binary developers run on their own machines. `oxlint` and `oxfmt` are npm binaries that already work; a shim around them would add a version matrix for no gain. Also rejected: a package installer (D22 already resolves an existing `node_modules` and installs nothing, which is correct), a task runner (`tsr` covers it, polyglot and repo-aware), and a browser-facing dev server with HMR (`@opentf/web-cli` covers it; a server runtime's bundling need is one deployable file, not an asset graph).

**Amendment (`esdev build --lib`, 2026-08-13).** `esdev build` was written for the artifact that gets deployed, and every one of its four settings is correct for that and wrong for a **library** — a package published to npm for other people's programs to import. Nothing in `esdev` served the author of one, so this repository's own two drivers (`@opentf/esrun-postgres`, `@opentf/esrun-redis`) were built by `tsc`, which is why `typescript` is a devDependency in a repository that otherwise has none.

**The unit of a library build is a directory, not an entry, and this is the decision the rest follows from.** A bundle has one root; that is what makes it one file. A library has none — which of its modules a consumer may import is decided by the package's `exports` map, long after the build ran. So `--lib` takes the source directory and makes **every module in it an entry**, the way `tsc` builds a `rootDir`. This is not a convenience: a module reached only as an import keeps just the exports its importer used. Found by building the Redis driver with an earlier version that took an entry — tree-shaking removed `BLOCKING_COMMANDS` from `protocol/blocking.js` because only a test imported it, and the failure surfaced as a `SyntaxError` in the consumer rather than as anything the build said. Setting `treeshake: false` was not enough; entry-ness is what preserves an export. **An export no current caller uses is not dead code in a library — it is the API**, and whatever really is dead, the consumer's build removes, since it can only shake what it was given.

The other three invert for one reason each, all the same reason: a library is an *input to somebody else's build*, so these are their decisions to make. **Dependencies stay external** — inlining `hono` publishes a private copy no consumer can dedupe, override or patch. **Nothing is defined and no condition asserted** — `NODE_ENV=production` and `worker` freeze the consumer's environment into the package. **Module structure is preserved** file for file, which is what makes a subpath in an `exports` map a real file.

**`.d.ts` is emitted, and derived rather than inferred.** A library is a typed contract, and a build that emitted only JavaScript would leave every author reaching for a second tool — which is exactly the state this found. oxc's isolated-declarations transform reads the annotations the source already carries and prints them; it runs no type checker, which is the same contract type-stripping has had since D59 (*erased, never checked*) and costs microseconds per file rather than a checker's pass. It is one more feature on the `oxc` umbrella already in the tree, so the dependency graph does not grow.

The price is TypeScript's own `isolatedDeclarations` rule: an exported signature must state its type. **A signature that does not fails the build with the whole list, rather than getting a declaration that had to be guessed** — a wrong `.d.ts` is worse than none, because it is believed. Measured before committing to it rather than after: the two drivers needed **nine annotations between them** (3 in postgres, 6 in redis), all on `defineDriver` results, `new Dialect(...)` constants and one defaulted parameter. `--no-types` is the way out for a library that would rather annotate later.

**Verified against the real packages, which is the only validation of a library builder worth having.** Both drivers build with `esdev build --lib src` into the tree `tsc` produced, their declarations typecheck under `tsc --noEmit` and a consumer resolves through them, and their unit suites pass unchanged under `esrun` against the esdev-built `dist/` — including the Redis file that caught the tree-shaking bug. Plus 7 end-to-end tests, each pinning one default *not* being applied. **Not changed here:** the packages' own `build.mjs` still runs `tsc`; switching them would put a Rust toolchain in their publish job, which is a release-pipeline decision rather than a tooling one.

**Amendment (`--dts-bundle`, a declaration linker, 2026-08-13).** `--lib` emits a `.d.ts` beside each module, which is what a package with subpath exports needs — `@you/pkg/pool` has to find a real `pool.d.ts`. A package whose `exports` map has one entry wants the opposite: one `index.d.ts`, so what is published is a file rather than a mirror of a source layout nobody outside the package should have to know about.

**Nothing off the shelf does this, and that was checked rather than assumed.** `tsc` emits one declaration per source file and has no declaration-bundling mode — `--outFile` is a legacy `module: none/amd/system` feature and does not bundle ESM declarations, which is precisely why `rollup-plugin-dts` and API Extractor exist. Rolldown's 23 Rust crates contain no `.d.ts` support; the only mention of one is a comment referring to `rolldown-plugin-dts`, which is an **npm** package (`cargo search` finds no such crate), and which works by running a *second* bundling pass over declarations tsc already emitted. So this is that second pass, written here over the `oxc` parser and semantic analysis already in the tree. No new dependency.

**Declarations travel as text, not as an AST.** Each one carries the bytes that produced it plus the byte ranges where a module-scope name appears; inlining is concatenation and renaming is a splice. The alternative — moving AST nodes between modules and printing the result, which is what a JavaScript bundler does — is the wrong trade for declarations on three counts. **JSDoc survives byte for byte**, and in a `.d.ts` the comments *are* the documentation: they are what an editor shows on hover, and oxc's printer already reindents the ones it keeps. **No parser arena outlives the module it came from**, so there is no lifetime plumbing to hold every AST open for the whole link. And **a rename is auditable** — a list of ranges and the name at each, which a test can assert exactly.

**The soundness condition is that no reference site is missed**, since a missed one leaves a dangling name in a file nothing runs and no test covers. So the sites come from semantic analysis — resolved references to a root-scope binding, the same information an editor's rename refactor uses — rather than from a hand-written walk over the type syntax, which would silently miss the corner nobody enumerated. Verified against exactly those corners: `extends`, a mapped type's `keyof`, both branches of a conditional type. (`oxc`'s `SemanticBuilder` does resolve TypeScript type references, which was the load-bearing unknown; `with_build_nodes(true)` is required, because a `Reference` carries a `NodeId` and the node table is empty without it.)

**The public API is the entry's exports and nothing else.** A type reachable only *through* a public one is inlined without an `export` modifier: it has to be present or the public type is meaningless, but exporting it would widen the package's surface past what its author wrote. TypeScript's own rules are followed where they are surprising — an explicit export beats an `export *`, and a name two `export *`s both offer is *excluded* rather than arbitrated, because the same package built by tsc would not export it either.

**Refused rather than guessed:** a namespace import, `export * as ns`, `export =`, `declare module`, `declare global`. Each needs a synthesised namespace or reaches outside the file to mean what it meant, and a `.d.ts` that is wrong is *believed* — nothing runs it. The refusal names the construct and points at the per-module build, where it stands as written. This is the same principle as the isolated-declarations refusal above, applied one layer out.

**Verified against the two drivers, and against a consumer rather than only a compiler.** Postgres' 8 modules link into one 253-line declaration and Redis' 14 into one of 1269; both typecheck under `tsc --noEmit`, and consumer files resolve real types through them.
