Redis needed three renames, and the export block restores their public spellings (`MessageContext$1 as MessageContext`) — a package's surface must not change because two of its internal modules happened to agree on a name. A deliberately wrong consumer is still *rejected*, which is what proves the renames kept their identities rather than merely compiling. Plus 8 end-to-end tests and 9 unit tests, one per property.

**Consequences:** `esrun` is unchanged — same flags, same messages, same behaviour, verified by its existing 290-test suite passing untouched against the extracted core. `esdev` runs a module or a snippet with the whole flag vocabulary today, and 15 end-to-end tests assert *parity* rather than coverage: the capability model, both D38 rules, the single grammar rule, the flag-after-source rejection, the watchdog and the error block are checked to be the same ones `esrun` presents. Nothing new is published: `esdev` is deliberately absent from the release manifest until it has features worth shipping. The cost this accepts is a second binary for anyone who wants both — V8 dominates at ~63 MB stripped — which is the reason `esdev` stays development-only rather than becoming a superset of `esrun`.

---

### D58 — UDP in `runtime:net`: messages, not streams — and two grants, because a socket is both ends · *Proposed (2026-08-11)*

**Context:** `runtime:net` spoke TCP and nothing else. Everything the runtime could reach was therefore connection-oriented, which rules out a family of things a server does routinely and cannot do any other way: DNS resolution, StatsD/DogStatsD metrics, syslog, NTP, SNMP, QUIC-adjacent protocols, mDNS/SSDP discovery, game and telemetry protocols, and any local agent that listens on a datagram socket. There is no polyfill for it — a datagram is not a framing you can put on top of a stream, it is a different service from the OS — so the gap was total rather than inconvenient. Deno answers with `listenDatagram`, Node with `dgram`, Bun with `udpSocket`; no standards body has one, because the WinterTC Sockets proposal is explicitly TCP-shaped and the web platform has no UDP at all.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **A datagram socket is not a `Socket`, and does not pretend to be.** `bind()` returns a `DatagramSocket` with `send`/`receive` and an async iterator, not `readable`/`writable`. The reason is not aesthetic: a stream would have to invent a queue in front of the kernel's, and the two things UDP actually guarantees — that a message arrives whole or not at all, and that it says who sent it — are exactly what a byte stream erases. A `ReadableStream<{ data, address, port }>` was considered and rejected for the same reason in a subtler form: it reads like a stream, so a caller reaches for `pipeTo` and discovers that backpressure on a protocol with no flow control means silently dropping datagrams somewhere.
- **The shape is Deno's, near enough to port.** `bind({ hostname, port })`, a `{ data, address, port }` message, `addr`, `close()`. Deno yields `[Uint8Array, Deno.NetAddr]` tuples; a named object is used instead, because a two-element tuple whose second element is itself a two-field object is a shape you look up every time. **Rejected: Node's `dgram`** — `createSocket().on("message")` is event-based, and every other surface in this runtime is promise-based; a port of it would be the only API here where the answer arrives by callback.
- **`bind` requires `NetListen`; `send` requires `Net` — both, and separately.** A UDP socket is simultaneously a server and a client, so gating it on one grant would be a hole in whichever was chosen. Under `Net` alone a program could bind a port and receive inbound traffic, which is precisely what `NetListen` exists to gate; under `NetListen` alone it could reach any host on the internet, which is what `Net` exists to gate. The consequence is stated plainly rather than smoothed over: a program that only sends still needs `listen`, because it cannot send without holding a port that answers — an ephemeral source port is a port, and the reply that comes back to it is inbound traffic. **Rejected: a new `Udp` capability**, which would be a third vocabulary word for a question the existing two already answer, and would leave `--allow-net` and `--allow-listen` with nothing to say about datagrams.
- **The address lists apply per send, not per socket.** `--allow-listen` is checked at the bind and `--allow-net` at **every** `send` and `connect`, because the destination is an argument here rather than a property of the socket. A single check at bind time would have scoped nothing at all: one datagram socket sends to as many peers as it likes.
- **`connect()` is offered, and it is not a handshake.** Fixing a peer makes `send(data)` legal without an address and makes the OS discard datagrams from anyone else — the filtering half is what makes it more than a convenience, and it is the only way to get ICMP port-unreachable reported back at all. The docs say no packet is sent, so a `connect` succeeding proves nothing about the peer.
- **Socket options are set at bind; membership is not.** `reusePort`, `reuseAddress`, `broadcast`, `ttl`, `multicastTtl` and `multicastLoopback` are configuration a program decides once — and two of them (`SO_REUSEPORT`, `SO_REUSEADDR`) are meaningless after the bind, since they must be set between `socket()` and `bind()`. `joinMulticast`/`leaveMulticast` are methods, because a responder joins and leaves groups while it runs. Absent means the OS default rather than a number chosen here, so there is one copy of each default and it is the kernel's.
- **The v4/v6 split is made once, at the socket, and refuses rather than ignores.** IPv4 and IPv6 carry TTL, multicast TTL and multicast loopback under different socket options, and setting the wrong one is not an error any OS reports — it silently does nothing. So the address family decides which of each pair is set, an IPv6 socket asking for `broadcast` is an error (IPv6 has no broadcast at all), and an IPv6 membership naming its interface by address rather than by index is an error too.
- **No forwarding task and no channel.** Unlike a stream socket, a datagram socket is held as a shared `UdpSocket` and each `receive` awaits `recv_from` directly. A reader task exists for TCP because bytes arrive whether or not anyone is asking; the kernel's datagram queue already is that buffer, and a second one in front of it would only add a place for datagrams to be dropped that the program cannot see. What that costs is a close signal — a parked receive holds the socket alive itself — so `close()` sets a flag *and* rings a `Notify`, and the receive registers for the bell before it reads the flag, so a close landing between the two is seen by one or the other and never by neither.

**Amended (2026-08-11), after the first pass shipped.** Seven gaps were closed, and the reasoning for each is the same shape: a datagram API that is only *nearly* complete pushes the missing part into a workaround, and a workaround for a socket option is not a thing a program can write.

- **`ref()`/`unref()`, with the counter split out of the receive.** A parked `receive()` kept the process alive with no way to say otherwise, so a program that binds a socket and stops caring never exits. The receive op is now `.unref()`'d and a per-agent counter holds the loop open instead — exactly the split D48's `worker_recv` made, and for exactly its reason: a parked receive cannot be taken back, so if *it* were the reason the loop runs, an `unref()` would not take effect until a datagram arrived, and on the socket a program is unref'ing, none ever does. The counter is shared with workers rather than duplicated (`worker_refs` becomes `handle_refs`), because "referenced host handles keeping this agent alive" is one question. **Unlike Node, a bound socket with no receive in flight is not itself a reason to stay alive** — that matches our own `Listener`, and it is the answer that hangs less rather than more.
- **`sendMany` / `receiveMany`.** One crossing for many datagrams. What they save is the crossing and not the syscalls, and the docs say so: the OS still sees one send per datagram. `receiveMany` takes one datagram it waits for plus whatever is *already* queued behind it, and never waits to fill a batch — a batch that waits trades the first datagram's latency for throughput nobody asked for. Rejected: `sendmmsg`/`recvmmsg`, which would cut the syscalls too but are Linux-only and would make the same API mean different things on different platforms.
- **Source-specific multicast** (`joinMulticast(group, { source })`), IPv4 only. This is the one multicast feature that is a *security* feature: the filter is applied by the network, so an unwanted sender's traffic never arrives, which no amount of receiver-side checking can match. Left the same way the OS models it — a membership with a source is a different membership, not a filter hung off the plain one, so it must be left with the source named again.
- **Post-bind setters** for the five options that can change (`setTtl`, `setMulticastTtl`, `setBroadcast`, `setMulticastLoopback`, `setMulticastInterface`), and deliberately **not** for the three that cannot (`reusePort`, `reuseAddress`, `ipv6Only`), which must be set between `socket()` and `bind()`. A setter for those would be one that quietly did nothing, which is worse than not having it.
- **`setMulticastInterface`** closes the multi-homed gap named above: the outgoing interface for a multicast send was the OS's choice with no override.
- **`ipv6Only` at bind**, because the platform default differs — Linux usually accepts v4-mapped traffic on a v6 socket, the BSDs usually do not — and a program that needs one answer could not previously state it.
- **`datagram.truncated`.** A datagram that did not fit was silently a prefix. The receive buffer is now one byte *past* the largest datagram IPv4 can deliver, which makes "filled the buffer exactly" an observation that can only mean truncation, since no real IPv4 datagram is that long. Reported rather than fixed: the bytes are gone at the OS, and a program parsing a prefix as a whole message is the failure worth naming.

Also in this pass: receive buffers are **pooled per socket** rather than allocated per datagram (a 64 KiB allocation was most of the cost of receiving a small one; the buffer is borrowed for one receive and returned by a destructor, so an abandoned receive does not drain the pool), and the end-to-end multicast test now compiles two platform facts into itself rather than assuming Linux — `SO_REUSEPORT` where sharing a port needs it (the BSDs) and a tolerated "skipped" where loopback multicast delivery is not something a CI runner has. **Still not solved:** the round-trip cost of a promise per datagram (pooling the receive buffers took it from 34.9 µs to **30.9**, against Node's 28.8, and what is left is the promise itself — `receiveMany` does not help a strict request/response exchange, where there is only ever one datagram to take); source-specific multicast over IPv6; a receive rate limit; and everything in the original list below.

**Consequences:** DNS, StatsD, syslog, NTP, mDNS and game protocols become writable in guest code, and `runtime:db` gains the transport its remaining backends need. `NetProvider` grows six methods — **all defaulted to a refusal**, so an existing embedder's implementation still compiles and simply has no UDP, which is the honest answer for a provider that does not implement it. `socket2` moves from a Unix-only dependency of `default-providers` to an unconditional one; it is already in the tree via tokio and hyper-util, so the graph is unchanged (D7). **Not solved here:** QUIC and therefore HTTP/3, which needs congestion control and its own TLS integration rather than a socket (D42 states the same thing from the other side); source-address selection on a multi-homed host (`IP_MULTICAST_IF` is not exposed, so the OS chooses the outgoing interface for a multicast send); `IP_MULTICAST_ALL`, which on Linux means a socket bound to a port still receives a group **another process** on the host has joined; unix-domain datagram sockets; and a receive that reports truncation — a datagram longer than 65,507 bytes is truncated by the OS exactly as it is for any other receiver, and the count of dropped bytes is not something the API returns. `reusePort` is Unix-only and refused elsewhere rather than silently binding exclusively, matching the TCP listener. Verified by 9 bind-layer unit tests (the ephemeral bind, a port in use, a shared port that a plain bind is still refused, every option read back off the socket, the v6 spellings, IPv6 broadcast refused, a group joined and left, and both malformed-group refusals) and 11 provider tests over real loopback sockets — a round trip whose reply goes to a port nothing announced in advance, three sends arriving as three datagrams including a zero-length one, a connected socket sending without an address *and* discarding a stranger's datagram, an unconnected send with no destination refused, a parked receive ended by `close()`, a closed socket that neither sends nor receives, a multicast send reaching two members on one port and stopping once both leave, a destination outside `--allow-net` refused (as is `connect`, which is the same reach by another route), a bind outside `--allow-listen` refused before the port is claimed, and the two id namespaces not overlapping — plus 3 end-to-end CLI tests driving the real binary (the round trip, boundaries and iterator termination; `connect`, and five validation refusals in the `SocketError` shape; multicast with the socket options set) and 2 permission tests asserting each grant separately, since a socket that could send under `listen` alone would be a hole in `net`. Documented per D27 (`SPEC.md`, `API.md`, `types/runtime-net.d.ts`, site `api/net` + `docs/guides/networking`, `CHANGELOG`).

---

### D57 — `runtime:hashing`: the hashes WebCrypto has no name for, and no capability for any of them · *Proposed (2026-08-11)*

**Context:** `crypto.subtle` (D9) covers SHA-1/256/384/512, HMAC, HKDF and PBKDF2, and that is the WebCrypto standard's entire digest surface. Four things a server does daily fall outside it. It is **one-shot**: `subtle.digest` takes the whole input at once, so hashing a 4 GB upload means holding 4 GB, and there is no `createHash().update()` to reach for instead. It is **unencoded**: every call site ends in the same byte-by-byte loop to get hex, which allocates a string per byte to produce a string the host already had — our own migration guide teaches that loop, which is the clearest possible sign it belongs in the runtime. It has **no password hashing** beyond PBKDF2, which is the weakest of the four options and the one no current guidance recommends first. And it has **no non-cryptographic hashes**, so a cache key or an ETag pays for collision resistance against an adversary who does not exist. Node answers all four with `node:crypto`; leaving them out is what makes `node:crypto` hard to migrate off.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **One module, both kinds of hash, one API.** `hash(algorithm, data, encoding?)` takes SHA-1/2/3, BLAKE3, MD5, RIPEMD-160, xxHash64, XXH3 and CRC-32/32C. The split between cryptographic hashes and checksums is documented, not spelled into two namespaces — a caller who picks `crc32` for a password has not been saved by a longer import path. It is enforced at the one place it changes an outcome: `hmac` refuses the checksums, because a MAC built on CRC-32 is not a MAC. **Rejected: a separate `runtime:checksum`.** Two modules to answer "what is the hash of this" is a taxonomy, not an API.
- **No capability, for any of it.** Hashing reads nothing and reaches nothing; what `hash("sha256", x)` tells a caller is a fact about `x`, which they already hold. `runtime:serialization` is the precedent (D56 cites it for the same reason). So a runtime granted no authority at all can import this module and use every function in it.
- **Except the salt, which is randomness, and randomness is granted.** A password hash needs a fresh salt. Rather than let an op help itself to entropy — the thing D9 was careful not to do anywhere else — the **module draws the salt in JS from `crypto.getRandomValues`** and passes it into the op. Three things follow, all of them correct: `password.hash()` needs `Entropy` because it genuinely needs randomness; `password.verify()` needs **nothing**, because the salt is inside the stored string, so a service that only checks passwords is granted nothing; and every op here stays a pure function of its arguments.
- **Encoding is the host's job.** `hex` / `base64` / `base64url` are computed in Rust and returned as strings, with bytes as the default. This is the ergonomic half of the module's reason to exist and it is also the faster half.
- **Incremental is a first-class shape, not a fallback.** `new Hasher(algorithm)` holds native state across `update()` calls; `digest()` ends it, and a second call throws rather than quietly starting a second hash. `hashStream(algorithm, stream, encoding?)` is the same thing in the line it is usually wanted in — `await hashStream("sha256", request.body, "hex")`. The hasher registry is per-agent by construction (it is built inside `install`, which runs once per `Runtime`), so unlike the provider-backed handles of D50 there is no shared counter to defend against.
- **Argon2id, bcrypt and scrypt — and the parameters travel with the hash.** Output is a PHC string (or bcrypt's `$2b$`), so nothing else has to be stored beside it, and verification reads the algorithm and parameters from the string rather than from today's configuration. That is what makes raising the cost possible at all: old hashes keep verifying, and `needsRehash()` says which ones to replace at the one moment the plaintext is in hand. `bcrypt` is included because existing databases are full of it; `scrypt` because they are sometimes full of that.
- **bcrypt refuses a password it would have truncated.** bcrypt hashes at most 72 bytes including its own NUL, and nearly every implementation silently ignores the rest — quietly making two different passwords the same password. Hashing past 71 bytes is an error here. **Verification stays truncating**, deliberately: a stored hash may have been written by one of those implementations, and verification must compute what *that* implementation computed or the migration locks users out.
- **MD5 and SHA-1 ship plainly.** Both are broken for signatures and neither is a checksum. They are needed for interop — an S3 ETag, CRAM-MD5, a legacy protocol — and refusing them, or gating them behind an `{ insecure: true }` flag, only pushes that work somewhere with no documentation at all. The docs say what they are not for.

**Rejected:** offloading password hashing to `TaskSpawner`. Argon2 at the default cost burns ~50ms of CPU and blocks the isolate for all of it, and moving it off-thread is the obvious fix — but `TaskSpawn` is a capability, so it would make password hashing require authority that hashing does not need, and unusable under deny-by-default. It runs on the calling thread, documented as blocking, and the answer to a login endpoint under load is a queue in front of it. **Also rejected:** a `Hashing` capability (it would gate nothing that happens); BLAKE3 through `hmac` (BLAKE3 has its own keyed mode — nesting it inside HMAC would be the wrong primitive under the right name); and re-implementing what `crypto.subtle` already does well, which is why `hmac` documents `subtle` as the answer when a `CryptoKey` or a JWK is already in hand.

**Consequences:** eleven new dependencies, all RustCrypto or equivalently vetted. `sha3`, `md-5` and `ripemd` sit on the same `digest` 0.11 generation as `sha2`/`sha1`/`hmac`, so they add algorithms rather than a third hash tree — `sha3` is held at **0.11 rather than 0.12** because 0.12 implements its hashes outside `digest`'s block-level `CoreProxy`, which is exactly what `Hmac<H>` is generic over, so on 0.12 there would be no HMAC-SHA3 at all. `argon2` 0.5 and `scrypt` 0.11 are held to one `password-hash` generation so one parser reads every string we write, at the cost of duplicate `pbkdf2` 0.12 / `salsa20` 0.10 — warn-level, the same trade D9 already recorded for the EC crates. **Not solved here:** BLAKE3's keyed and derive-key modes, and its XOF (variable-length output); Argon2's optional secret ("pepper"); CRC-32 variants beyond the two shipped; and a way to hash a file without reading it through JS. Verified by 14 unit tests — a published vector for every one of the fifteen algorithms, incremental agreeing with one-shot for all of them across a 7-byte chunking, the RFC 2202/4231 HMAC vectors, every password algorithm round-tripped, and both bcrypt boundaries — plus 15 end-to-end CLI tests covering the module surface, the encodings, every input type including an offset view, agreement with `crypto.subtle` on the four shared digests, and the capability claim under `--deny-all`. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/runtime-hashing.d.ts`, site `api/hashing` + the migration guide, `CHANGELOG`).

---

### D56 — `runtime:db`: a driver kit in JS, transport in Rust, and no new authority · *Locked (maintainer sign-off, 2026-08-10)*

**Context:** a server runtime without a database story sends every user to a driver written for someone else's I/O model. The peers split two ways: Bun ships a driver per backend as runtime API, Node and Deno leave it to npm. Neither gives a third party the fast path — an npm driver reaches the socket through a compatibility layer, and a built-in driver's internals are not exported, so the second backend starts from nothing. The audit that opened this work also found that the transport half was already built: D28's `net_start_tls` is the mid-connection upgrade libpq's `SSLRequest` needs, and `SystemNet`'s reader already fills a 64 KB buffer per `net_read`. What was missing was not the plumbing but the substrate above it.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **Rust owns transport and the embedded engine. JS owns every protocol.** No wire-protocol codec goes below the op boundary, ever. The test is stated as a rule rather than an aspiration: **adding MySQL, Redis, or any other socket backend must require zero new Rust.** A patch to `crates/` in a protocol phase is a design failure, not a tradeoff. The Postgres backend is the first proof — it needs no new Rust at all, because `net_connect`/`net_start_tls`/`net_read`/`net_write` already do what it asks for.

- **No new capability.** `runtime:db` reaches nowhere `runtime:net` and `runtime:fs` cannot already reach; it composes existing authority rather than adding any. Wire backends need `Net`, scoped by `--allow-net=db.internal:5432`; `sqlite:` needs `FileRead`, plus `FileWrite` for a read-write open, scoped by the root jail (D25) and the path allowlists (D38). **Rejected: a `Db` capability.** It would need its own scope grammar and would be strictly *coarser* than host-allowlisting already is — "may talk to a database" says less than "may reach this host on this port". A capability marks authority; a module that only composes authority does not get one.

- **Pooling stays in JS.** A provider-side pool is implementable and protocol-blind, and it is still the wrong place: it *adds* op crossings to the hot path. A JS pool serves a cache hit in **zero** ops and a release in **zero**; a Rust pool costs one async op for each, on a query whose whole cost is a write and a read. The document that asks to amortize the crossing cannot then add two. The deeper reason is that the pool's interesting decision is not "is a socket free" but **prepared-statement affinity** — route this query to the connection that already prepared it — which is protocol state and lives in JS regardless, so a Rust pool would be a second bookkeeping of a set JS already keeps. Two things only a provider-side pool could do are noted and **not** taken: sharing connections across agents, which is exactly what D50 forbids and would need its own decision rather than arriving as a side effect; and a deployment-enforced connection cap, which belongs on `Net` generally if it is ever wanted, not on `db`.

- **One row-batch format for every backend.** Rows cross as **one byte buffer per batch in Postgres `DataRow` layout** — per column an `int32` length (`-1` for NULL) then raw bytes — with column descriptors emitted once per cursor. Never as `Value::Array` of `Value::Object`: structural marshaling is recursive and per-value, which is the cost this design exists to avoid. Postgres already sends this layout; the embedded engine is made to emit it. So the decode layer — a per-query accessor class generated from the descriptors, prototype getters, `DataView` numerics, `TextDecoder` per column only when the column is read, a fresh buffer per batch — is written **once** and is the hot path for native and third-party backends alike. A MySQL driver transcodes into the layout and inherits it. **No `Proxy` for lazy rows**: it is a known V8 deopt, and a generated class keeps shapes monomorphic. **No buffer reuse**: it silently corrupts any row retained past its batch; an opt-in fast path with documented invalidation may come later, never the default.

- **`runtime:db` is one module with two documented tiers**, not a subpath. `connect`, `sql`, `Connection`, `Statement`, `Rows`, `Transaction`, `DbError` are the application tier; `registerBackend`, `defineRowShape`, `decodeBatch`, `TypeRegistry`, `Dialect`, `mapError`, `BaseConnection`/`BaseRows`, `Pool`, `FrameReader`, `ByteWriter` are the driver tier. **Rejected: `runtime:db/driver`.** It would keep the application surface smaller and let the kit stabilize separately, but no `runtime:` module has subpaths and the namespace precedent costs more than the tidiness buys.
- **A kit primitive ships when a backend proves it.** Everything above is public API from the moment it exports, so nothing exports on speculation: the registry, row shapes, type registry, dialect, error mapping and base classes ship with `sqlite:`, which exercises them; `Pool`, `FrameReader` and `ByteWriter` wait for Postgres, their first consumer. This is SPEC §6's no-stubs rule applied to an API surface instead of a crate.
- **A conformance suite is part of the kit.** `runBackendConformance(factory)` is exported so a third-party driver can prove it behaves like the built-ins. An ecosystem where ORMs can rely on cross-driver behaviour needs drivers able to demonstrate it, not merely intend it.

- **`sqlite:` names a file format and a dialect, the way `postgres://` names a wire protocol.** It is implemented by `turso_core`, and **`turso` appears nowhere in the public API** — not the scheme, not an export, not an option — so the implementation is swappable without an API break. `otfdb:` is reserved for the OpenTF engine: userland cannot claim it, and neither can it re-register a built-in.

- **The engine gets a jailed VFS, not the filesystem.** `turso_core::IO` is a real seam, and **every** file the engine opens — the database and its `-wal`/`-shm` siblings — passes through our `open_file`. So authorization happens at a choke point we own, against the same jail and allowlists `runtime:fs` uses, and no sidecar escapes because no filename had to be guessed. This is why the dependency is `turso_core` with `default-features = false` (its own `fs` feature dropped, since we supply the IO) rather than the `turso` wrapper, which pulls `mimalloc` and `tantivy`. Engine work is blocking and runs on the `TaskSpawner` seam.

- **The encryption key is an option, never a URL parameter.** `connect("sqlite:./app.db", { key })`. Keys in URLs leak into logs, error messages and stack traces; a key supplied as a URL parameter is **refused with an explicit error** rather than quietly honoured.

- **`query(q: string | QueryAst, params?)` is the contract from day one.** The OpenTF engine will take a MessagePack-encoded AST rather than SQL text, and a string-only signature could never admit it as a first-class backend. The AST rides `runtime:serialization`'s existing MessagePack — there is no second encoder. Backends that accept only one form reject the other by name (`ERR_DB_QUERY_FORM`).

- **An in-memory database needs no capability** (*amended 2026-08-09*). `sqlite::memory:` names no file, reads none and writes none, so `FileRead`/`FileWrite` would gate nothing that happens, and a grant that guards an unrelated operation teaches people to hand out grants. What it costs is memory, which guest JS can already spend without asking, so it is not new authority either — `runtime:serialization` is the precedent for a capability-free surface. It is a **third op that takes no path**, rather than the existing one with a flag: an ungated op that accepted a path would be a way to open any database on disk without `FileRead`, and the distance between "ignores the argument" and "stops ignoring it after a refactor" is one careless edit. Named in-memory databases (`:memory:name`) are refused, because SQLite's named form exists to *share* one between connections and nothing here shares.

  Found the hard way, and worth writing down: **the engine chooses its storage from the `IO` it is handed and from nothing else.** `Database::open` uses the IO given it; the `:memory:` → memory-storage mapping lives in a convenience constructor no embedder supplying its own VFS can use. Passing the jailed VFS with a `:memory:` path therefore produces a *file called `:memory:`* while `is_in_memory_db()` reports true — the two disagree and nothing errors. The dispatch belongs to us, and every future custom-VFS embedder inherits the same obligation.

- **The engine's scratch space is memory** (*amended 2026-08-09*). Some statements ask the engine for a temp file, and it takes one from the OS temp directory — outside the root jail by construction, so the VFS refuses it and the statement fails. `TempStore::Memory` is set on every connection, which is the same answer `runtime:fs` already gives by refusing to put its temp files in the OS temp directory (D25). Redirecting the path into the jail was rejected: it means recognising the engine's own temp naming, and it leaves scratch files in the user's project. The cost is stated rather than hidden — work that would have spilled to disk is now bounded by memory. **Found by the conformance suite on its first run**, which is the argument for shipping one.

**What the implementation showed** (*added on locking, 2026-08-10*). The central
claim held: `@opentf/esrun-postgres` speaks the wire protocol in JavaScript over
`runtime:net` and added **no Rust to the runtime**, and it passes the same
conformance suite `sqlite:` does. The shared row layout paid for itself — a
Postgres `DataRow` frame *is* the encoding the decoder reads, so rows are copied
into a batch as they arrive and never transcoded.

Two of the acceptance criteria were met and one was not, and the reason is worth
keeping. Text-heavy rows were to tie and win by 1.2×; memory is lowest of the
four runtimes on every non-streaming workload. Numeric-heavy rows were to "win by
a wide margin" and won by 7% — until binary result formats, which took the scan
from 72 ms to 51.6 ms (1.46× `postgres.js`). The measurement that decided it:
**decoding was 54% of a numeric scan**, and 8% afterwards.

The lesson underneath it is the one to carry forward. A prepared-statement cache
bought 6%, from which the wrong conclusion was drawn — that the round trip
dominates, so decoding was not worth attacking. A round trip is a **fixed cost
per query**: the whole cost of a point query, and negligible against ten thousand
rows, where decoding is what scales. Both numbers were right; generalising one to
the other's shape was not.

Three things this decision did not anticipate and which the work added: a
connection is one conversation, so a query issued during a streaming result is
refused rather than queued (`ERR_DB_CONNECTION_BUSY`) — queueing would deadlock,
since the open result only finishes when the caller drains it. `Pool` shipped
into the driver tier as planned, with `release(clean)` defaulting to **false**.
And `AbortSignal` became portable rather than driver-local, because `turso`
exposes an interrupt: `sqlite:` stops a statement mid-flight, so cancellation
means the same thing on both backends instead of something weaker on one.

**What the second backend showed** (*added 2026-08-10*). Redis was named in this
decision's own test — "adding MySQL, Redis, or any other socket backend must
require zero new Rust" — and `@opentf/esrun-redis` met it: RESP2/RESP3 over
`runtime:net`, TLS, pooling, no Rust. That much was expected. What it found is
that the *other* claim in this decision was a declaration rather than a fact.

`query(q: string | QueryAst)` was written into the contract "from day one" so
that "an engine which never speaks SQL can be a first-class backend rather than
a special case". The signature was there and the door was not: `normalizeQuery`
threw `ERR_DB_QUERY_FORM` for every AST unconditionally, so the only way to
accept one was to override `query`/`execute` wholesale and lose the signal
handling and closed-connection check with them. A contract nothing had exercised
had quietly become a contract nothing *could* exercise. **The lesson is narrower
than "test more": an extension point with no consumer is a comment.** The AST
form should have shipped with a backend using it, exactly as `Pool`,
`FrameReader` and `ByteWriter` were made to wait for Postgres by this decision's
own no-stubs rule — the rule was applied to the driver-tier exports and not to
the query form, and that is the inconsistency that cost the time.

Two further places had assumed every backend was a SQL backend, both invisible
until something was not one. `transaction()` wrote `BEGIN`/`COMMIT`/`ROLLBACK`
into itself, so a key-value store inheriting it would have sent a `BEGIN` to a
server that has never heard of one; and `executeMany` passed `_executeMany` only
the query's *text*, which is `null` for an AST backend. Both are now declared
capabilities (`supports.sqlText`, `supports.queryAst`, `supports.transactions`)
with overridable seams behind them.

The conformance suite had the same assumption and it mattered most there.
Fifteen checks written in SQL DDL, run against a backend with no SQL, is not a
demanding suite — it is fifteen guaranteed failures, which would have made the
report worthless precisely for the backend that most needed checking. A check
now declares whether it needs SQL, and one a backend cannot express is skipped
**with a reason** rather than failed. The reason is reported rather than merely
counted, because "13 skipped" with no explanation is how a driver author
concludes they passed something they never ran.

Where Redis deliberately does *not* follow Postgres is also worth recording,
since both answers are correct and the difference is the protocol. A second
command issued while a result is open is **refused** on Postgres and **queued**
on Redis: there, an open result set finishes only when the caller drains it, so
queueing deadlocks; here, a RESP reply is complete once read, so every exchange
finishes on its own and `rows.exhausted` is always true. The portable
`ERR_DB_CONNECTION_BUSY` is a constraint every wire protocol *can* have, not one
they all do — and a driver that copied Postgres's refusal here would have been
refusing something safe.

**The tier list above is the plan, not the inventory** (*noted 2026-08-11*).
Five names in it were never built and are not exported: `Statement` and
`Transaction` (statements are prepared inside a driver and a transaction is a
callback, so neither became a type), `TypeRegistry` (each driver owns its own
type mapping), `BaseRows` (`Rows` needed no base class once the row layout was
shared) and `FrameReader` (Postgres has one, and it is Postgres's — nothing
else wanted it). `registerBackend` is gone, replaced by `defineDriver` below.
What actually ships is: `connect`, `sql`, `queryAst`, `sqlite`, `Query`,
`Rows`, `DbError`, `DbErrorCode` in the application tier; `defineDriver`,
`Driver`, `BaseConnection`, `PooledConnection`, `Pool`, `Dialect`,
`defineRowShape`, `defineRecordShape`, `decodeBatch`, `encodeParams`,
`encodeParamSets`, `splitParams`, `ByteWriter`, `mapError`, `asDbError` and
`runBackendConformance` in the driver tier. The list is left as it was written
because a decision records what was decided; this note records what was true.

**What the backend that does not exist showed: rows are not always bytes**
(*amended 2026-08-11*). The row layout above is stated as though every backend
has bytes to hand over, because both backends that existed when it was written
did. A backend whose values are already JavaScript — a document store answering
JSON, a graph or vector service over HTTP, an in-process engine holding objects
— has nothing to decode, and the layout made it *encode* its values so that
`decodeBatch` could immediately take them apart again.

This was not hypothetical by the time it was found: `@opentf/esrun-redis` was
doing exactly that. A RESP reply is fully in memory and already JavaScript, and
the driver packed it into `DataRow` frames — duplicating the kit's four value
tags into the package to do it — for the sole purpose of satisfying `Rows`. So
a `RowSource` may now answer `{ records, done }` instead of `{ bytes, rows,
done }`, paired with `defineRecordShape`; `Rows.fromObjects` is the one-line
form. The byte layout is unchanged and is still the right answer for every
backend that was handed bytes, which is every wire protocol.

Batching survived the move deliberately. It buys nothing on the wire for a
reply already in memory, but it is what keeps `LRANGE 0 -1` followed by a
`break` from building ten million row objects first — the same reason it
existed on the byte path, which the first records implementation lost and a
test now pins.

**One concept, two drivers, two vocabularies** (*amended 2026-08-11*).
`LISTEN`/`NOTIFY` and pub/sub are the same arrangement — a connection given
over to delivering messages the server pushes — and the two drivers had
invented `listen`/`onNotification`/`listening`/`onListenError` and
`subscribe`/`onMessage`/`subscribed`/`onSubscribeError` for it independently.
Neither was wrong; having both was. The portable surface is now
`subscribe`/`unsubscribe`/`subscribed`/`subscriptions`/`onMessage`/
`onSubscribeError`, declared by `supports.subscriptions`, with `_subscribe` and
`_unsubscribe` as the driver seam.

The rule this followed is D56's own: a kit primitive ships when a backend
proves it. Two backends had proved this one and the proof had been read as "two
drivers, each with a feature" rather than as "one feature, spelled twice" —
which is what a driver tier exists to catch. **The generalisable form: when the
second consumer of a concept arrives, the question is not whether it works but
whether it agrees.**

Three refusals fall out of it, and all three are by name rather than by
absence. A pooled connection cannot subscribe, because a subscription needs a
connection that does not come back. A cluster cannot, because Redis pub/sub is
not cluster-aware and a cluster-wide subscribe would deliver some messages and
silently drop others. A backend with no subscriptions says so in `supports`.

Four smaller corrections went with it. `supports.sqlText` became
`supports.queryText` — the flag means "takes text", and a backend speaking
Cypher takes text without being a SQL backend, which the old name could not
express. `executeMany` now reports per parameter set, because the default batch
path had each result in its hand and discarded them, leaving a batch of inserts
against a key-generating backend with no way to learn its keys. `Row` and
`Rows` became generic, since `DbOutput` describes the built-in backend and the
type claimed it described all of them while Postgres was already returning
`Temporal` values. And `ERR_DB_THROTTLED` and `ERR_DB_NOT_FOUND` were added
with mappings from both shipped backends rather than on the strength of
backends that do not exist yet.

**Two more asymmetries, found the same way.****Two more asymmetries, found the same way.** `withConnection` existed on a
pool and not on a connection; `usable` and `reusable` on a connection and not
on a pool. Each is meaningless on the other *as an implementation* and
essential on both *as a contract*, because otherwise code holding "a
connection" must know which kind it holds — an ORM would have to demand a pool
or write itself twice. Both are now on both, and on the portable `Connection`
type. A client with no single session to lend refuses `withConnection` by name,
which is the same shape as a backend refusing `transaction`. The conformance
suite checks all three, and it checks them on backends with no SQL, where the
type system is not doing the work.

**What the third driver showed: a scheme is a global, and a driver is a value****What the third driver showed: a scheme is a global, and a driver is a value**
(*amended 2026-08-10*). `registerBackend(scheme, factory)` and `backendSchemes()`
are removed. `connect(url, options)` now **requires** `options.driver`, and the
built-in SQLite backend is `sqlite`, an ordinary driver defined with the same
`defineDriver` a third party uses.

The registry was the last piece of this design that worked by ambient state.
Which backends existed depended on which modules had been evaluated, so the
dependency was invisible at the call site and order-sensitive; a scheme was a
global name claimed by whoever imported first, which is why the decision above
had to reserve `otfdb:` and forbid replacing a built-in — both of those rules
exist only because the namespace was shared, and both are gone with it. Two
implementations of `postgres:` — a fork, a fake for a test, a migration between
two — could not coexist. As a value, a driver needs none of it.

The consequence that mattered more than the tidiness: **because the driver is
part of the call, `connect` can return that driver's connection**, and the
second entry point every driver had grown was unnecessary. Redis had shipped
`Redis`, `createClient`, `createSubscriber`, `createPool`, `createCluster`,
`createSentinelClient` and `createSentinelPool` — seven ways in, because the
registry's `connect` could only promise the portable `Connection` and Redis's
own vocabulary had to be reached some other way. The command surface now sits on
`RedisConnection` itself, one object answers both vocabularies, and the package
exports three drivers instead. Postgres lost its own `connect`/`createPool` the
same way.

Pooling followed. It is now `pool: true` on the same call, served by one
`PooledConnection` in the driver tier; `PgPool` and `RedisPool` had each written
out the same borrow-per-call wrapper, the same "return the connection when a
streaming result ends", and their own answer to what a pooled `query` returns.
The one question a protocol-blind `Pool` cannot decide is now asked by one name
on every backend — `reusable`, beside `usable` — where there had been three
spellings (`status === "I"`, `clean`, and nothing at all on `sqlite:`).

**The generalisable form of it:** the extension point that is a *value* the
caller passes beats the one that is a *name* the callee looks up, whenever both
are possible. It is visible where it is used, it types, it composes, and it
needs no registry, no reservation and no rule about who may replace what.

**Also rejected:** a `node:sqlite` compatibility layer, or Node compatibility of any kind; an ORM or query builder in the runtime — the kit exists so those are written *on* the runtime, not *in* it. **Rejected for Redis specifically:** presenting `MULTI`/`EXEC` as `transaction(fn)` — it queues commands and applies them together, but does not roll back one that fails at `EXEC` time, so the helper would commit half a body that threw; a backend saying it has no transactions is more useful than one whose transactions silently are not.

**Consequences:** `providers` gains an `EmbeddedDb` trait and `default-providers` its `turso_core` implementation; that is the whole of the Rust surface, and it does not grow when backends do. The dependency is pinned at a **pre-release** (`turso_core` 0.8.0-pre.3, MIT) — accepted deliberately, since it is the only reliable Rust implementation and the `sqlite:` scheme insulates callers from the swap, but 0.x churn is a real maintenance cost until it stabilizes. `EncryptionOpts` takes its key as a hex `String`, so key material transits a non-zeroizable Rust allocation; documented in SECURITY.md rather than papered over. Requesting binary result formats from Postgres will make that driver **extended-protocol-only** — result format codes live in `Bind`, and the simple query protocol cannot ask — which costs multi-statement query strings; the restriction is decided here rather than discovered in the phase that hits it. Benchmarks cannot reuse `bench/run.sh`'s one-script-per-runtime shape, since esrun uses `runtime:db` while the comparison uses `postgres.js`; a `bench/db/` with per-runtime scripts against a shared workload and a documented Postgres container is part of the Postgres phase. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/runtime-db.d.ts`, site `api/db` + `docs/internals/database`, `CHANGELOG`).

---

### D55 — A WebSocket is an HTTP request that changed its mind · *Proposed (2026-08-08)*

**Context:** D29 gave `runtime:websocket` a `serve()` that binds a listener of its own, and D47 recorded what it could not do: "`wss:`, which waits on RFC 8441 extended CONNECT". That note conflated two things. RFC 8441 is WebSocket over **HTTP/2**; a plain `wss:` server is TLS plus the ordinary HTTP/1.1 `Upgrade`, and nothing was waiting on an RFC. What was actually missing was a way to reach a WebSocket through the HTTP server — which is also how a service gets one port and one certificate for both protocols. Every peer does it that way and none has a dedicated WebSocket listener: Node attaches `ws` to an `https` server's `upgrade` event, Deno has `Deno.upgradeWebSocket(req)` inside the handler, Bun has `server.upgrade(req)`. esrun was the outlier.

**Decision:** `upgradeWebSocket(request)` in `runtime:websocket`, returning `{ response, socket }`. The dedicated-port `serve()` stays.

- **In `runtime:websocket`, not `runtime:http`.** It mints a `WebSocketConnection` and that class lives there, so an upgraded socket is the *same type* a `serve()` accept yields and `broadcast()` reaches both without a second kind of connection. A server doing both imports from both modules, which is honest about the two things it is doing.
- **Joined in the ops layer, not at either seam.** `HttpServerProvider::upgrade` surrenders the connection and `WebSocketProvider::adopt` takes it; the runtime is what puts them together. The alternative — the HTTP provider returning a *WebSocket connection id* — needs no new type but produces an id that means nothing unless that provider was wired to the very WebSocket provider the runtime is using. The coupling would move out of the signature and into a footgun.
- **`futures-io` in the trait crate.** The upgraded stream crosses as `Box<dyn AsyncRead + AsyncWrite + Send + Unpin>` spelled in `futures_io`, which is a no_std-able trait crate and the fourth dependency `providers` has ever had. Spelling it in tokio's traits would put a runtime in the crate whose whole job is to not name one; `tokio_util::compat` bridges in `default-providers`, where tokio already lives.
- **The socket's id is a promise.** hyper resolves a takeover only *after* the `101` is written, so a handler must return the response before there is a connection at all — awaiting first would deadlock on a response the function has not returned yet. `WebSocketConnection` therefore holds a promised id, exactly as `runtime:net`'s `Socket` does for a dialled connection, and sends before it settles queue.
- **The handshake headers are the host's.** `Sec-WebSocket-Accept` is a SHA-1 of a key the guest never sees; a digest nobody can get wrong beats one everybody has to. The `101` itself needs a privileged construction too, since Fetch bounds `new Response` to 200–599 — an informational status is something a server emits, not something a script builds.
- **Upgrading is a *use* of the request, so it is checked as one.** `ws_upgrade` consults the same per-agent registry `http_ops` records request ids in (D50), which meant lifting that registry into `builtins` so both modules share it. Without it a worker could take a connection it was never handed — and more quietly than answering the request, since the parent's handler would simply never see its socket.
- **An upgraded connection is not counted twice.** It has no server permit and no per-peer slot: the HTTP server that accepted it already spent both, and charging it again would make one connection consume two budgets.

**Consequences:** `wss:` works today. `HttpServerProvider` and `WebSocketProvider` each gain one defaulted method, so an existing implementor keeps compiling and simply refuses to upgrade. Two bugs surfaced in testing and are worth recording, because both were silent rather than loud. First, hyper resolves an `OnUpgrade` only on a connection polled with `serve_connection_with_upgrades` — with the plain call the `101` went out correctly and the socket then said nothing, forever. Second, the accept key and the upgrade handle were held in one map, and whichever of `respond` and `upgrade` ran first took the other's half with it; the handshake went out without its three headers and every client hung. They are separate maps now. **Not solved here:** RFC 8441, so over TLS a client must negotiate `http/1.1` — browsers do for `wss:`, and a client forcing `h2` receives whatever the handler returns for a non-upgrade request; ~~and subprotocol negotiation~~ — **done in the same cycle**: `upgradeWebSocket(request, { protocol })` answers one the client offered, refusing any other at the call rather than letting the client reject the handshake, and `connection.protocol` reports the result on `serve()`-accepted connections as well (the provider had always reported it; the prelude was discarding it). Verified by an end-to-end test under the real binary (an ordinary request and an upgrade on one port, `broadcast()` across two upgraded sockets, and a hand-built `Request` refused), a worker test that a foreign request cannot be upgraded, and a live `wss:` handshake whose `Sec-WebSocket-Accept` matches RFC 6455 §1.3's own test vector. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/`, site `api/websocket` + `docs/guides/websocket` + `docs/internals/websockets`, `CHANGELOG`).

### D54 — A path named on the command line is part of the run · *Proposed (2026-08-08)* · *amends D25, D38*

**Context:** D25 jailed every filesystem access to the detected project root, and D38 added `--allow-read`/`--allow-write` scope lists that narrow within it — "a list narrows the jail and never widens it". Serving HTTPS then turned out to be impossible. D31 has the cert and key travel **inline** rather than as paths, precisely so that reading a file stays the filesystem's privilege and serving needs no grant beyond `NetListen` — which means the *guest* reads the PEM, through the jail. Certificates live in `/etc/letsencrypt/live/…`, where the renewal process writes them; no project root contains that, and copying them in defeats renewal. The HTTPS examples in `API.md` and on the site were therefore code that could not run, which a five-line reproduction confirmed:

```
REFUSED ERR_JAIL_ESCAPE - path /etc/ssl/certs/ca-certificates.crt escapes the filesystem root jail
```

The day before, the same reasoning had been taken one step further and an out-of-jail entry made an *argument error* — closing the last door while making the message clearer.

**Decision:** inside the jail a path list narrows, as before. **Outside it, a named path adds that subtree.**

- **The jail is the boundary a *guest* cannot move; the command line is a different party.** Nothing a program writes — a path, a symlink, a `..`, an `--import-policy` entry — widens anything. What widens is a string typed at launch by whoever deployed the service, which is the same authority that already picks `--allow-run=/usr/bin/git` and `--env-file`. Reading it as "the sandbox is now advisory" would be true only if guest code could reach the flag; it cannot.
- **Per access kind, not per run.** `read_roots` and `write_roots` are separate, so `--allow-read=/etc/letsencrypt` does not make the certificate replaceable. Two flags were always two grants; this keeps them two.
- **Module resolution is untouched.** The loader detects its own root and never consults these lists, so a granted path makes bytes readable, not code importable — otherwise `--allow-read` would quietly be a way to run anything on the disk. Pinned by a test rather than left to the structure.
- **Rejected: a `--tls-cert`/`--tls-key` flag pair** reading the PEM before the jail exists. It is the tighter answer for certificates specifically — the guest never sees the bytes — but it solves one case and leaves every other file outside the project (a CA bundle, a read-only dataset, a socket path) exactly as stuck, and it puts TLS material on a different footing from every other input.
- **Rejected: leaving it.** The status quo required a wrapper around every HTTPS deployment, and the documented examples stayed wrong.
- **A root is still not a target.** `reject_root_mutation` now applies to every root, so removing a granted directory out from under its own grant is refused exactly as removing the jail is.

**Consequences:** `confine` takes a root *set* rather than one root, and both filesystems carry `read_roots`/`write_roots` (the jail first, so it is the root an error names — and the message now lists the granted paths too, since a run that still cannot reach a file should see what the grant it was given did cover). The argument error added the previous day is withdrawn, and the `--help` hint goes back to `--allow-read=./data,/etc/ssl/certs`, which now works. **Not solved here:** a path grant is a subtree, so `--allow-read=/etc/letsencrypt/live/example.com` is the tightest spelling available — there is no single-file grant; and nothing narrows *what within* a granted subtree may be read, which is what the scope list already does inside the jail. Verified by 2 provider tests (a named outside path readable; a sibling of it still `ERR_JAIL_ESCAPE`, and a read grant not conferring write) and 3 end-to-end CLI tests (the granted path reads while `/etc/hostname` stays refused, a read grant does not become writable, and a granted path is readable but not importable), plus a real HTTPS server started against a certificate outside the project root — the case that could not work before. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/`, site `docs/security` + `docs/guides/securing-runtime` + `api/cli` + `docs/internals/filesystem`, `CHANGELOG`).

### D53 — A send queue a sender can see, and a bound for when nobody looks · *Proposed (2026-08-08)*

**Context:** D47 named the last of the three gaps it left: "backpressure a sender can feel, since `send()` is fire-and-forget by design and writing faster than a peer reads accumulates pending host-side sends with nothing bounding the total". The command channel to a connection's actor holds 16 messages; past that each `ws_send` parks holding its payload, and the guest never learns, because `send()` returns immediately and the WebSocket API has no way to say "full". A fan-out to a room where one peer has stopped reading therefore grows without limit — a memory leak with a network interface, driven by a remote party.

**Decision:** two halves, because the problem has two. **`connection.bufferedAmount`** is what a sender can feel; **`maxBufferedAmount`** (8 MiB, on by default) is what happens when nobody looks — a connection past it is closed with `1013`.

- **`bufferedAmount` rather than a new mechanism.** It is the IDL's own answer to this question, the client `WebSocket` already had it, and a server connection not having it was the actual omission. Nothing new to learn, and code written against a browser reads across.
- **On by default, unlike the connection caps.** D45's reasoning for `None` was that the right number follows from a deployment's descriptor budget, which this crate cannot read. That does not transfer: a queue this deep is a peer several messages behind on a large payload or thousands behind on a small one, and no application intends one. What a default risks here is closing a connection that would have recovered; what its absence risks is the process. So it ships on, generously.
- **Closed, not throttled, and not thrown.** `send()` cannot report a full buffer without breaking its contract, and blocking the guest on a remote party's read rate is worse than dropping that party. Closing is the only outcome that ends the spending, and `1013` (Try Again Later) says what happened — the server is not refusing the client, it is refusing to hold more for it.
- **Reserved before the message reaches the channel.** The parked sends *are* the thing being bounded, so counting only what the 16-slot channel accepted would count none of them. `fetch_update` rather than load-then-add, or two concurrent sends both reading a value under the bound would both be admitted and the bound would be a suggestion.
- **Signalled out of band.** The overflow is announced on a dedicated `Notify` rather than as a `Cmd`, because the command channel is precisely what is full at that moment — a close queued behind the backlog it exists to end would park with everything else.
- **Per connection, including in a broadcast.** A fan-out is a list of sends, so the peer that fell behind is the one closed for it and the rest of the room is untouched — the same reading D50 applied to `ws_broadcast`'s ownership checks.

**Consequences:** `WsServeOptions` gains `max_buffered_amount` (the fourth break to it this cycle; pre-1.0 per SPEC §14) and a `DEFAULT_MAX_BUFFERED_AMOUNT` associated const, so the number lives in the trait crate and the prelude sends "unset" — one copy to keep true, as `WsTimeouts::default()` already arranged. Client connections get the default too, since no server's options apply to one and a slow server is the same problem from the other end. **Not solved here:** a `send()` that can *wait* — a promise a guest could await for room — which would be a truer backpressure primitive but is not what the WebSocket API is; and any pacing of `broadcast()` as a whole, which stays as fast as its slowest admitted peer. A bug surfaced while writing the tests and is worth recording: the first implementation signalled with `notify_waiters()`, which wakes only *currently registered* waiters — and the moment a queue overflows is exactly the moment the actor is busy draining rather than parked, so the close was silently never sent. `notify_one()` leaves a permit behind and is correct. Verified by 2 provider tests over real sockets — a peer that completes the handshake and then reads nothing, written to concurrently until it is closed, and a peer that drains sixteen times the bound without ever being closed — plus 3 runtime-layer tests pinning the crossing (the default arriving as the provider's own constant, `0` meaning off rather than unset, and unusable values rejected before the bind). The first was checked to fail without the fix, and did — it is what caught the `notify_waiters` bug. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/`, site `api/websocket` + `docs/guides/websocket` + `docs/internals/websockets`, `CHANGELOG`).

### D52 — A connection cap that says whose connections they are · *Proposed (2026-08-08)*

**Context:** D45 capped an HTTP server's concurrent connections and D47 did the same for WebSockets, and both recorded the same hole in the same words: "one peer can still take every slot — it needs a peer identity plus a policy". A cap on the total bounds what the deployment *spends* and says nothing about who spends it, so a single client reaches it exactly as a thousand clients do, and the server is then full for everybody. D44 added the peer address; this is the policy that applies it.

**Decision:** `maxConnectionsPerIp` on both servers — the most connections one peer address may hold at once — unlimited by default.

- **Refused, not held.** This is the one place the two caps deliberately differ, and the difference is the whole point. The whole-server cap makes an excess connection *wait*, because the excess is legitimate traffic that will be served as soon as a slot frees, and waiting in the kernel backlog costs the server nothing. A per-peer excess is by definition one client past its own share, and holding it would be the hold the cap exists to prevent: the connection is already accepted, so it costs a descriptor, and the peer decides when it ends. Closing returns both immediately.
- **Taken after `accept`, which is the only place it can be taken.** The whole-server cap is taken *before*, so nothing is spent on a connection the server will not serve — the better position, and unavailable here, because the peer is not known until the connection exists. What is spent is bounded instead: the refusal happens before the nodelay, the span, the task, and the TLS or WebSocket handshake.
- **Off by default, for a sharper reason than the total is.** The count is per address, so everything behind one NAT or one load balancer shares a budget — with a proxy in front, every connection has the same source and a cap here caps the whole service. That is a footgun a default cannot avoid, so the number belongs to whoever knows what sits in front of the server, and the docs say so where it will be read.
- **An address at zero is forgotten, not kept at zero.** The keys come from whoever connects, so a table that only ever grew would be its own slow exhaustion under a botnet — the resource this exists to bound, reintroduced one level down. A refused connection leaves no entry either.

**Consequences:** `HttpServeOptions` and `WsServeOptions` each gain `max_connections_per_ip`, which breaks an embedder's construction of them (pre-1.0 and unstable per SPEC §14 — the same break D43/D45/D47/D51 made). The two servers share one `peer_limit` module rather than counting separately, so the policy cannot drift between them. **Not solved here:** a cap shared across several servers in one process, which is still per `serve()`; a rate limit as opposed to a concurrency limit, which is a different question with a different answer; and any notion of client identity beyond the TCP peer — a forwarded header is the deployment's to trust (D44), so it stays out of a limit the server enforces itself. Verified by 5 unit tests on the limit (one peer held to its share, a flooder not shutting out fifty others, addresses forgotten at zero and on refusal, no bookkeeping without a limit, v4/v6 as distinct peers), 2 provider tests over real sockets — an HTTP peer whose third connection is closed rather than queued while the server keeps serving, and a WebSocket peer whose second `connect` fails and whose share returns when its connection ends — and 3 runtime-layer tests pinning the crossing (the two caps as independent numbers, neither implying the other, and unusable values rejected before the bind). Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/`, site `api/http` + `api/websocket` + `docs/http` + both internals pages, `CHANGELOG`).

### D51 — A request body's deadline is earned, not fixed · *Proposed (2026-08-08)*

**Context:** D43 bounded every phase of a connection that could stall — the handshake, the first byte, the request head, an idle keep-alive, a silent HTTP/2 peer — and named what it left open: "a slow *body* read (a 1-byte-per-minute upload is still unbounded — it needs a body wrapper and its own thinking)". `header_read` stops the instant the head is complete, so a peer that sends a well-formed head and then dribbles its body is past every timer the server has. It holds a connection, a task, a descriptor, and the handler awaiting the body, at the cost of one byte a minute. That is slowloris moved one phase later, against a clock that has already stopped.

**Decision:** the body gets a deadline it **earns by arriving** — `start + bodyRead + received / bodyMinRate` — defaulting to 30s and 1024 bytes per second.

- **A flat cap was the obvious answer and is the wrong one.** Measured in elapsed time, a 100 MiB upload over a slow link and a dribbler are the same connection: any cap generous enough for the upload is generous enough for the attack, and any cap tight enough for the attack breaks the upload. What separates them is not how long they take but how much they send while taking it, so that is what the rule reads.
- **An idle timeout is the other obvious answer, and is worse.** It is what Node (`requestTimeout` aside) and nginx (`client_body_timeout`) do, and a dribbler resets it with every single byte — it stops a peer that goes *completely* silent and nothing else. The attack this exists for walks straight through it.
- **A floor to beat, not a rate to sustain.** 1 KiB/s is about a hundredth of a dial-up modem. A fast upload finishes long before its own deadline matters; a genuinely slow client is never near the line. The shape is Apache's `mod_reqtimeout`, arrived at independently and then checked against it.
- **A chunk already in hand is delivered, even on the poll where the deadline passed.** The point of the bound is bytes that never came, not an upload that finished close to the line.
- **`bodyMinRate: 0` is a rate, not an omission** — it says a slow body earns nothing, making `bodyRead` a flat cap for a deployment that wants one. `bodyRead: null` removes the bound, the same spelling every other timeout uses. Reading `0` as "unset" would hand back the default allowance, which is the opposite of what was asked.

**Consequences:** `HttpTimeouts` gains `body_read` and `body_min_rate`, which breaks an embedder's construction of it (pre-1.0 and unstable per SPEC §14, and the same break D43/D45 made). The bound is applied per *request* rather than per connection, so each body on a kept-alive or multiplexed connection gets its own allowance — a peer cannot spend one body's credit on the next. The failure surfaces to the handler as the body stream erroring with `ERR_TIMED_OUT`, naming what arrived and how slowly, which is the only honest signal once the head has been accepted. **Not solved here:** total request duration, which stays deliberately unbounded (a cap would break SSE and long-polling, and there is no per-route override to escape it); and a *response* the guest produces slowly, which is the guest's own business. Verified by 6 wrapper unit tests on a paused clock (a body inside its grace, the dribbler, the large slow upload that must survive, `min_rate: 0`, no limit at all, and the failure's shape) plus 2 provider tests over real sockets — a byte-a-minute body on a real connection cut off, and an 8-chunk upload running four times past its grace completing untouched — and 5 runtime-layer tests pinning the crossing (grace and rate as themselves, `0` as a rate, `null` as removal, one named leaving the rest, and six malformed values rejected before the bind). Documented per D27 (`API.md`, `types/`, site `api/http` + `docs/http` + `docs/internals/http`, `CHANGELOG`).

### D50 — A host handle belongs to the agent that created it · *Proposed (2026-08-08)*

**Context:** D7 puts the capability check on the op that *acquires* a resource — `net_connect` needs `Net`, `system_spawn` needs `Run`, `http_serve` needs `NetListen` — and leaves the ops that *use* the resulting id ungated, on the reasoning that the id could only have come from a checked call. D48 made that reasoning false. A worker is a second agent with its own capability set, but the providers behind these ops are process-wide (`Arc<SystemCommands>`, `Arc<SystemNet>`, `Arc<SystemHttpServer>`), their ids come from one counter and start at 1, and `globalThis.__ops` is reachable from guest code by design (`harden.js` freezes the binding, not the object). So `new Worker(url, { permissions: [] })` — an agent granted *nothing* — could call `system_kill(1, "SIGKILL")` and kill its parent's child, or `http_respond(2, …)` and answer a request its parent was serving. Both were reproduced against the real binary; the hijacked response reached the client and the legitimate handler's own `respond` was the one that failed.

**Decision:** an id is not authority. The agent that mints a handle records it, and every op that takes one checks it first, refusing with a new `ERR_FOREIGN_HANDLE` (`NotAllowedError`).

- **In the ops layer, not the providers.** `builtins::install` runs once per `Runtime`, so a registry built there is exactly agent-scoped, needs no id at all to identify "this agent", and is an `Rc<RefCell<HashSet<u64>>>` that cannot leave its thread. Putting it in the provider would mean inventing an agent identity, passing it across the seam, and trusting every embedder's implementation to consult it.
- **A code of its own, not `ERR_CAPABILITY_DENIED`.** The caller may hold the capability and still not hold *this* handle; the fixes differ. Nor `ERR_NOT_FOUND` — the resource usually exists, it is simply not this agent's to name.
- **Every id in a batch.** `ws_broadcast` takes a list, and a broadcast is a list of sends: one foreign entry is one foreign send.
- **Releasing is a per-kind judgment, not a rule.** Giving an id back bounds the set and nothing more — a provider counter never reissues a number, so a retained id can never become another agent's. A kind is therefore released only where its end of life is *exact*: a request when its response is complete (at `respond` for a buffered body, at `http_response_body_close` for a streamed one, because a streaming handler may still be echoing the request body into it), a socket at `close()`, a child, a file descriptor. Listeners, HTTP servers and WebSocket servers are kept — one per program rather than one per unit of work — and so are WebSocket connections, which outlive their own `close()` because the drain reads on until the peer's answering frame arrives. Releasing there would answer an ordinary teardown with a refusal aimed at a different mistake.
- **`MessagePort` is unguessable instead of owned.** A port id is *meant* to move between agents, and it moves inside structured-clone bytes that JS writes and reads — there is no host-side moment at which the receiving agent's claim could be recorded, and a host op to "claim" one would be forgeable by exactly the caller it must stop. So `ProcessPortHub` mints ids from the CSPRNG (53 bits, the width that survives the trip through a JS number) rather than from a counter. Holding the id is still the authority; what changes is that it can no longer be reached by counting.

**Consequences:** eight ops modules gained the check — `net_ops`, `sync_fs_ops`, `system_ops`, `http_ops`, `ws_ops`, `worker_ops` — covering sockets, listeners, file descriptors, child processes, HTTP servers, in-flight requests, WebSocket connections and servers, workers, and `BroadcastChannel` subscriptions. `fetch_ops`, `compression_ops`, `encoding_ops` and `serialization_ops` needed nothing: their registries were already agent-local. `net_start_tls` gives up the old socket id for the upgraded one, which is the only place a handle is deliberately replaced. One bounded leak is accepted: a request id whose handler never responds stays in the set, the same leak the provider already has. Three lifecycle bugs surfaced while making the suite pass, each one a place where JS reached the host after giving a handle up — `Socket.close()` running twice (an explicit close plus the readable's `cancel`, plus read EOF on a socket that disallows half-open), the writable's half-close firing after the whole socket had gone, and `Listener.accept()` being called once more after `close()`. All three were previously invisible because the provider answered a dead id leniently; they are now closed-once in the prelude, which is what those APIs already promised. **Not solved here:** an embedder that hands two `Runtime`s the same provider and *wants* them to share a handle has no way to say so — the honest shape for that is a transfer op, which nothing has asked for. Verified by 5 unit tests on the registry and 3 end-to-end tests under the real binary, each one a repro of an exploit that worked before the fix: a worker killing its parent's child, answering its parent's request, and writing to its parent's socket while *holding* `net`. Documented per D27 (`API.md`, site `docs/security` + `docs/guides/securing-runtime`, `CHANGELOG`).

### D49 — A worker's environment is handed to it, not inherited · *Proposed (2026-08-06)*

**Context:** D48 gave a worker its own capability set, so `env` is grantable per spawn. But the grant is binary: with it, the worker reads the deployment's environment (narrowed by `--allow-env=<names>`); without it, nothing. There is no way to say "this variable and no other" at the spawn, and `--allow-env` is set by the deployment rather than by the code that knows what the worker is for. Passing configuration by `postMessage` works, but nothing a library reads by convention — `DATABASE_URL`, `PORT` — is reachable that way.

**Decision:** `new Worker(url, { env })`, taking `"inherit"` (the default: the host environment, still needing the `env` capability and still narrowed by the deployment) or an object of variables (precisely those, and **no capability required**).

- **A handed environment is data, not authority.** A parent can only pass values it could already read, so this attenuates — the same move `permissions` makes, one level down. Requiring `env` for it would mean the narrower thing needed the broader grant; and granting `Env` to serve it would also unlock `args`, `cwd` and `platform`, which is a widening nobody asked for. The op that reports it (`process_env_provided`) is therefore ungated, and can only ever report what a parent handed *this* worker.
- **A handed environment wins over the host's.** Holding `env` is not a reason to ignore the narrower thing the parent chose.
- **Secret-looking names are re-masked on arrival**, by the same key convention `runtime:process` already applies, so a `Secret` passed straight through stays one.
- **Rejected: Node's `SHARE_ENV`.** A shared, mutable environment is an undeclared side channel between agents; `postMessage` is the declared one. For the same reason a parent's own `env.X = …` stays invisible to its workers: each agent seeds `env` from the host snapshot, not from the parent's object.

**Consequences:** `WorkerSpec` gains `env`, `Process` gains a defaulted `provided_env()`, and `WorkerProcess` — which already existed to narrow a worker's process view — serves it. `esrun --deny-all --allow-workers --allow-imports app.js` can now hand a worker exactly one secret with no environment access at all. (`--allow-imports` because starting a worker means reading its entry module, and reading a module is what `imports` grants — Node needs `--allow-fs-read` alongside `--allow-worker` for the same reason, and Deno needs `--allow-read`. The refusal says so and names the flag.) Verified by 4 end-to-end tests (handed env without the capability, precedence over the host's, `{}`, and a malformed option throwing from the constructor rather than through `onerror`). Two gaps surfaced while building it and were fixed in passing: `permissions.has("workers")` threw, having been left out of the prelude's name list when D48 added the capability; and the build script did not re-run when only the op registrations changed, so a stale snapshot could misbind `__ops` by position (D8) — `rerun-if-changed` now names the runtime and engine sources.

### D48 — Workers: a second agent, over a provider seam, granted nothing by default · *Proposed (2026-08-06)*

**Context:** `API.md` listed "Workers / multi-thread" as a durable non-goal on the grounds that multi-isolate belongs to the embeddable VM layer (Layer B). That boundary moves; the principle behind it does not.

**Decision (maintainer sign-off pending):**

- **`Worker` is the HTML dedicated worker, and spawning is a provider.** `runtime` still owns no thread and no loop (ARCHITECTURE §1/§5): starting an agent arrives as `WorkerHost`, like `NetTransport` before it, with the OS-thread implementation confined to `default-providers`. Layer B substitutes a scheduler-backed host and nothing above changes. Note that `Worker` is **not** in the WinterTC Minimum Common API — this is a deliberate step into the HTML Standard, in the direction Deno and Bun already took.
- **Module workers only.** `type: "classic"` throws. This runtime evaluates every input as a module (SPEC §8); there is no classic-script path for a classic worker to use, which is the same reason `require` is absent. Deno refuses them too.
- **A worker starts with no capabilities**, is granted them explicitly (`new Worker(url, { permissions: ["net"] })`), and can never be granted what its parent lacks. **This diverges from Deno**, which clones the parent's `PermissionsContainer` unmodified. Ours is stricter and follows from deny-by-default (D7): no chain of spawns widens the original grant. The cost is that an `esrun` worker starts with nothing while its parent has everything, which will surprise people arriving from Deno — so it is documented rather than silent.
- **A worker's static import graph loads under the parent's authority.** Without this, deny-by-default would mean single-file workers only. It is safe because *instantiation runs no guest code*: the host loads and links the graph, narrows to the child's set, and only then evaluates. That is why `load_module_source` is now split into `instantiate_module_source` + `begin_evaluation`.
- **Nesting is allowed and bounded by the capability chain**, not by withholding the constructor. A worker may spawn if it holds `workers`, and may only pass on what it holds.
- **Process control stays with the agent that owns the process.** `onSignal` is refused inside a worker (a watch suppresses the default action, so it decides whether the process declines to die), and `exit()` in a worker ends that worker without setting the process's exit code.

**Rejected:** an `isMainThread` flag. It is a Node-ism from a design with no worker global scope; HTML, Deno and Bun all distinguish the two by the shape of the global, and so do we — the presence of a `WorkerScope` provider is what installs `postMessage`/`onmessage`/`close()`.

**The messaging that came with it.** `structuredClone` is now HTML's StructuredSerialize/Deserialize over V8's `ValueSerializer` rather than a hand-written JS clone: two implementations of one algorithm would have drifted, so that a value cloning fine through `structuredClone` failed through `postMessage`. It also fixed three real conformance bugs (a class-prototyped object threw; symbol keys were copied; a view over a transferred buffer threw). `SharedArrayBuffer` crosses as one allocation, `BroadcastChannel` reaches the whole agent cluster via `BroadcastHub`, `MessagePort` becomes host-backed via `PortHub` so it can be transferred, and streams transfer by piping across a port pair with backpressure carried.

**The constraint that shaped all of it:** an op cannot carry a JS object graph. Op handlers receive the closed `Value` enum, so a `Map`, a cycle or a class instance arrives as its `String(value)` coercion. The graph is therefore flattened to bytes *on the JS side*, by an engine primitive, before the op — the op boundary keeps its existing contract and `Value` is untouched. Deno's `op_message_port_post_message` has the same shape.

**Two things to remember, both found by a failing test:**
- V8 runs the whole of `WriteValue`/`ReadValue` under a `DisallowJavascriptExecutionScope` whose failure mode is a `CHECK`-crash, so *any* JS-capable API — a property read included — aborts the process from inside a serializer delegate. Blink never meets this because its host objects are C++ wrappers; ours are JS classes with private fields. The delegate opens an `AllowJavascriptExecutionScope` around the codecs it calls.
- Anything in the snapshot-baked prelude that depends on the host must be read **lazily**. One blob is restored into every agent, and the builder isolate has no providers at all, so `const hosted = __ops.broadcast_available()` at load time baked `false` into every launch. `worker.js` lives in `post_snapshot_source` for the same reason.

**The one `unsafe` in `engine`:** `Send`/`Sync` for the handle to a `SharedArrayBuffer`'s backing store, so it can be carried between threads. Sound because reaching one allocation from several isolates on several threads is exactly what that V8 type exists for, and its `shared_ptr` control block is atomically refcounted.

**`OpDecl::unref`.** An open `MessagePort` kept the process alive, because its receive pump is an outstanding op — so `new MessageChannel()` with an `onmessage`, ordinary code that used to exit cleanly, hung. `unref` marks a receive that only *this* agent could satisfy: still polled, still resolving, but no longer a reason for the loop to stay open. Justified narrowly — a port whose peer is in this agent can only receive if this agent runs code, so waiting while the loop is idle is provably futile. A port transferred to a worker is held open by that worker instead. **Not** for a receive whose sender is elsewhere: a live worker *does* keep the process alive, as in Node and Deno.

**Consequences:** `Capability::Worker` is the thirteenth, host-facing as `workers` (`--deny-workers`). `providers` gains four traits (`WorkerHost`, `WorkerScope`, `BroadcastHub`, `PortHub`) and `default-providers` their process-scoped implementations. Console writes become line-atomic, since more than one agent writes to stdout now. `navigator.hardwareConcurrency` arrives, being the number a pool is sized from. Verified by 21 end-to-end tests against the real binary, 4 stream-transfer conformance cases, and the full curated suite at 319/319 under the CLI. **Left for later:** the D3a zero-copy op crossing, now measured rather than assumed (see SPEC §7). **(Amended 2026-08-06:** cross-agent `blob:` URLs were "left for later" here; they are now **not planned**, along with `blob:`/`data:` worker URLs. Both schemes exist to move code and data within a page — on a server the file is already on disk and the bytes already cross by `postMessage` — and no server-side use case was found for either. `new Worker("data:…")` and `new Worker("blob:…")` are refused by the module loader with the scheme named, which is the behaviour to keep.**)**
